FOREWORD SEASONAL UNEMPLOYMENT presents many difficulties in the equitable administration of unemployment compensation, particularly when attempts are made, as in many States, to charge the costs of benefits against an individual employer's account through experience-rating or employer-reserve provisions in their statutes. 

Many State legislatures and unemployment compensation administrations are already facing the question of differentiating between industries and occupations which are normally maintained throughout the year and those of a seasonal character. 

Methods which have been used to protect unemployed seasonal workers in Great Britain, Germany, and Austria represent many approaches to the problem over long periods of administrative experience. Foreign practice, while relating in many ways to situations not analogous to those in this country, cannot fail to illuminate some aspects of the diverse seasonal problems which confront the 

States. 

This study of British, German, and Austrian methods of adjusting unemployment insurance programs to the special characteristics of seasonal industries and seasonal workers was prepared in the Bureau of Research and Statistics of the Social Security Board by Franz Huber, who served temporarily on the Bureau staff while on leave from the Committee on Social Security of the Social Science Research Council. The data in this study were compiled during the summer and fall of 1938. The statements refer to the status of unemployment insurance systems in all three countries as of the close of 1938. The Board makes the report available in printed form as a background to problems and studies in this field in connection with the Federal-State program of unemployment compensation. 

EWAN CLAGUE, Director, Bureau of Research and Statistics.

IN GREAT BRITAIN, GERMANY, AND AUSTRIA

MERLI QAWEMI. 

HOWWE MOUGER 2 VID WEBUSIV

PART I Summary 

Seasonal workers present difficult administrative and fiscal problems in unemployment insurance. The most extensive field of seasonal employment, agriculture, has been excluded from coverage under virtually all unemployment insurance programs, at least at the beginning of their operations. But there remain in the general field of nonagricultural employment many industries with marked seasonal fluctuations controlled by climate or custom. Adjustment of the framework of unemployment insurance provisions to the special circumstances of periodic seasonal fluctuations in employment opportunities has been found necessary through statutes, administrative regulations, or benefit decisions. The experience of the three largest compulsory unemployment insurance systems of Europe, those of Great Britain, Germany, and Austria, affords a history of various expedients used in efforts to preserve a balance between the social objective of compensating workers during periods of involuntary unemployment and the financial objective of safeguarding the unemployment insurance fund.

The methods utilized by these three countries indicate wide variations in approach, depending upon the principles on which the individual program is based and upon the Government's labor policy prevailing at a given time. Over a period of years many different answers have been given to questions such as the following: If seasonal workers contribute to the unemployment insurance fund, should they receive benefits at the same rate as other workers? Is the system designed primarily for the relief of needy unemployed workers regardless of the extent to which they contribute, and does this broad social purpose eliminate the necessity for differentiating between seasonal and nonseasonal workers? Should the system draw a sharp distinction between workers who are wholly dependent on their wages and those who take available seasonal jobs but do not seek year-round employment?

By requiring a qualifying period of covered employment and contributions, Great Britain, Germany, and Austria have all denied benefits to workers who are engaged for very brief periods in covered employment. Great Britain now requires as a condition for the receipt of benefits at least 30 weekly contributions in 2 years, though 3

day's employment in each of 30 weeks is sufficient to meet this qualification. In Germany a worker is ineligible for benefits unless he has been employed for as much as 52 weeks in 2 years, and in Austria unless his covered employment has amounted to as much as 20 weeks in 12 months. Thus in each of these countries seasonal or other intermittent workers who have had only a short period of employment are in general disqualified from receiving benefits even though they have contributed to the unemployment insurance fund. 

In addition to such qualifying conditions, all three countries have found it expedient to impose special restrictions upon seasonal workers, as such. By statute or administrative practice, therefore, each country has defined seasonal workers or seasonal industries, has set the limits of the season and off season for seasonal employment on either a nation-wide or local basis, and has established criteria for differentiating between seasonal and nonseasonal workers. 

Within the framework of their definitions and in accordance with the fundamental objectives of their programs, each country has built up a body of precedent for adapting unemployment insurance to the seasonal problem. Great Britain, with emphasis on insurance principles, has adhered rather consistently to the thesis that compensating workers for inevitable wage loss during seasonal unemployment would be subsidy rather than insurance. With strict regard for the repute of the program, effort has been made to prevent the use of the unemployment insurance fund for the payment of benefits to unemployed workers who are not available for suitable work during the off season. Austria, with emphasis on the relief aspects of unemployment compensation, pays benefits only on a needs basis and, in taking account of the claimant's income, severely restricts the eligibility of workers who can subsist on the income or resources of other members of their households. Germany takes a stand somewhere between these two extremes: Workers are entitled to benefits on an insurance basis for a period of 6 weeks; thereafter they are eligible only on a needs basis.

DETERMINATION OF "SEASONAL WORKERS" 

Great Britain

Seasonal unemployment does not seem to be an important problem In British unemployment insurance or, for that matter, in the British national economy. The temperate climate of the British Isles reduces

SUMMARY 

5 considerably the number of wholly and moderately seasonal industries, and the nearly all-inclusive coverage of the unemployment insurance system allows almost complete pooling of the seasonal with the general occupational risk.

Up to 1931 the determination as to what constituted a "seasonal worker" had been made by the Umpire, the highest appeal tribunal in British unemployment insurance. When in that year special provisions for seasonal workers were enacted, the Umpire's findings served as the basis of the statutory regulation, which as a matter of fact was but an enactment of principles long before established by experience and practice. Under these regulations, as amended in 

1935, a person whose normal employment is in a "wholly" seasonal occupation is considered a seasonal worker. A wholly seasonal occupation, in turn, is one in which, at certain regularly recurring periods of the year, no substantial amount of employment is available in the district in which the worker resides. Included in the category is employment in holiday and health resorts and in industries in which the period of operation is determined by weather, climate, custom, or law.

In general, the number of industries which have been regarded as seasonal in Great Britain is small. The British attitude is that the principle of the pooled risk allows and calls for payment of benefits during the slack season to workers in "moderately" seasonal occupations in which employment fluctuates regularly in the course of the year although some employment is always available even when operations are reduced. The building and clothing trades are foremost in this category. In such industries, unemployment during the year is a probability, whereas for wholly seasonal industries it is a certainty, and payment of benefits would represent not insurance but subsidy.

The British do not attempt to determine by statute the specific occupations that are wholly seasonal. Practical application and interpretation are left to the local appeal agencies, courts of referees on which employers and employees are represented in equal numbers. 

This decentralized administration of a nation-wide uniform system is most significant. Individual treatment of each seasonal worker's claim is correlated with local conditions by agencies thoroughly familiar with the industrial structure and customs of the region. In interpreting the statutes, the courts of referees give special attention to the question whether a worker's seasonal employment is his normal employment or whether economic conditions rather than his own

volition make him look for and follow a wholly seasonal occupation. 

Only if a worker has, for a number of years, been employed in a wholly seasonal occupation and has had no other employment is he considered a seasonal worker. Very decidedly, the worker and his industrial status are placed in the center of the problem, not the industry or the establishment in which the individual happens to be employed.

Germany 

Contrary to the experience in Great Britain, the seasonal problem has been a matter of great concern to the German unemployment insurance system. Industries which are only slightly seasonal in 

Great Britain show marked fluctuations in Germany. In view of the nature and scope of the German unemployment insurance system, the problem of seasonal workers had to be taken into account from the very beginning. In Germany, as elsewhere, the basic approach is found in the method of determining who is to be considered a seasonal worker.

From 1928, the year after the unemployment insurance system was established, until 1934, by which time fundamental changes in the purpose and character of the program had abolished all special provisions for seasonal workers, a seasonal worker was considered to be one who had been employed for a uniformly and strictly defined period of time in a seasonal occupation in a seasonal industry. Both seasonal occupations and seasonal industries were again defined in the statutes by a detailed enumeration, and the determination was applied equally to all regions of the country. 

The specific definition of a seasonal worker as one who had been engaged in a seasonal industry and in a seasonal occupation (both were necessary) for more than half of the 26 weeks preceding his registration for benefits during the off season introduced chance as a major factor of the determination. Furthermore, it did not allow for differentiating between wholly seasonal workers (who looked for and performed only seasonal work and did not expect employment during the off season), seasonal workers who sought and obtained off-season employment, and workers who had taken up seasonal work temporarily because no employment was available in their regular nonseasonal trade. While the first type of seasonal worker is decidedly not an active member of the labor market during the off season, the dovetailing worker and, to an even higher degree, the chance seasonal worker offer their services at any time during the year.

SUMMARY 

7 This defect in the statutes was partially remedied later by changing the basis of seasonal determination to one-half of 52 weeks; but even then, any worker who had been employed in a seasonal industry and occupation for 26 weeks and 1 day was considered a seasonal worker when he became unemployed during the off season. When, in 1931, the special provisions for seasonal workers became applicable not only to the off season but to any spell of unemployment during the year, the seasonal factor was even farther removed from the determination of a worker's benefit rights. Austria

The considerable volume and degree of seasonal fluctuations in Austria had led to an early attempt to find an actuarially sound and socially equitable solution of the seasonality problem. Since the year 1931 special provisions for seasonal workers have been in force, and seasonal workers are considered to be those who belong to occupations in which unemployment for a part of the year is customary. Under the 1931 regulations the industries considered seasonal were designated by law; under the 1935 provisions they were fixed by administrative order, thus making a more flexible determination possible. Two categories of seasonal workers were established: first, workers employed in certain establishments, if the latter were conducted on a seasonal basis, i. e., completely closed down or operating at considerable reduction during the off season; second, workers in trades or industries which by their very nature must be classified as seasonal. While not as exhaustive as the German classification, the Austrian provisions are considerably more detailed than the British. Austria's differentiation between seasonally conducted establishments and industries which are seasonal by their very nature would seem somewhat superior to the combined classification of seasonal industries and occupations in the German system. 

DETERMINATION OF "OFF SEASON" 

Great Britain

The same principle of individual interpretation and application of deliberately vague provisions of law by experienced local authorities which is practiced with regard to the determination of "seasonal worker" applies with at least equal importance to the fixing of the term "off season" and, implicitly, that of the "season." For sea-

sonal workers whose normal employment is in an occupation followed by them in one district only, off season is defined as that part or those parts of the year during which persons are not normally employed in the occupation and district in question. If they are employed in more than one district-as, for example, fish workers, who follow the various fishing seasons along the coast of England and Scotland-then the determination must be made for each district separately. In holiday and health resorts, the off season is considered to be all the year except the holiday season. Under these general provisions, the courts of referees determine periods of season and off season for the various seasonal occupations, basing their findings on local industrial and climatic conditions. As a rule, their determination is not questioned by the Umpire. 

Courts of referees may, particularly if other sources of information are not available, determine the season in a particular occupation by fixing its beginning as the date upon which 25 percent or more of all the workers in the industry became employed and its termination as the date on which 75 percent of the workers were discharged. 

This determination is, of course, applicable only to the district over which each court has jurisdiction. While the Umpire admitted that this method was a "useful guide," he emphasized that it could not be used to decide that the duration of a season varied from one year to another. It is in the nature of the season, according to the Umpire, that it should be of regular annual recurrence and that it should cover approximately the same period each year. Germany

Disregarding local climatic and industrial conditions, Germany established a fixed off-season period for the whole country-determined by the national unemployment insurance agency-comprising the 4 winter months from December through March. The choice of these dates shows clearly that the Germans were primarily concerned with winter unemployment in the so-called outdoor occupations. Although for several years the district labor offices were authorized to establish additional off-season periods for certain occupations which they might consider seasonal, there is no evidence that they made use of this discretionary power to any considerable extent. When, in 1931, the restriction of benefit rights of seasonal workers was extended to apply throughout the whole year, definition of the off-season period became unnecessary.

SUMMARY 

Austria

Austria, whose methods have been closer to the German than to the British system, used the German way of determining "off season," though in a somewhat modified form. Since it distinguished between a priori seasonal industries on the one hand, and establishments conducted on a seasonal basis in certain industries which in themselves were not necessarily seasonal, on the other, it established two "season" periods, each uniform for the category. The determination was made by the central administrative agency, and although discretion was given to the district (State) labor offices to establish other seasonal periods, none had been made by 1938. The district offices were free, however, to determine the "not unfavorable" state of the labor market in certain occupations for the purposes of disqualifying seasonal workers from receiving emergency benefits during the season.

In the case of a priori seasonal industries, the seasonal period was the same as that used in Germany-April through November; for seasonally conducted establishments it lasted from May through September and, if there was a winter season, from December 15 through March 15.

WHOLLY SEASONAL AND "DOVETAILING" WORKERS 

There is little difference of opinion as to whether or not seasonal workers should be entitled to unemployment benefits during the employment season. Seasonal workers may become unemployed even during the busy season, and if unemployment is at all an insurable risk, loss or lack of a job during the season must entitle insured workers to benefits. As a matter of fact, British experience shows that a seasonal worker's risk of becoming unemployed during the season is about equal to that of a nonseasonal worker. We find, therefore, no unemployment insurance law imposing any special restrictions on payment of benefits to seasonal workers during the time when their employment opportunities are most favorable, with the possible exception of Austria, which, by practice if not by law, disqualified seasonal workers from the right to have their benefit duration extended during the season.

On the other hand, unemployment during the off season involves special financial and social problems. British opinion is that unemployment during the off season should, in principle, not give the right 159727-40- -2

to benefit unless the applicant can prove that he works during the off season in another occupation. The German and Austrian unemployment insurance systems, which are inherently more relief than insurance systems, pay benefits to seasonal workers during the off season but only under certain restrictions. While both Austria and Germany assume that there is a certain amount of dovetailing of employment by seasonal workers, they do not take it into account in determining the eligibility of seasonal workers for benefits during the off season. 

PAYMENT OF BENEFITS DURING THE OFF SEASON 

Great Britain

From an economic and actuarial point of view, the British principle of paying unemployment benefits to persons while they are bona fide members of the labor market is the most consistent and the clearest. A worker who normally depends for his livelihood on seasonal work but who must and does supplement his income by off-season work is, in principle, entitled to benefits during the off season if he can prove that he ordinarily gets other work. To qualify, such a worker must show not only that he has met the general eligibility requirements but also that he has been employed during the off season in each of the 2 complete insurance years preceding the beginning of the current off season for a period amounting, in the aggregate, to at least one-fourth of the combined duration of these two off seasons. 

Employment during the current off season may be substituted for that during the off season in any one of the 2 preceding insurance years. 

He must further prove that, taking into account his individual circumstances, his industrial experience, and the industrial structure of the district, he can reasonably expect employment for a substantial part of the off season. If, however, a seasonal worker can prove that the aggregate duration of the seasons in the district or districts in which he is normally employed amounts to 39 weeks in the year, he is not subject to these special regulations. Nor will he be affected by them if, in 4 out of 5 consecutive insurance years within the last 10 insurance years prior to the registration of his claim for benefits, he had paid at least 150 weekly contributions, i. e., if he has had covered employment for about 38 weeks on the average during those 4 years. 

Before the 1935 amendments went into effect, neither the Umpire

SUMMARY 

11 nor the courts of referees had had any statutory criterion for deciding whether off-season employment was of a "substantial extent," the term used in prior regulations. Since then, however, the allowance of benefits to seasonal workers during the off season is calculated mathematically on the basis of employment during off seasons. This off-season work may be in either covered or noncovered employment, although before 1935 it had to be the former. 

There is only one other special provision for seasonal workers in the British unemployment insurance law; namely, the clause providing that a worker may elect voluntary exclusion from coverage if he is employed in an occupation of a seasonal nature which ordinarily does not extend over more than 18 weeks in the year, if he is not ordinarily employed in any other covered employment. Such persons would never qualify for benefits during the off season and rarely during the season.

Germany 

While we thus see a consistent development in the British method of dealing with seasonal workers, the German approach to the problem has varied. According to German principles, a seasonal worker should be entitled to benefits even during the off season, though possibly at a reduced rate or duration. The factor that decided whether he should receive benefits during the off season was not that he normally worked during the slack season, but was, generally, the financial status of the unemployment fund. Since the latter was always precarious, seasonal workers had to be subject to certain restrictions. Not that the Germans disregarded the seasonal problem in general and the possibilities of dovetailing employment in particular; they simply failed to work out a satisfactory solution. 

During the period 1927-31, three methods were tried: extension of the waiting period, reduction of the benefit duration, and reduction of benefit rates. The main defect of all three methods was that the centralized and strict determination made impossible any evaluation of basic differences in either economic or personal factors. 

The extension of the waiting period was the first modification to be tried. Expressed as a flat duration-either 1 or 2 additional weeks-the method allowed small chance for individual treatment of seasonal workers. And, with a maximum benefit duration of 26 weeks and an off season of about 16 weeks, it could hardly effect great savings to the fund. At the same time this small difference of 1 or 2 weeks did not represent any noticeable differential between persons

who are normally not active members of the labor market during the off season and those who, as a rule, look for and obtain off-season 

Not much more successful was the second attempt, the reduction of the ordinary benefit duration to 6 weeks during the off season. 

This reduction could be justified only on the ground of preventing the fund from becoming exhausted. If for wholly seasonal workers unemployment during the off season is a certainty, there would seem to be no reason to pay benefits to them during that period, no matter how short the benefit period chosen. If, on the other hand, unemployment during the off season is a probability but not a certainty for workers who dovetail employment, these workers should receive benefits during the slack season unless they have exhausted their statutory benefit rights.

The third method, that of reducing the benefit rates for seasonal workers during the off season, would seem to have a certain social justification if, as was true in Germany, the reduction affects only highly paid seasonal workers.

From 1931 to 1934 seasonal workers' benefit rights were limited not only during off-season periods but during the season as well. The distinction between season and off season lost its significance, and the true nature of seasonal employment was no longer taken into account. 

In 1934 all special provisions for seasonal workers were discarded. Austria

In addition to the socially objectionable practice of disqualifying seasonal workers from emergency benefits during the season, Austria applied two methods in dealing with the seasonal unemployment risk: (1) imposing higher contribution rates for seasonal workers and (2) prolonging their waiting periods. Higher contribution rates for seasonal workers were imposed in the original law of 1920 which had established "risk groups," dividing industries into three classes, one with an average risk which paid the "normal" contribution rate; one including particularly stable industries which paid lower rates; and the third comprising industries with higher than normal unemployment risk which paid higher contribution rates. This latter group included the seasonal industries. The difficulty of administering these provisions and the insufficient financial return of the higher rates led shortly to their abolition. A similar attempt was made in

SUMMARY 

13 1931, when contributions of seasonal workers were increased during the season.

Both methods attempted to balance the seasonal risk by higher contributions and were based upon the principle of paying ordinary benefits to seasonal workers regardless of season or offseason unemployment.

A second and from a social point of view more satisfactory method had been initiated by the district labor offices, and in 1935 this was made the statutory basis of the treatment of unemployment during the off season. Its principle is found in the particular character of the unemployment insurance law in Austria. No person is entitled to benefits, not even for the first 12 weeks, whose "means of livelihood is not endangered" by his unemployment. For most workers this requirement had no particular significance since a wage or salary earner's means of livelihood is endangered when he loses his job. In the case of seasonal workers (salaried employees are not affected by any of the seasonal workers' provisions) the endangered state of their livelihood may be determined on a stricter basis if they become unemployed during the off season and have had high earnings during the busy period. In addition to the normal 7 days, such workers undergo a waiting period the duration of which depends on the amount of their average seasonal earnings or the length of their seasonal employment. The computation of the length of this additional waiting period is based on a comparison of the earnings of a seasonal worker with those of a nonseasonal worker of equal occupational training and skill over the same period. In the computation only earnings or employment during the season are counted.

Thus the status of the seasonal worker in the labor market during the off season is disregarded. Whether or not he finds dovetailing work, the application or the method of application of the additional waiting-period provisions remains fixed. 

The computation of the additional waiting period is simplified by the fact that in all seasonal industries where collective wage agreements exist, an average scale can be applied for all members of a certain occupation, making individual calculation unnecessary. Thus the worker himself knows exactly how long his waiting period will be. The pronounced social character of the Austrian unemployment insurance system permits modifications in the length of the additional waiting period, in view of family responsibility, unsatisfactory season, and similar reasons.

CONCLUSION 

The problem of payment of benefits to seasonal workers during the off season will always resolve itself into two main questions: one of equity and social purpose, the other of actuarial soundness and financial stability of the fund. Any effort to solve the seasonal problem in unemployment compensation will have to be directed toward a compromise between the just needs of the individual and the collective guarantee of the funds accumulated for the various types of unemployment. The British approach, in particular, seems worthy of close study because it has proved to be remarkably adaptable to changing conditions and at the same time has retained the fundamental principles of insurance. 

In the United States the seasonal problem in relation to unemployment is more complex than in Europe. The absence of requirements for employee contributions in the majority of State laws, the emphasis on employer liability for stabilizing employment as reflected in adjusted experience-rating provisions for employer contributions, and the closer relationship between benefits and wages all throw the problem into a perspective which has no exact duplicate in any European system. The techniques employed by Great Britain, Germany, and Austria in attempts to fit seasonal employment into the pattern of their general unemployment insurance systems should, nonetheless, prove instructive to students of unemployment insurance in the United States.

PART II Seasonal Workers and Unemployment Insurance in Great Britain INTRODUCTION 

The British unemployment insurance system, established in 1911, although restricted in coverage, was the first nation-wide program for the protection of wage earners against the hazard of involuntary unemployment. In 27 years of application to the problems of unemployment insurance, the British law has been amended and revised frequently, coverage has been extended to include virtually all manual workers, benefit provisions have been liberalized, and, at the same time, regulations and special orders have been devised to prevent the abuse of the system by persons for whom the program was not primarily designed. Since Great Britain has constructed a system which seems to be both socially equitable and financially stable, an analysis of its experience is of value to those who are concerned with the principles and practice of unemployment compensation in the 

United States.

The British attitude toward seasonal unemployment is illustrated by the Ministry of Labour's statement in 1931 when the problem of seasonal unemployment was outlined for the Royal Commission on 

Unemployment Insurance.

Two classes of seasonal workers were distinguished: (1) those who are employed in seasonal trades characterized by a busy season alternating with a slack period during which some employment is available and (2) those employed in occupations in which the work is of a wholly seasonal nature, lasting for only a more or less definite period of the year and providing no employment outside the season. "Examples of the first class are the building, clothing, and certain other trades. There are large numbers in this class, which represents one of the main types of unemployment for which the insurance scheme was intended to provide" (21: pp. 44-46). 1 It was held that the first class should be entitled to unemployment benefits for unemployment at any time during the year, regardless of season. Unemployed workers of the second class, on the other hand, should not receive benefits for unemployment during the off season. 

1 See appendix A, chart I. 

2 See pp. 65-66 for literature cited. 15

SEASONAL WORKERS 

British policy is based on the position that payment of benefits to unemployed workers who have been employed for only a few months each year in a highly seasonal occupation and who make no effort to find other work during the off season is a violation of the insurance principle on which their program is founded. An unemployed seasonal worker is, therefore, ineligible for unemployment insurance benefits unless he is genuinely seeking work and available for employment during the off season in occupations which he customarily follows at various times during the year. Benefit decisions are made locally, allowing employment conditions in the district and the employment record of the individual worker to be taken into account. 

There is general agreement that seasonal fluctuations in Great Britain are far less severe in volume or intensity than elsewhere in Europe or in the United States, probably due, in large measure, to the even climate and mild winters of the British Isles. The busy periods of the various seasonal industries in Great Britain, moreover, do not coincide; hence, in many cases, workers may dovetail employment in successive operations throughout the year. The employment of agricultural workers is highly seasonal but these workers, who constitute only about 5.6 percent of the gainfully occupied population in 

Great Britain, have never been covered by the general unemployment insurance program. In 1936 a separate system was established for agriculture with contributions and benefits adjusted to the special needs of agricultural workers.

A study made by the International Labour Office in 1928, which analyzed the experience of British trade-unions for the years 18871920, pointed out that in Great Britain seasonal fluctuations in employment were small compared with those in other countries but that they were still far from negligible, since more than one-fifth of the total average number unemployed in December and January of each year were seasonally unemployed (32: pp. 354–355). It appeared that unemployment showed a marked tendency to increase in December (this was true of 31 out of the 34 years studied), to decrease from January to April, to increase in August, and to decrease again in November. These data refer only to organized workers in Great 

Britain. Inclusion of nonunion-labor groups might considerably modify the picture of seasonal fluctuations. The basic trends, however, would probably remain the same since, as is indicated in chart II of appendix A, unemployment of insured workers showed a similar periodic rise and fall during the years 1922–36.

SEASONAL WORKERS THE ORIGINAL UNEMPLOYMENT INSURANCE SYSTEM, 1911-20 

The trades covered by the original British unemployment insurance law of December 16, 1911 (1: pt. 2, pp. 92, 120), were almost exclusively seasonal industries: building, construction work, shipbuilding, mechanical engineering, iron founding, construction of vehicles, and sawmilling (including machine woodwork) carried on with one of the other insured trades. Under that act a worker could not qualify for benefits unless he had engaged in covered employment in each of at least 26 separate calendar weeks in the preceding 5 years. Both the coverage provisions and the length of the qualifying period (not expressed in amounts of earnings but in time periods) show that the insurance system was originally designed to pay benefits for seasonal unemployment without any distinction as to season or off season, and most of the covered workers, unless they were employed for only a few weeks a year, could, in principle, qualify for benefits. The provisions relating to duration of benefits, however, limited the benefits to 15 weeks in any 1 year and made them payable on the basis of 

1 week of benefits for every 5 weekly contributions. Thus, only those workers who had been engaged in covered employment for 75 weeks could draw benefits for the maximum of 15 weeks. This ratio provision, as in any similar determination of benefit duration, took into account the personal rather than the occupational or seasonal unemployment risk. As long as the covered groups belonged to trades with reasonably similar seasonal risk, no difficulties were expected. No special provisions for seasonal workers were incorporated in the original unemployment insurance statute.³

It was considered particularly important when the unemployment insurance system was created in 1911 that the law be designed to influence employers to establish hiring methods and working conditions in accord with the general interest. With regard to wholly seasonal trades, particularly in short-season industries, no difficulties could be expected under the ratio provisions which, together with the maximum of 15 weeks a year, kept wholly seasonal workers from drawing benefits for a considerable time.

Because of the limited coverage provisions and the fairly uniform occupational seasonal risks covered, the British system during the years 1911-20 is of little value as a guide to possible treatment of seasonal workers under laws in the United States. 

It is interesting to note that in the health insurance system, enacted simultaneously, special provision was made with regard to contributions of employers and workers in seasonal trades during season and off season (1: pt. 1, sec. 50 and 2d sched.).

GREAT BRITAIN 

19 4 

GENERAL STATUTORY PROVISIONS, 1920-27 

The British Act of 1920 extended the 1911 coverage under unemployment insurance to virtually all trades and industries covered at present, with the exception of domestic service and agriculture (the latter has been covered by a special "agricultural system" since 1936).

The principal exclusions were (and are) certain kinds of employment regarded either as reasonably stable or as inconsiderable. Qualifying provisions remained practically unchanged. The ratio of duration of benefits to number of contributions paid was changed from one-tofive to one-to-six, but otherwise none of the changes affected seasonal workers. The new ratio placed a stricter limit on benefit duration, but both contribution and benefit rates were flat amounts applying to both seasonal and nonseasonal workers, and the maximum period remained 15 weeks in an insurance year. Ninety weeks of contributions were necessary to qualify a worker for the full benefit duration of 15 weeks. Other eligibility conditions for benefits required that the claimant serve a waiting period (although this had no particular application to seasonal workers), that he be capable of work and available for work but unable to obtain suitable employment (whether covered or not), and that he be engaged normally in covered employment (2). 

The eligibility conditions were greatly liberalized in 1921, introducing a new and broader concept of unemployment insurance. 

Unemployed persons who could not fulfill the qualifying-period requirement were entitled to emergency benefits (so-called uncovenanted benefits) if, besides fulfilling the other qualifying requirements, they could prove that they were normally employed in covered employment and that they were genuinely seeking, but unable to obtain, full-time employment (3: p. 3). The question whether a claimant satisfied this condition was decided by the Minister of Labour, who had the authority to refer it to local employment committees for their recommendations. The benefit period was extended to 26 weeks in any insurance year, but the ratio provisions were retained. In the Unemployment Insurance (No. 2) Act of 

1924, the condition that the claimant be genuinely seeking work was for the first time applied to all claimants, whether for "standard" or for "extended" benefits (4: p. 2). 5 This change was made for administrative reasons only; since the week was a period of 

6 working days, the one-to-six ratio worked much better than the former one-to-five. 

The local employment committees are advisory bodies established for the district served by each local office and consist of a chairman and an equal number of representatives of employers and of insured persons.

SEASONAL WORKERS 

Together with the ratio provision, the requirement of "genuinely seeking work" and of being "available for work" put restrictions on payment of benefits to seasonal workers during the off season in all trades in which the work was of a wholly seasonal nature beginning and ending on some more or less definite date. This group, in the opinion of the Ministry of Labour, comprised workers who, although their main occupation was seasonal, "seek, and in fact, sometimes obtain, employment during the off season" (22: p. 45).

The experience during the years 1924-27 while the ratio provisions were in force, together with the "genuinely seeking work," "available for work," and "unable to obtain suitable employment" conditions, shows the operation of two methods of limiting benefit payments during the off season to workers in wholly seasonal trades (24: p. 231): (1) An automatic test and limitation by the ratio provision, of which the Blanesburgh Committee said in 1927: "The automatic test" (i. e., the ratio provision and the 26-week maximum) "rigidly applied would, we fear, deny benefit to many such persons" (i. e., those genuinely unemployed), "except in times of very good trade." (2) A more flexible test, adapted to the particular circumstances of the individual case through interpretation of the clauses "genuinely seeking work," "available for work," and "unable to obtain suitable employment" in connection with unemployment occurring during the off season. 

Local insurance officers who make the initial determination of benefit eligibility in Great Britain and local courts of referees which serve as appeal tribunals are guided by the decisions of the Umpire (the final authority on all disputed claims) on special cases he has heard and by his policy in matters of fundamental importance. The policy in the treatment of seasonal workers during the off season which is apparent in the Umpire's decisions during the years 

1924-26 is described below."

The "Genuinely Seeking Work" Clause 

On July 14, 1926, the Umpire determined the general meaning of the clause "genuinely seeking work" and its application to seasonal workers during their off season in a lengthy decision on a disputed claim of fur machinists who had become unemployed shortly before their off season. According to this decision the previous employment record of the claimant is the most important factor to be taken into account. The employment record of every insured person in • See appendix A, chart III, for a list of important decisions on off-season unemployment.

GREAT BRITAIN 

21 Great Britain is available in the record division at Kew, the central recordkeeping office of the unemployment insurance system. If an applicant has been steadily employed for many years, the assumption is that he prefers work to idleness and would rather obtain a job through the employment office than apply for benefits. An applicant who is genuinely seeking work is expected to make efforts on his own behalf to find work. Furthermore, the claimant should look not only for employment to which he is accustomed, but for any suitable employment for which he is qualified and which he may reasonably expect to obtain. Thus, the question whether the seasonal worker was looking for and obtained employment during the off season was an important factor in determining his eligibility for benefits during the off season. The later unemployment insurance statutes of 

1931 and 1935 embodied the principle that to establish eligibility for benefits an applicant must demonstrate not only his willingness to accept suitable work in another occupation but also the likelihood of his obtaining such work. 

The term "off season" was not defined in any of the Umpire's decisions during the period 1920-27, nor was there any attempt in the statutes to determine its meaning. Since climatic and geographic conditions vary, the determination of the beginning and end of the season was left to the local courts of referees. The British have never attempted to fix the exact dates of the beginning and end of the season or off season as in German, Austrian, or Polish laws which establish fixed dates normally applicable to all seasonal industries throughout the country or applicable to specific industries or regions.

In determining whether an applicant's efforts to find work on his own behalf were of such a nature as to prove that he was genuinely seeking work, four factors were to be taken into account according to the British Umpire: (1) What kind of employment is suitable for the applicant? Presumably, as the Umpire said, the type of work for which he is registered, although other kinds of employment may also be suitable. Wage rate and work conditions to which the claimant has been accustomed in his usual occupation, his experience and training, and his age and qualifications have to be taken into consideration. In the case of skilled workers, unskilled and poorly paid work may be considered unsuitable. (2) What chance has the applicant to obtain work by his own efforts? (3) What are the usual 

But see pp. 39–40.

SEASONAL WORKERS 

means of finding work of the kind which is suitable for the claimant? (4) Has the applicant availed himself with reasonable thoroughness and speed of the normal and most effective means of obtaining suitable employment?

The requirement that the unemployed worker look for suitable employment of other kinds during the off season is applied particularly to workers in an industry which has seasonal fluctuations. 

In considering what employment may be considered suitable for such workers during the off season, special consideration must be given to the kind of work which they have usually obtained during the slack period and which other persons engaged in the same seasonal trade have been able to obtain. Persons employed in seasonal trades in which wages are higher than in nonseasonal trades may not refuse employment as unsuitable on the ground that the remuneration is lower than the wages they normally receive in their own trade, if the remuneration for such off-season employment is not lower than they might reasonably expect to obtain. "These observations," the Umpire reasoned, "apply with more force to trades which are strictly seasonal, that is, those in which, except at certain seasons in each year, there is no employment to be had. Workers in those trades may be, but are not necessarily, wholly seasonal workers, that is, workers who do not desire and do not seek work during the off season. If they claim to be entitled to benefit in the off season they must show clearly that they are looking for other work in the off season. Unless they do so they cannot be regarded as genuinely seeking work." In several cases the Umpire had disallowed claims of persons employed in seasonal trades when it seemed clear from their records and other circumstances that they had made no real effort to get employment except at certain seasons of the year. "Workers of that kind have been disqualified," explained the Umpire, "not because their usual occupation was seasonal, but because they themselves were seasonal workers, who only desired and sought work at certain seasons." 

During the period 1924-26, insurance officers (who make the initial determinations) disallowed a total of 1,131,966 claims, out of which 274,712 (about 24 percent) were denied on the ground that the claimants were not genuinely seeking work; the courts of referees disallowed for the same reason 75,698 (about 32 percent) of a total of 236,400 denied claims; and the Umpire disallowed, on the same ground, 1,070 (about 11 percent) out of a total of 9,904. No information is available, however, to show how many of the applications disallowed for this reason were claims of seasonal workers.

GREAT BRITAIN 

23 

The "Unable to Obtain Suitable Work" and "Available for Work" Clauses 

In an appealed case in 1923, the Umpire disallowed the claim of a fish curer, who had applied for benefits during the off season. During the years 1920-22, this worker had had seasonal employment as a fish curer from June to September and from October to November but had been unemployed for the remaining months because of the seasonal nature of her occupation. In the initial determination, the insurance officer had disallowed her claim because he did not consider her "unemployed" during the period in which she normally had no occupation, either covered or excluded. This decision of the Umpire, because of its importance as a precedent, is quoted in full: 

This applicant seems to have normally followed the employment of a fish curer from June to November (inclusive) in each year. During the remainder of the year she lives at home in a district where at that time, owing to the seasonal nature of the industry, there is no possibility of her obtaining employment as a fish curer in that district. 

She does not appear to have had any training or experience which would fit her for any other kind of work which is available locally, nor is there any evidence that she has been accustomed to do any such work, or to make serious efforts to find such work, in the period from December to May. 

As she is a single woman there is no apparent reason why she should not look for work in other districts where employment in fish curing is to be had when it is not to be had in her own district, and as she has not done so I am not satisfied that she is unable to find suitable employment. 

If, on the other hand, her domestic ties make it impossible for her from December to May to leave that district-in which during that period there is no possibility of her getting work locally-I am of the opinion that she is not available for work during that period."

Similar reasoning was responsible for the disallowance of claims of fish workers who, because of health, domestic circumstances, or age, could not, as was customary, take up seasonal work in other districts during the off season in their own district.10 

General Requirements Affecting Eligibility and Benefit Duration 

The decisions of the Umpire during the period 1920-27 reflect the statutory changes in eligibility requirements as applied to local characteristics of seasonal work, although the law itself did not differentiate between seasonal and other employment. Discussion of

10 See appendix A, chart III, Available for work, Case No. 8988/24.

SEASONAL WORKERS 

the statutory changes during the period and their effect upon seasonal workers must, of necessity, be limited to general terms. It is difficult, if not academic, to evaluate the effect upon seasonal workers of the frequent emergency legislation of the period which granted benefits without regard to qualifying requirements or maximum duration (34: pp. 12-13, 18-19). Nonetheless, the principles underlying the suspended qualifying-period requirements, ratio provisions, and maximum duration of benefits had important implications for seasonal workers.

Benefit duration, except for uncovenanted benefits, was automatically limited by the ratio provisions and by a flat maximum. The maximum duration of benefits for 1 insurance year (a period extending from July to July) was, in 1920-21, 15 weeks; in 1921-28, 26 weeks. The ratio of weeks of contributions to weeks of benefits was 6 to 1.

From 1920 to 1924, a minimum of 12 contributions paid under the act prior to application for benefits was required for eligibility. 

From 1924 to 1928, 20 contributions paid in the year preceding the application and a total of 30 contributions paid in the 2 insurance years preceding the application were required, except for uncovenanted benefits.

This single requirement of 20 weeks of contributions during the preceding year operated to limit the number of seasonal workers who might qualify for benefits. The two principal classes of workers employed in wholly seasonal occupations were (1) those employed during the season in holiday resorts and (2) those engaged in characteristically seasonal industries, such as fishing, vending ice cream, manufacturing jam, and playing professional soccer.

According to two surveys by the Ministry of Labour in 1930 and 1933, the period of seasonal employment in 1930 for resort workers ranged from 19 to 22 weeks for men and averaged about 19 weeks for women; in 1933 the season was 18 to 26 weeks long for men and 18 to 19 weeks for women. The 1930 figures given above refer to surveys of workers unemployed in January 1931; those for 1933 were based on a study of cases disallowed during that year. If the duration of seasonal work had been similar in 1920-27, many seasonal workers would doubtless have qualified for benefits under the general eligibility conditions in effect during those years. Generally speaking, the season in resorts lasts from 4 to 5 months in the year, and the season for other wholly seasonal trades lasts from 5 to 6 months. Any generalization for one period would have to be applied very cautiously

GREAT BRITAIN 

25 to other periods, but other sources indicate that for the period 1920-27 the average duration of the season in these occupations varied from 4 to 6 months a year so that the qualifying requirement of 20 weeks affected some but not all seasonal workers who had no employment during the off season. It must, however, be noted that, since certain seasonal workers, particularly fish workers, followed the season in various districts, the average length of seasonal employment was probably overstated in the inquiries (35: pp. 208-225).

The data compiled in the 1930 survey of the volume and duration of off-season employment are of especial significance because such information is ordinarily difficult to obtain. Among seasonal workers in holiday resorts, about 20 percent of the men employed in hotels and boarding houses, 22 percent of those employed in sports and games, 25 percent of those engaged in road transport, and 23 percent of seamen, stewards, and yachtsmen had employment during the off season, the average varying from 9 to 11 weeks in the year 1930. Among women workers in holiday resorts, 24 percent of those employed in hotels and boarding houses had off-season employment averaging 11 weeks during 1930, and 21 percent of shop assistants averaged about 

10 weeks' off-season employment.

Among men employed in fishing and similarly seasonal occupations, 20 percent of the maltsters, 57 percent of the salmon fishers, 37 percent of the fish workers, and 25 percent of the ice-cream vendors had off-season employment, averaging from 7 to 10 weeks in the year; among women, 7 percent of the fish workers and 12 percent of the jam and preserve workers had off-season employment, averaging from 

14 to 15 weeks in 1930.

Despite the fact that short periods of off-season employment enabled many seasonal workers to meet the qualifying conditions for eligibility, the Umpire's decisions applying the "genuinely seeking work" and the "available for work" requirements frequently restricted benefits for seasonal workers. These regulations and interpretations, together with the ratio and flat benefit-duration provisions, were designed to safeguard the unemployment fund from excessive drains resulting from payment of benefits to seasonal workers during the off season.

THE UNRESTRICTED PAYMENT OF BENEFITS, 1927-30 

The Unemployment Insurance Act of 1927 (operative April 19, 1928) brought about two important changes with regard to benefit 159727-40- -3

SEASONAL WORKERS 

duration (5). As a supposedly temporary expedient, the ratio provision was abolished and the flat maximum benefit period repealed. 

The qualifying-period requirement was liberalized by changing the specification from 20 weeks of covered employment in the preceding insurance year to 30 weeks in the preceding 2 insurance years. Simultaneously, however, the "genuinely seeking work" requirement was enforced more strictly, particularly by the new provision that every claim, after 78 days of benefits, be reviewed by the courts of referees. 

Of these new provisions, which were the result of the investigation conducted by the Blanesburgh Committee, the Royal Commission on Unemployment Insurance said: "The qualifying rule of 30 contributions was as lenient a condition for the receipt of insurance benefit às could well be provided in an insurance scheme. A worker who had been engaged in insurable employment for 30 successive weeks would be able to qualify for benefit at any time in the following 74 weeks under the relaxed rules for transitional benefit,' a claimant was entitled to a clear run of transitional benefit for 96 weeks, if he had paid 8 contributions immediately before claiming, or for an unlimited period, if he had paid 30 contributions at any time." (Cf. 

24: pp. 27-29.) The Blanesburgh Committee expected that their proposal would disqualify only a small proportion of workers, namely, those who were continuously unemployed for any length of time and who were unable to obtain work for an average of 15 weeks every year. 

Throughout the period 1928-30, claims of persons who worked only during the season were, as a rule, disallowed during the off season on the ground that the claimants were not genuinely seeking work or that they were not available for work. This policy was in line with earlier decisions of the Umpire. The principle that seasonal employment alone would not, as a rule, entitle the worker to benefits was so clearly recognized that provision was made in the act of 1927 whereby a person whose occupation was seasonal and did not ordinarily extend over 18 weeks in the year could obtain a certificate of exemption from coverage. This certificate of exemption relieved such persons from paying the employee contributions, although their employers and the Government remained liable for their share of the total contribution due. This latter provision was to avoid discrimination on the part of the employer against persons not exempted. The number of seasonal workers who took advantage of this exemption was inconsiderable, as indicated in table 2.

Although the number of persons taking advantage of this exemption may seem small in comparison with the total number of insured persons employed in wholly seasonal occupations, the number represents a considerable proportion of unemployed seasonal workers. 

The exact ratio is not known, but a general conclusion may be arrived at from a survey made by the British Ministry of Labour (cf. 35: p. 209). Since most of the seasonal trades are summer trades, the maximum seasonal unemployment would probably occur in January.

The actual percentage was smaller because a substantial number of unemployed seasonal workers-those who had not registered and those engaged in winter employmentwere not included in the total of registered unemployed.

THE ANOMALIES REGULATIONS, 1931-33 

The provisions of the 1930 unemployment insurance law (6) were based, to some extent, upon the report of the so-called Morris Committee (20) which had been appointed in July 1929 as a result of increasing criticism of the disallowances imposed under the "genuinely seeking work" clause. 

With regard to seasonal workers, the most important change made by the 1930 act was the repeal of the "genuinely seeking work but unable to obtain suitable employment" provision, for which was substituted a clause by which a claimant was disqualified for a period of 1 to 6 weeks if it was proved by the insurance officer (onus of proof placed upon him, not the claimant) that (a) the claimant without good cause refused or failed to apply for or refused to accept a suitable situation, or (b) the claimant without good cause refused or failed to carry out any written directions given him by the employment office to assist him in obtaining suitable employment. 

The emphasis in applying the general provisions of the unemploy-

GREAT BRITAIN 

29 ment insurance law to seasonal workers thus shifted from a restrictive interpretation of the "genuinely seeking work” clause to disqualification for refusal of suitable employment. The consequences of this shift were apparent. The maximum disqualification period was reduced to 6 weeks instead of the duration of the off season (unless it was ruled that a claimant was not "available for work," which remained a condition for eligibility). The provision that a worker had to prove that he was "unable to obtain suitable employment," which had been an integral part of the benefit eligibility requirements since 1911, was abolished, and the burden of proof was transferred from the claimant to the employment office, which had to show that the applicant had refused suitable work. The definition of suitable work, especially with regard to seasonal workers, became one of the most important factors in addition to the "available for work" clause.

A decision of the Umpire, dated January 15, 1931, which disqualified a seasonal worker (a chambermaid) who had refused off-season employment at wages lower than those paid her in the same locality during the season, demonstrates the general principle followed in the determination of suitability of work with regard to wages.¹¹ 

There is no doubt that the liberalized provisions of the 1930 act admitted to benefits certain seasonal workers who were not seriously interested in work during the off season. From a general point of view, the Royal Commission on Unemployment Insurance outlined the problem as follows: ". . . we find the present disqualification for benefit, as contained in section 4 of the Unemployment Insurance Act, 1930, to be largely noneffective in checking the claims of persons who do not desire to work or are not making reasonable efforts to obtain work, and we do not think the test in its present form could be made effective" (24: p. 235). It was often uncertain whether seasonal workers wished to obtain full-time employment or preferred to rely on their seasonal work. The commission felt that a flat limited duration of benefits, together with additional days of benefits on the basis of a ratio provision, was the best check of an abuse of the system by such workers.

The Unemployment Insurance (No. 3) Act of 1931 was designed to cope with difficulties which had existed long before the passage of the 1930 law but which had since become apparent. The 1931 "Anomalies Act” required the Minister of Labour to formulate regu- "See appendix A, chart III, Disqualification on account of refusal of suitable work, Case No. 1047/31.

SEASONAL WORKERS 

lations for certain classes of workers, including seasonal workers, imposing "such additional conditions and terms with respect to the receipt of benefit and such restrictions on the amount and period of benefit, and make such modifications in the provisions of the Unemployment Insurance Acts relating to the determination of claims for benefit, as may appear necessary" to remove certain difficulties which had arisen.

Seasonal workers to whom such special regulations were to apply were defined as "persons whose normal employment is employment for portions of the year only in occupations which are of a seasonal nature." To assist the Minister of Labour in drawing up these regulations, a special advisory committee was to be appointed, consisting of a chairman and other members, three of whom were to be chosen after consultation with the General Council of the Trades Union Congress, three after consultation with the National Confederation of Employers' Organizations, and one after consultation with the Treasury (7: pp. 1-3). 

This committee was appointed by the Minister of Labour on 

August 31, 1931, and made its first report on September 28, 1931. A draft regulation submitted to the Advisory Committee by the Minister of Labour defined the term "seasonal workers" and determined their benefit rights during the off season as follows:

A member of the class of persons whose normal employment is employment for a portion or portions of the year only in an occupation or occupations of a seasonal nature was to be entitled to benefits for any days of unemployment during the off season (the portion or portions of the year in which members of that class of persons are not normally employed in any of these seasonal occupations) only if, in addition to all other benefit eligibility requirements, he fulfilled two conditions; (a) he should have to prove covered employment during the off season to a "substantial" extent during each of the three years immediately preceding his application for benefits, and (b) taking into account all the circumstances of his individual case, his former experience and employment, and the industrial character of the district of his residence, he should be able reasonably to expect to obtain covered employment in his district during a substantial part of the off season. An occupation was to be considered seasonal if, during a substantial portion of each year, no considerable amount of employment was normally available in the particular district (21). 

Two main principles stand out in this determination. The test of whether or not a claimant was a seasonal worker was based on the nature of the person's normal employment, i. e., that from which he obtained his principal means of livelihood. Such a practice avoided the disadvantages connected with the Austrian and German regula-

GREAT BRITAIN 

31 tions which involved only the occupation or establishment in which the worker happened to be engaged when he became unemployed. 

The second principle was the complete administrative decentralization of the application of the special provisions for seasonal workers, a method which had proved disastrous in Germany in 1927 but which was operating satisfactorily in Austria. No attempt was made to determine the length of the season by statute as was done in Germany, Austria, and Poland. In Great Britain, it was left to the local courts of referees to determine the duration of the season in the district, and these courts based their decisions upon the economic conditions prevailing in the region. 

The requirement that off-season employment be covered employment might seem to have imposed a severe hardship in many cases. 

This was unusual, however, because coverage under the British law is almost all-inclusive. It nevertheless discouraged work in noncovered employment during the off season instead of encouraging employment in work of any type, whether covered or noncovered. 

The Advisory Committee, commenting on the draft regulations submitted by the Minister of Labour, objected to the requirement of 3 years' off-season employment in a covered occupation for establishing a record of substantial covered employment during the off season and held that 2 years would be sufficient. They pointed out, furthermore, that the draft left some doubt about the status of persons who follow a seasonal occupation in more than one district when the various seasons do not coincide, as in the herring-curing industry. 

The Advisory Committee doubted also whether the regulations as drafted would be applicable to persons whose normal employment is at a holiday or health resort during holiday seasons only. They suggested making the special provisions applicable to seasonal workers during the off season (a) when their normal employment was in one district only, in which case the off season was to be considered that part or those parts of the year during which persons are not normally employed in that occupation in the district in question; (b) when their employment was carried on in more than one district, in which case the off season was to be considered that part or those parts of the year during which persons are not normally employed in that occupation in the districts in question; and (c) when their normal employment was in an occupation at a holiday or health resort in which employment is largely dependent on the presence of visitors, in which case the off season was to be that part or those parts of the year other than the holiday seasons.

SEASONAL WORKERS 

The regulations were enacted on October 2, 1931, in the form suggested by the Advisory Committee and became effective on the following day (11). The general purpose of these "Anomalies Regulations," to make payment of benefits to seasonal workers during the off season conditional upon their willingness to accept employment in the off season, is expressed in the statement of the Royal Commission on Unemployment Insurance:

The workers dependent on seasonal trades can secure full employment only by supplementing their main trade by some other trade in the off season; this may, in the circumstances, be impossible, and before unemployment insurance was introduced they may have been forced to subsist in the off season on the earnings of their seasonal employment (24: p. 88).

When the Advisory Committee considered the draft regulations, the question arose whether the payment of contributions by seasonal workers during those times of the year "when they have little or no risk of being unemployed" should not give them a right to draw a limited amount of benefits during the off season without the application of special conditions requiring them to prove their desire to obtain work in the off season (21: p. 4). This step, however, was not considered necessary if the special regulations for the off season were reasonable, and the Advisory Committee unanimously was of the opinion that the special provisions should be applied from the beginning of the off season. The Committee might have added that seasonal workers are subject to the risk of unemployment during the season as well. During the period from October 1931, when the draft regulations became operative, to June 1932, about 27,000 claims for benefits during the off season were disallowed. An analysis of 10 percent of these cases was made in order to ascertain the extent to which seasonal workers had received benefits during the season. 

A large number of seasonal occupations were included in the sample: 

Hotel and boarding-house workers, attendants (pier, beach, chair), fish workers, seamen on pleasure boats, ice-cream vendors, sugar-beet workers, shop assistants in holiday resorts, jam and preserve workers, and workers in several other occupations numerically less important. 

Their employment records during the 1931 season showed the following situation: The average duration of the 1931 season as a whole was a little over 5 months for men and slightly less than 5 months for women. About 38 percent of the men had a season lasting 6 months or more, 44 percent had a season lasting from 4 to 6 months, and 18

GREAT BRITAIN 

33 percent a season lasting less than 4 months; for women these percentages were 21, 66, and 13, respectively. Of the men, 63.4 percent received benefits at some time during the season; and of the women, 60.5 percent. For men, the average number of benefit days during the season was 43, almost one-third of the average season; for women, the average number of benefit days was 39, about 30 percent of the season. Occupational differences were apparent in the amount of benefits drawn, but these differences were less conspicuous for women than for men. More than 50 percent of men and women drawing benefits during the season received payments for not more than onefourth the duration of the season, and about 20 percent received them for more than one-half of the season. "The analysis showed," concludes the Ministry of Labour, "that the total amount of contributions paid during the season by persons disallowed benefit during the off season, together with the contributions paid in respect of them by their employers and by the Government, was considerably less than the amount of benefit drawn by such of them who were unemployed during the season" (37).
