1932_ 19331934. 100.00 18.45 19.72 18.81 19.85 12.92 10.25 1935--- 1936__ The decentralized administration of the seasonal workers' order is one of the outstanding characteristics of the British system. By this means the law is adjusted to particular local conditions and to individual circumstances. The possibility of diverse interpretations, however, presents a disadvantage, as was pointed out in the minority report of the Royal Commission on Unemployment Insurance:

In the absence of any central guidance either from the Ministry of Labour or the Umpire, insurance officers [who make the initial determination] and courts of referees [who act as appeal tribunals for first appeals] were left to interpret the regulations in their own way. Many disallowances resulted which were subsequently shown on appeal to the Umpire to have been wrongly imposed. It is quite clear that, if the Umpire had issued rules to courts of referees and insurance officers when the regulations came into effect, it would have avoided, not only the disallowance of benefit in a large number of cases, with consequent distress to claimants, who, as subsequent events showed, were qualified for benefit, but also a considerable amount of unnecessary work for courts of referees and the Umpire (24: p. 437)."

The charge made by the minority report, that too great diversity of interpretation is permitted by decentralized administration, seems hardly justified in view of the difficulties incident to the early administration of the new provisions. The claims adjudication experience of insurance officers and courts of referees, together with the cumulative decisions of the Umpire, would, it was expected, form a body of precedent both for future determinations and for further legislation (see appendix A, chart III).

The principles underlying the seasonal workers' regulations of 1931 were expressed in the first report of the Royal Commission on Unemployment Insurance, which had found that under the more liberal conditions established by the Unemployment Insurance Act of 1930, it had become possible for many wholly seasonal workers Material in brackets interpolated by author.

GREAT BRITAIN 

35 to receive benefits each year during the entire off season. It was pointed out that many of the certificates of exemption which had been taken out in 1929 were canceled by the workers in 1930. "In our view," the Commission said, "a worker who habitually obtains his (or her) living for the year by work in a seasonal occupation for a part of the year, should not be deemed to be unemployed and qualified for benefit during that part of the year which is the off season." It recommended that (1) seasonal workers should be entitled to benefits during the season under the general provisions of the law applying to any claimant, but that (2) during the off season a claimant who, according to his employment record and the judgment of the insurance officer, was a seasonal worker should not be entitled to benefits unless he could prove to the court of referees (a) that he had in the past worked during the off season for a reasonable time in covered employment, and (b) that, in view of the industrial circumstances of the district, he could reasonably expect to obtain covered employment during the off season in the district in which he was residing at that time (cf. 22: pp. 44-46).

Between October 3, 1931, when the new regulations became effective, and April 29, 1933, when the Ministry of Labour reported on the operation of these provisions, the courts of referees considered 59,650 cases, in 15,482 of which benefit was allowed, and in 44,168 of which benefit was disallowed; during the same period the Umpire in 5,359 appealed cases allowed 2,762 claims and disallowed 2,597.

This total number probably included some duplications, for some persons doubtless had more than one claim during the period (cf. 

25: p. 4). Typical classes of claimants affected by the new regulations were (1) those in holiday resorts: waitresses and servants in boarding houses, hotels, and the like; attendants at amusement places, beaches, piers; swimming attendants; yacht hands; clerks and shop assistants; (2) those in other localities: fishermen; professional soccer players; maltsters; bookmakers' clerks; and icecream makers and vendors.

Since the statute was drawn up in general terms subject to local interpretation, the Umpire was called upon to make a series of important decisions and interpretations to serve as a guide for insurance officers and courts of referees and to clarify certain questions: (1) What is seasonal employment in the individual case and when is seasonal employment a person's normal employment, making him a seasonal worker subject to the regulations? (2) What is the duration of the season and the off season? (3) What is a "sub-

SEASONAL WORKERS 

stantial" period of employment during the off season? (4) What is the status of a claimant who follows two or more occupations during the year?

Seasonal Employment Defined 

The regulations defined seasonal employment in a general way as employment in any district in occupations in which no substantial amount of work is normally available in that district during a certain part of the year, or as employment at a holiday or health resort which is largely dependent on the presence of visitors during the season. Seasonality was thus determined by the locality (work place), the occupation, timing, and employment opportunities. A seasonal worker was defined as "a member of the class of persons whose normal employment is employment for a part or parts of the year only in an occupation or occupations of a seasonal nature.' 

The Umpire rendered a series of decisions on determining when a seasonal employment is normal employment. On November 26, 1931, 7 weeks after the new regulations had gone into effect, he ruled, in disallowing a claim of an ice-cream salesman:

A person's normal employment is not a seasonal employment because for one or two seasons he has been able to obtain seasonal work only, despite his desire for employment which is not seasonal. When, however, over a number of years an insured contributor has had no substantial period of employment outside his seasonal employment, and when that has been substantial, a strong presumption is raised that seasonal employment is his normal employment. To say that a man is normally employed in a seasonal employment is only another way of saying that as a general rule he is employed in a seasonal employment.10

In another decision the Umpire ruled that if a claimant had in the past had nonseasonal employment, the fact that he had for the past two seasons engaged in seasonal employment only was not in itself evidence that seasonal employment had become his normal employment." Generally speaking, it was not presumed that such seasonal work had become the claimant's normal employment until he had engaged in seasonal work for three seasons without doing any considerable amount of other work between seasons. Even then, in certain cases it was possible to refute this presumption if he could show that he had taken up seasonal work because, in spite of his 

1 See appendix A, chart III, Seasonal employment deemed to be "normal employment," 

Case No. 23143/31. 

14 See appendix A, chart III, Seasonal employment deemed to be "normal employment,"

Case No. 1035/32.

GREAT BRITAIN 

37 efforts, he had failed to get nonseasonal work. This liberal application was particularly important in view of steadily increasing economic difficulties, especially in depressed areas. 

It may be noted, on the other hand, that in Germany and Austria the application of fixed schedules of time periods and occupations had made it difficult to differentiate between bona fide seasonal I workers and those who were in the seasonal category by chance and I who happened to lose their jobs during the strictly defined "off season." In Great Britain, however, persons employed in a seasonal occupation during part of the year but normally employed throughout the rest of the year in other work, whether covered or not, were not disqualified during the off season.

The nature of seasonal employment, which was the basis for the decision whether a person was affected by the seasonal workers' special provisions, was defined by the Umpire as follows: 

It should be stated that Regulation 2 [i.e., the seasonal workers' regulation] was not meant to deprive of benefit persons whose occupations were in a trade or business (other than at holiday or health resorts) which fluctuated in volume at different seasons of the year, and who were unemployed because of the reduction in the number of employees during the slack season. The fact that more people are employed in the busier season of a trade or business than at the slack season does not constitute the occupations which are followed therein occupations of a seasonal nature. 

There are occupations of various kinds which are followed in connection with a trade which is governed mainly, if not entirely, by the requirements of the customers of the trade, with the consequence that the trade fluctuates, and in that respect may be described generally as being of a seasonal nature, but it is not strictly seasonal in the sense that it is limited to a particular period of time, either by natural agencies (as in the case of the fruit season) or by the supply of raw material (as in fish curing) or by legislation (as in salmon fishing) or by rules (as in association football)."

The Umpire ruled in the same decision that, before a claimant's occupation could be considered seasonal, there should be evidence that employment is afforded year after year for a certain period only, such as the season at a holiday or health resort, the salmon-fishing season as regulated by act of Parliament, or the hop-picking or herring-curing seasons, which are controlled by climate. On the other hand, the Umpire held that if the claimant is engaged in an occupation which decreases in volume at certain seasons of the year (as in the building and tailoring trades), and in which employment 15 Material in brackets interpolated by author. See appendix A, chart III, Definition and interpretation of seasonal employment, Case No. 1088/33.

SEASONAL WORKERS 

has been lost year after year not because a definite season for which the person is engaged has ended, but because there is less work and consequently fewer people are needed-that occupation cannot be considered seasonal. In such cases the seasonal workers' regulations were not to be applied.

To determine whether a person's seasonal employment was his normal employment, the local authorities had access to the claimant's current unemployment book, showing payment of contributions for one insurance year, to their own records (the unemployed person's register), and to the claimant's previous employment record at Kew. 

The insurance officer cannot disallow claims of seasonal workers but must refer such cases to the courts of referees. If he is not satisfied with the decision of the court of referees, he may refer the case to the Umpire. 

Extent of the Season and Off Season 

After it had been established that a claimant's normal employment was in an occupation considered seasonal according to the terms of the statute and in the light of the Umpire's decisions, it was necessary to determine the limits of the season and of the off season. 

In the terms of the statute, "off season" was defined as "that part or those parts of the year during which persons are not normally employed in that occupation in that district." "That occupation" meant the seasonal worker's normal employment, and "that district" referred to the district in which the person was normally employed. 

If a person was normally employed in more than one district, the term referred to all those districts in which the person was employed during the year, as when fish workers followed the various fishing seasons along the coasts of England and Scotland. The off season for employment in health and holiday resorts was to be considered all the year except the holiday season.

Neither the statute nor the Umpire defined "off season" in fixed terms of months or dates, this being one of the determinations left to the courts of referees. As a rule the Umpire did not question the determination of the "season" or "off season" arrived at by the courts of referees. "I hesitate," he said in an appeal brought before him, "to differ from the court of referees, whose local knowledge of the duration of the season or seasons has been applied in the determination of this case." 16 ... 

18 See appendix A, chart III, Extent of off season, Case No. 24784/31.

GREAT BRITAIN 

39 The Umpire issued more detailed instructions, however, for determining the duration of the "season" in holiday and health resorts when he decided that the season was to be determined by the presence of visitors at the resort as a whole rather than at one particular hotel.¹7

Benefit Eligibility During the Off Season 

If a claimant was a seasonal worker to whom the special regulation applied and if he had become unemployed during the off season, he was not entitled to benefits during the off season, except when he could prove (note that the onus of proof is on the worker) that he had fulfilled, in addition to the general eligibility requirements (30 weeks of covered employment in the 2 years preceding his claim, registration with an employment office, capability of and availability for work), the two following conditions: (a) Within each year of the period of 2 years immediately preceding the date of his application for benefits, he must have been employed in covered employment during the off season to a substantial extent, and (b) Taking into account all the circumstances of the claimant's caseand particularly in view of the claimant's industrial experience—and taking into consideration the industrial circumstances of the district in which he is residing, he must have reasonable expectation of obtaining covered employment during a substantial period of the off season. 

A claimant had to fulfill both requirements in order to become eligible for benefits during the off season. The Umpire overruled a court of referees which had allowed the claim of a waitress who had fulfilled the second condition, that of reasonable expectation of obtaining employment, but who was unable to show off-season employment during the 2 preceding years except for some employment for week ends in 2 different months. "The court of referees appears to have overlooked the fact that the requirements of . . . (a) must also be satisfied before benefit can be allowed." 18 The term "within each year" in subsection (a) was interpreted by the Umpire as not to allow "averaging" the off-season employment of a single year over a 2-year period. A "substantial" amount of off-season work must have been obtained in each of the off seasons occurring during the two 12-month periods immediately preceding the application for benefits.19 

17 See appendix A, chart III, Extent of off season, Case No. 1464/33. ... 

18 See appendix A, chart III, No "substantial" period of covered employment during the off season, Case No. 23269/31. 

19 See appendix A, chart III, No "substantial" period of covered employment during the off season, Case No. 25019/31.

SEASONAL WORKERS 

The rather indefinite "substantial extent" to which a claimant must prove covered off-season employment was generally interpreted by the authorities to mean covered employment for 25 percent of the off season. The Umpire's insistence upon this minimum employment, although apparently a deviation from the principle of local and individual determination, was actually in accord with it because this uniform interpretation of "a substantial period of the off season" insures equable decisions, based upon the proportion of the off season spent in employment rather than upon its calendar duration. 

The term "substantial" was interpreted in several decisions of the Umpire. In Case No. 5796/32, upholding a decision of a court of referees, he said: ". . . .. Examination of a number of cases of employees at the . . . racing track in question shows that they are in employment for not less than 75 percent of the year "This decision referred to a dispute as to whether or not certain workers should be affected by the seasonal workers' regulations; other decisions show that the term "substantial" was used interchangeably with "a substantial period." 20

A claimant, normally employed during the off season, who was unable, on account of illness or other disability, to show covered offseason employment to a substantial extent, was held ineligible for benefits during the off season by an Umpire's decision of January 23, 1932.21

Multiple Occupations During the Year 

A rather complex question arose in connection with the seasonal workers' regulation in cases where a person had two jobs which kept him busy throughout the year, each job offering employment for a part of the year only. The Umpire ruled that, if the two jobs were held regularly over an extended period of time and offered yearround employment, the claimant was not to be considered a seasonal worker. It made no difference whether one of those jobs was either seasonal or noncovered. In one case, a person had for 23 years been a pointsman on an electrical amusement railway for 5 months in the year, from May to October, and during the intervening months, from November to April, he had been regularly employed as a woodchopper with one employer.22 In another case a worker had, for the preced- 

20 See appendix A, chart III, Definition and interpretation of seasonal employment, Case 

No. 6366/32.

See appendix A, chart III, Seasonal employment owing to disability deemed to be "normal employment," Case No. 2081/32. "See appendix A, chart III, Two employments followed during the year, Case No. 6232/32.

GREAT BRITAIN 

41 ing 4 years, been engaged as a maltster during the season which lasted from October to May. During the off season in each of those years he had had noncovered employment as a farm laborer. In both cases the Umpire held that the claimants were not seasonal workers and were therefore entitled to benefits at any time if they had fulfilled the general benefit eligibility conditions. Although they had been normally employed in two occupations of a seasonal nature, their employment had not been for "a part or parts of the year only." 28 

The experience gained during the first 2 years of operation of the special seasonal workers' provisions showed that in the last 3 months of 1931 a total of 19,063 claims of seasonal workers were disallowed, 20,490 in 1932 and 19,432 in 1933.24 A large proportion of these disallowances may have related to the same individuals in successive years and some to the same person in the same year. When the 

Unemployment Insurance Statutory Committee reported in 1935 on the seasonal workers' orders, they expressed the opinion that, at the end of the first year's operation, the guidance furnished by the Umpire's decisions on the 1931 regulations was expected to make possible the payment of benefits in cases in which benefits had formerly been disallowed by the courts of referees (cf. 26: p. 6). It is also probable that many seasonal workers, after having had their claims disallowed in the first year, did not apply for benefits in subsequent years. This would fulfill the prediction of some critics that the regulations would be applied to more and more workers as time went on. Subsequent developments in the period 1934-36, however, show a considerable reduction in the number of disallowed claims. 

In estimating the results of the disqualifications under the seasonal workers' provisions, the Unemployment Insurance Statutory Committee pointed out that 4 out of every 1,000 applications were disallowed under these provisions. The disallowances were almost evenly divided between men and women. The saving for the unemployment fund, i. e., the amount of benefits which would have been paid out had special regulations not existed, was estimated by the Statutory Committee to have been between £200,000 and £400,000 a year (as compared with a total expenditure of £40,600,000 for benefits during the year 1934) or approximately 1 percent of the total amount paid for benefits in a year (26: p. 6). This estimate corresponds roughly to the finding that 0.7 percent of the total number registered as unemployed in January 1931 were "seasonal workers" (36). The Com- 

22 See appendix A, chart III, Two employments followed during the year, Case No. 17136/32. 

24 Disallowed by the courts of referees. Insurance officers could not disallow such claims. 10-

SEASONAL WORKERS 

mittee pointed out, however, that its estimate was hardly accurate enough to indicate actual savings to the fund; nor was it a measure of the additional cost to the unemployment fund if the special regulations were repealed altogether, because many seasonal workers, who knew that they had no chance to qualify for benefits during the off season, would then file applications.

THE ANOMALIES REGULATIONS, 1933-35 On August 12, 1933, the Advisory Committee on Draft Regula tions, which had been appointed in 1931 and was responsible for the seasonal workers' regulations of that year, suggested several changes on the basis of their 2 years' experience and in the light of the Umpire's decisions. Under the existing regulations, a worker, in order to be eligible for benefits during the off season, besides fulfilling other requirements had to prove that "having regard to the circumstances of his case, and particularly to his industrial experience and to the industrial circumstances of the district in which he is residing, he can reasonably expect to obtain insurable employment in that district during a substantial period of the off season." The Umpire had interpreted "district in which he is residing" to mean the locality where a claimant happened to be residing on the exact date of his application for benefits.25 So strict an interpretation meant a serious handicap for persons whose seasonal employment took them into various districts during the year, because many persons who could reasonably have expected to find work in a neighboring district found it impossible to qualify for benefits. The Umpire pointed out the difference between "is residing" and "resides," apparently having in mind a possible amendment of the phrase. The Advisory Committee promptly suggested the substitution of "resides" and the deletion of the phrase "in that district." Regarding the determination of seasonal work as the normal employment of persons previously engaged in nonseasonal occupations, the Advisory Committee made no suggestions because, they explained, the decisions of the Umpire had formulated adequate standards. They did, however, suggest the greatest vigilance in bringing the Umpire's attitude to the notice of all workers who, through ignorance of the general policy, might refuse temporary seasonal jobs from fear of being classified as seasonal workers.

See appendix A, chart III, Employment available in claimant's district of residence, Case No. 3818/32.

GREAT BRITAIN 

43 An even more significant change, likewise originating in a decision of the Umpire, was suggested by the Advisory Committee with regard to the period preceding application for benefits during which a person must have had covered off-season employment to a substantial extent. Under the 1931 regulations, a claimant applying for off-season benefits, whose normal employment was seasonal, had, among other conditions, to prove "that he has within each year of the period of 2 years immediately preceding the date of his application for benefit, been employed in insurable employment during the off season to a substantial extent." The clause "immediately preceding the date of his application for benefit" was poorly phrased because it introduced a period which changed every time the claimant became unemployed during the off season after a period of off-season employment, and also every time he signed the unemployment register at the local office for the purpose of keeping his claim for benefits up to date, a process sometimes required every day during his unemployment. Thus "the period of 2 years immediately preceding the date of his application for benefit" had to be determined and analyzed each time the claimant reported at the employment office. This was so impracticable that the Advisory Committee proposed to substitute for the changing period of 2 years prior to the application for benefits a fixed period of 2 years preceding the beginning of the current off season. This time period was "fixed," in that it did not change for the whole length of the off season, yet flexible, because the beginning of the off season was not definitely fixed by the calendar but by actual local conditions. The adoption of a fixed period ending with the beginning of the current off season would, however, have penalized seasonal workers who had qualified for benefits because they had had substantial covered employment during the current off season and sufficient employment during the preceding off season. In order to give such persons an opportunity to qualify for benefits, the Advisory Committee suggested this alternative to the requirement of substantial off-season employment during the 2 years preceding the date when the current off season started: off-season employment in 1 of the 

2 preceding years and employment during the current off season if it was substantial in relation to the duration of the current off season. 

These proposals were enacted, on August 21, 1933, by the Unemploy ment Insurance Anomalies (Amendment) Regulations of 1933 and became effective on September 7. Together with the 1931 regulations, they were the statutory basis for the treatment of the seasonal workers' problem until 1935 (13).

SEASONAL WORKERS 

Under the 1933 regulations, the Umpire issued a number of decisions which are worthy of mention. One dealt with the length of the salmon-fishing off season which, according to law, lasts from September 1 to February 15 of the following year, both dates inclusive.

During this time it is unlawful to catch salmon otherwise than by rod and line; i. e., particularly by means of nets. The claimant in question had for several years been employed in salmon fishing for the duration of the whole season as defined by law and for a time after September 1. In the period between the first of September and the date of separation, he was busy with drying and storing the nets and with other work incidental to closing the season. The Umpire decided that the claimant should be entitled to benefits for the days between September 1 and September 16, the latter being the day on which the claimant and most of the other salmon fishers were actually dismissed.20 This decision is another instance of the individual treatment, independent of the calendar and of other fixed schedules, accorded seasonal workers.

Important, though technical in nature, was the Umpire's decision of April 8, 1935, regarding the duration of the season in cases where the amount of seasonal work varies each year. The court of referees had, in the case of a beet-sugar worker, inferentially fixed the date of the beginning of the next season by determining the off-season period beforehand. The Umpire, however, said that it was evident that such findings had been based in part on forecast rather than fact because experience had shown that the season varied considerably in that particular district; and that unless the off season was properly determined "it would not be possible in many cases to say, when a claim is made, whether the conditions for receipt of benefit had been fulfilled." 27 In several decisions the Umpire had expressed the opinion that the season was to be defined by its normal extent and that its defined duration should not vary from year to year merely because more or less work was available in a particular seasonal industry or occupation. There were, of course, annual variations as at holiday resorts where the season is fixed with reference to Easter or Whitsunday, 7 weeks later. Otherwise, the period of the season was changed by the operation of permanent factors, either natural or technical. In the case under consideration, however, the variation of the season was not the result of weather conditions or of the size of the 

26 See appendix A, chart III, Extent of the season, Case No. 15879/33.

See appendix A, chart III, Determination of the season, Case No. 3499/35.

GREAT BRITAIN 

45 crop in various years, but was forecast by the court of referees. "It is in the nature of a season," the Umpire explained, "that it should be of regular annual recurrence and that it should from year to year cover approximately the same period. If, in a particular year, those in a position to control a particular seasonal industry decide that for trade or other reasons the industry should be suspended in that year, it could not be held, in my opinion, that the season did not exist in that year or that the claim of a seasonal worker could be disallowed on the footing that it was an off season."

In determining the beginning and end of the off season, the courts of referees usually employed the following procedure, particularly when other important data were not available: The beginning of the season was fixed as the date upon which 25 percent or more of all the workers in the industry became employed and the termination as the date by which more than 75 percent of the workers were discharged. This determination by the courts of referees was, of course, applicable only to the district over which each court had jurisdiction. 

The Umpire regarded this method as "a useful guide in determining, from an examination of conditions over a number of years, the duration of the season in a particular industry and district, but not as a standard by which it could be decided that a season varied from one year to another." These percentages of 25 and 75 were based upon the Umpire's interpretation of the term "substantial" as normally equal to 25 percent of the regular volume or duration of seasonal employment. The definition of "seasonal occupation" ruled employment as seasonal if no substantial amount of work was available during the off season; thus the off season began when less than 25 percent of the normal volume of work was available, which presumably would be the case when more than 75 percent of the workers were separated. 

The Umpire qualified his decision by ruling that this method should not be considered standard, for it would be impossible to apply it in such cases as the determination of the season at holiday resorts. 

The status of younger workers who had just entered the labor market was determined by the Umpire in a decision of November 30, 1933. 

If a person's first employment was seasonal,28 and he became unemployed at the end of the season so that he could not prove off-season employment to a substantial extent in two preceding off seasons, he "See appendix A, chart III, Seasonal employment deemed to be "normal employment,"

Case No. 15695/33.

SEASONAL WORKERS 

was not entitled to benefits during the off season, because seasonal employment was considered his normal employment. 

The 1933 amendment, which made it possible to substitute the current off season for one of the seasons in the previous 2 years, further liberalized benefit eligibility for the off season, by reducing the period of disqualification. Before 1933, the disqualification referred to the whole period of the current off season even when it consisted of 2 or more separate periods. A seasonal worker in a holiday resort might have worked during two seasons of the year, one at Easter for 5 days and another during the summer season, from Whitsuntide to about October 23. There were, therefore, two parts of the year "other than the holiday seasons" which were considered the off season. When a claimant became unemployed on October 24, and could not prove any off-season employment, he was disqualified from benefits during the off season. Under the 1931 regulations his benefits would have been disallowed during any part of the off season which in this case, would have been the period from October 24 to Whitsunday of the following year, regardless of whether he had had, in previous years, 5 days' employment around Easter. Under the 1933 regulations, however, he could be disqualified only for the duration of the current off season, which was from October 24 to Easter Sunday of the following year.

If he could then fulfill the benefit eligibility requirements, he would become entitled to benefits during the current off season (Easter to 

Whitsunday).29 A similar decision was made by the Umpire on

March 15, 1934, in which the claimant had three seasons, and therefore three off-season periods, in the year.80

Whether or not a person's normal employment in a seasonal occupation is still to be considered his normal employment after he has, because of old age or permanent disability, become incapable of exercising that occupation, was the subject of several of the Umpire's decisions. It was ruled that such a person's former occupation could not be considered his normal employment since he was not seeking such employment and was permanently barred from it by his disability; he was, therefore, not subject to the seasonal workers' regulations. 81 20 This example is based on the Umpire's decision, Period of current off season, Case

THE SEASONAL WORKERS' ORDER, 1935 

The regulations of 1931 and 1933 affected two classes of workers when they were normally engaged in seasonal employment-the group employed at holiday and health resorts and the group employed in fishing and similar occupations. The regulations were not aimed at industries like building (which was treated as seasonal in Germany and Austria) or clothing, in which, in spite of considerable seasonal fluctuation, there is, in Great Britain, a substantial amount of work throughout the whole year and in which the work, even at holiday resorts, does not depend upon the presence of visitors. 

In spite of the flexibility of the statutory provisions with their decentralized administration and individual application to districts and persons, it was inevitable that the operation of the seasonal workers' orders should result in individual inequities and apparent inconsistencies. A significant modification of the British unemployment insurance system was effected when the Unemployment Act of 1934 distinguished sharply between unemployment insurance and unemployment assistance. The latter became a separate system of assistance for workers ineligible for insurance benefits, financed out of Government funds. Its adoption abolished the so-called transitional payments (emergency or extended benefits) and reestablished the insurance character of the system which, through borrowing from the National Treasury, had accumulated a debt of £115 million. Besides this financial reorganization, the most important modification was the provision for additional days of benefits. In addition to the flat maximum duration of 156 days in a benefit year, a person was entitled to further days of benefits in the same benefit year at the rate of 3 days' benefits for every 5 weekly contributions paid during the preceding 5 years, less 1 day for every 5 days of benefits received during those 5 years (8: pp. 3-4).

At the same time the volume of unemployment in Great Britain, which had ranged from 20.7 to 22.8 percent of the total insured population in 1932, dropped to 17.4-22.8 percent in 1933, to 15.9-18.5 percent in 1934, and to 13.9-17.5 percent in 1935 (cf. 31: pp. 49-50).

At the request of the Minister of Labour, the Unemployment Insurance Statutory Committee, created by the act of 1934 as an advisory committee on unemployment insurance, reported, on July 5, 1935, on the operation of the seasonal workers' regulations. This report was based on hearings at which representatives of workers' and em-

SEASONAL WORKERS 

ployers' associations had an opportunity to express their views and suggestions and at which local officers of the Ministry of Labour in districts particularly concerned with seasonal workers were examined orally (cf. 26).

Referring to the statement of the Royal Commission on Unemployment Insurance that the seriousness of the seasonal problem lay not only in the unnecessary expenditure involved but also in its "effect on the repute of the scheme" and its "encouragement of methods of industrial organization which may be harmful to trade and employment in general," the Statutory Committee recommended that the principles of the provisions in force be retained, but suggested certain amendments in order to avert certain abuses prevalent in the years 1931-35. The Statutory Committee pointed out that, at the time when the Royal Commission made its report, "the repute of the insurance scheme was, undoubtedly, being damaged by the spectacle of persons who had never sought work in off seasons, who did not propose to do so, and were not in obvious need, or worse off than they had been for generations, settling down at the end of each season to draw benefit with unfailing regularity till the next season began. 

Nor can it well be questioned that the possibility of this happening must weaken the incentive, on the one hand, to employers to spread work over the seasons and, on the other hand, of seasonal employees to seek alternative occupations." This danger, in the Statutory Committee's opinion, still existed in 1935, for reports of the divisional controllers to the Ministry of Labour showed that many people did not appear ordinarily to want work except during the season.32 Requiring seasonal workers to pay contributions during the season while denying them benefits during the off season, the Statutory Committee reasoned, was no departure from the insurance principle even when applied to those workers who sought work during the off season but could not obtain it. "On the contrary, insurance means insurance against a risk; insurance against a certainty is not insurance but subsidy." As was shown previously, seasonal workers were exposed to about the same degree of unemployment risk during the season as were nonseasonal workers; therefore the majority did not apply for exemption from payment of contributions, but retained coverage in view of the risk of unemployment during the season.

Of certain changes considered at length by the Statutory Committee, one was to establish schedules of seasonal and nonseasonal ** The divisional controllers are the heads of the divisional offices (nine in the country) whose function is solely that of supervising the operations of the local offices.

GREAT BRITAIN 

49 occupations in order to define the groups of persons affected by the seasonal workers' special regulations. The Statutory Committee felt, however, that these definitions would give rise to more difficulties than they would solve and would involve "extremely contentious demarcations of industries. The line taken by the present orders is on the whole logical, leaving the Umpire to consider on the merits of each case what is seasonal work, in place of having to interpret a series of names of interlocking industries and occupations." " 83 A second proposal, which, incidentally, is incorporated in a modified form in the Belgian voluntary unemployment insurance system, was to establish a special seasonal workers' system. Seasonal workers were to have the option of registering in a special system in which both contributions and benefits would be paid only during the season." The administrative difficulties of separating seasonal from nonseasonal workers are obviously great, particularly in Great Britain where coverage is very broad. This solution, it was believed, would hardly be acceptable to the seasonal workers concerned and would not resolve the difficulties and grievances in connection with benefit eligibility in the off season. A particular hardship would be imposed on those seasonal workers who had little or no unemployment during the season, but at the same time had little chance of obtaining employment during the off season; they would gain nothing by being exempted from the payment of contributions during the off season since they would presumably be unemployed and consequently free from contribution payments during that time.

A third proposal, similar to the German regulations of 1928, was to pay benefits for a limited period during the off season to seasonal workers who did not fulfill the special benefit eligibility conditions, particularly if they had drawn no benefits during the season. Such a policy would probably have removed many objections to the regulations in force because it would have made possible the payment of benefits to all seasonal workers, many of whom may have felt that they were forced to pay contributions during the season without being able to draw benefits during the off season when their need was most urgent. The administrative difficulties of this plan would have been considerable, but the principal objection raised by the Statutory Committee was that such a policy would still involve the payment of large sums during the off season to workers who neither "Note in this respect, on the other hand, the detailed schedules of seasonal industries and occupations established in Germany. (See appendix B, charts II, III, IV, and V.) 

For details of the Belgian regulations for seasonal workers, see 32: p. 129.

SEASONAL WORKERS 

expected nor seriously desired work outside the season. It would constitute a subsidy, not an insurance benefit, especially since seasonal workers drew as much in benefits during the season as did nonseasonal workers. The Statutory Committee expressed these implications of the problems as follows: "The admission that individual members of the class who have exceptionally regular employment, and draw less than their share, have a grievance calling for remedy, cuts at the root of the pooling principle involved in insurance" (26). 

While the Statutory Committee was of the opinion that the underlying principles of the seasonal workers' regulations were sound and should be retained, they proposed two groups of amendments designed to remedy certain anomalies arising under them, one to exempt from the regulations certain classes of persons with relatively steady employment records, the other to make it easier for seasonal workers to qualify for benefits during the off season.

The first group included two amendments: (1) A provision that the special regulations should not apply to persons who could prove that in any period of 4 out of 5 consecutive insurance years in the last 10 complete insurance years, they had paid 150 weekly unemployment insurance contributions (about 38 weeks a year for 4 years). 

Thus a person who had had 150 weeks of covered employment (note however that a weekly contribution does not necessarily mean a full week of employment) in the 208 weeks was to be exempted from the seasonal worker's disqualification. This provision was expected to have two effects: 

First, it would increase considerably the period of time during which a person could, upon losing his regular employment, engage in temporary seasonal work without becoming subject to stricter benefit eligibility conditions. It will be remembered that the Umpire had, as a rule, presumed that if a person had been employed in seasonal work for 3 or more years, his seasonal employment was his "normal employment." Under the proposed amendment, the employment record for the preceding 10 years could be examined and, if the provisions of the amendment had been fulfilled, the claimant's normal employment could be considered nonseasonal. 

Thus even 6 years of employment in an occupation of a seasonal nature might be discounted. The second effect of this amendment was to exempt regular workers in long-season industries (for instance maltsters, whose season lasts as long as 7 months or up to 30 weeks a year) from the seasonal workers' disqualification if they obtained 7 or 8 weeks of covered employment during the off season. Furthermore, in certain cases thig amendment would provide exemption for persons working regularly for 2 or more shorter seasons which, if added, would amount to employment for 

8 or 9 months a year.8 85 The proposed substitution of "employment" for "covered employment" with regard t off-season work made this plan even more liberal. (See par. (1), p. 51.)

GREAT BRITAIN 

51 (2) Workers whose normal seasonal employment exceeds 39 weeks a year in the aggregate, regardless of where, within Great Britain, the work was performed, should, although considered seasonal workers, not be subject to the special regulations for seasonal workers. This proposal was in line with the Umpire's decisions on the definition of "substantial amount” as equal to 25 percent, and superseded the ruling that off-season employment in various districts could not be combined in determining the "substantial” volume of off-season employment. This amendment placed workers following seasonal work from district to district in the same position as those whose seasonal employment was in one district only. 

The second group of four amendments liberalized benefit eligibility during the off season for seasonal workers: (1) The requirements of a "substantial amount" of covered employment during the off season in the 2 preceding years (or during the off season in 1 of the 2 preceding years and during the current off season) and of the reasonable expectation of substantial covered employment during the off season in the district in which the claimant resided were to be changed by substituting "employment" for "covered employment," thereby including noncovered employment as well. Although it is difficult to ascertain the practical effects of these provisions, the principle involved is of such importance that this amendment is to be considered of great significance. (2) If the aggregate of the employment obtained in the off seasons of the 2 previous insurance years, or in the off season of 1 of these 2 years and that of the current insurance year was a substantial amount (more than 25 percent), the claimant would be entitled to benefit when he had fulfilled all other requirements. (3) All off seasons falling within an insurance year were to be regarded as a single off season. This was the logical extension of the foregoing proposal. (4) Periods of sickness, if properly certified, were not to be taken into account in the determination of "substantial employment" during the off season. This provision was designed to supplant the Umpire's decision that a claimant who had been unable to obtain substantial employment during the off season on account of sickness had not fulfilled the special eligibility requirement for that off season. This action seemed logical since periods of sickness, if certified properly, were not taken into account in calculating the general qualifying period of 30 weeks in the 2 preceding years; thus the period of the 2 preceding years was extended by periods of sickness (26). 

The Unemployment Insurance Statutory Committee, although its study was primarily concerned with unemployment insurance, pointed out the following with regard to unemployment assistance: Previously, a seasonal worker who was ineligible for regular unemployment insurance benefits during the off season because he did not fulfill the special eligibility conditions was automatically disqualified from receiving unemployment assistance during the off season. This ruling had im-

SEASONAL WORKERS 

posed hardships not foreseen when the Unemployment Act of 1934 was passed. Under part II of that act, an unemployed person who for any reason did not fulfill the conditions for benefits (including those for seasonal workers) was referred to the poor-law authorities. 

Thus a seasonal worker who lost his employment during the season and exhausted his right to insurance benefit even 1 day before the end of the season and continued unemployed during all or part of the off season, was entitled to unemployment assistance throughout the off season. But the worker who had not exhausted his right to insurance benefits at the end of the season, became (unless he had fulfilled the special seasonal workers' eligibility conditions) ineligible both for insurance benefits and for unemployment assistance as soon as the off season opened. Similarly, a seasonal worker who lost his employment 

1 day after the off season had begun, instead of a day before, was ineligible for unemployment assistance (26: pp. 10-11).

The Statutory Committee on July 4, 1935, sent to the Minister of Labour a draft order, to be transmitted to Parliament for approval (26: app. B, p. 12). Issued August 2, 1935, this draft order became operative on August 29 of the same year, thus applying to the offseason periods which normally began in the fall of the year (14). 

Both the draft and the order recognized the Umpire's general interpretation of "substantial" with regard to the amount of off-season employment (now either covered or noncovered) required for benefit eligibility by requiring that such off-season employment amount, in the aggregate, "to at least as much as one-quarter of the whole of the combined extent of the off seasons" in question. A minor provision, likewise included in both the draft and the order, stipulated that the calendar year should be substituted for the insurance year in the determination of off-season employment in all cases where the beginning of the insurance year (July) would fall during the off season. 

The Seasonal Workers' Order of 1935 presented a noteworthy lib eralization of benefit eligibility requirements and a restriction of the scope of the special provisions. The effect was to increase the benefits since additional seasonal workers were able to fulfill the eligibility requirements. In its report on the financial status of the unemployment fund for the calendar year 1935, the Unemployment Insurance Statutory Committee, while not giving an exact estimate of the increase in benefit payments which resulted from the relaxation of the seasonal workers' special provisions, concluded that the additional cost seemed likely to exceed their original estimate of £50,000 a year.

GREAT BRITAIN 

53 When this figure is compared with about £44,000,000 expended in benefit payments for the year 1935, the proportion is seen to be inconsiderable. This proportion is, of course, subject to variations, and may increase as total benefit expenditures are reduced (cf. 27; p. 5).

Thus far (December 1938) there have been no changes in the 1935 legislation. In addition to the 1935 order, the voluntary exemption for seasonal workers employed for less than 18 weeks a year is still on the statute books, but, as shown in table 2, its effect is of decreasing importance.

When the agricultural system was established in 1936 (10), it was necessary to amend the Seasonal Workers' Order of 1935 (16: p. 1) 

to make it inapplicable to agricultural workers. Persons who are insured under both the general and agricultural systems are subject to the seasonal workers' order as far as general (nonagricultural) benefits are concerned.38

The 1935 regulations have been effective in clearing points of controversy in the interpretation of certain provisions in previous regulations. It is seen that the number of benefit claims of seasonal workers disallowed by the courts of referees dropped from 13,391 in 1935 to 10,583 in 1936 (see table 4), which would seem to indicate either that the liberalization of the statute made it easier for certain seasonal workers to qualify for benefits during the off season or to prove employment of a duration sufficient to exempt them from the application of the special conditions; or that, with increasing frequency, wholly seasonal workers refrained from applying for benefits during the off season because they knew that their applications would be denied.

The method of counting legal holidays in calculating the extent of the off season and of one-fourth of the off season (the period of employment required for eligibility) under the new regulations was established by the Umpire in a decision of April 3, 1936. A "maker-up" of Christmas cards had engaged in this seasonal occupation for many years and, since no other employment could be proved, she was clearly a seasonal worker. The off season in her case was from December 24 to May 31 of the following year and it had to be proved that she had been employed during the off season in each of the 2 insurance years preceding the beginning of the off season current at the date of her claim for benefits and that such employment amounted in the aggregate to at least one-fourth of the * For details see 17.

SEASONAL WORKERS 

combined extent of the off seasons of the 2 insurance years. In the district in which the claimant resided and was employed, the recognized holidays in the Christmas-card trade were 2 days at Christmas and 3 days at Easter, which were always within the off season, and there was another recognized holiday of 3 days at Whitsuntide which might or might not fall within the off season. The claimant had worked 66 days altogether, 40 days in one of the two off seasons and 26 days in the other. The aggregate duration of both off seasons was 273 days (136 and 137, respectively) when the holidays were included but only 260 days (128 and 132, respectively) when the holidays were not taken into account. One-fourth of the aggregate extent of the combined off seasons would have been 68.25 days in the first case and 65 days in the second. Since the Umpire ruled that holidays should not be taken into account, the claimant was declared entitled to benefits because she had had employment during the off season for 66 days, 1 day more than one-fourth of the total length of the combined off season.87

With reference to the clause exempting persons with long employment records (150 contributions in any 4 out of 5 consecutive insurance years in the last 10) from the special seasonal workers' provisions, the Umpire ruled on January 1, 1936, that the number of contributions paid or, in other words, the amount of covered employment, did not determine whether or not a person was a seasonal worker. Although a claimant who had a long employment record was classed as a seasonal worker if he was engaged normally in a seasonal employment, the disqualification during the off season did not apply to him. In the same decision, the Umpire expressed the opinion that noncovered employment might be taken into account in determining whether seasonal work was a person's normal employment. In that particular case the claimant had had 2 years' covered employment and 3 years of work that was noninsurable because she was too young to fall within the scope of compulsory unemployment insurance. The Umpire took into account these 3 years of noncovered employment and decided that the claimant's normal employment was of a seasonal nature. On the other hand, the Umpire decided that the "normality" of the claimant's seasonal employment had been broken by 7 months of nonseasonal employment and by 2 short *See appendix A, chart III, Legal or local holidays in computing extent of the off season (and one-fourth thereof), Case No. 2192/36.

GREAT BRITAIN 

55 periods of casual work so that it could no longer be presumed that her normal employment was seasonal.88 Before the 1935 order came into effect, neither the Umpire nor the courts of referees had any statutory criterion for deciding whether off-season employment was of a substantial extent. Since then, however, the Umpire has ruled, on June 30, 1937, that the allowance of benefits during the off season to seasonal workers "depends upon the result of a mathematical calculation. You first ascertain the number of days comprised in each of the three combinations of two offseasons. Next you ascertain the number of days on which the claimant has been employed in each of the said combinations. If the number of days of employment in any one of those combinations equals or exceeds one-quarter of the total number of days in the same combination, the seasonal worker is entitled to benefit during the off-season, but not otherwise." " Although this process introduces a somewhat automatic method of determination, it does not deviate from the basic principle of decentralization and individual application of the test of eligibility. 

GENERAL STATUTORY PROVISIONS IN FORCE IN 1938 

Great Britain, the only country which provides unemployment insurance for agricultural workers, has two systems of unemployment insurance, one for agriculture and the other for all other industries. 

Although both are administered by the same Government agency, the Ministry of Labour, their funds are kept separate within the unemployment fund in "general" and "agricultural" accounts.

Coverage Thus, the British unemployment insurance system is probably more inclusive than any other, a circumstance which is of great importance because it affords the most nearly complete pooling of the various occupational risks, including seasonal fluctuations. Since the establishment of the system in 1911, coverage of industries and occupations *See appendix A, chart III, "Normal employment" determined by taking into account employment noninsurable because of youth of claimant. Normality of such seasonal employment held to be broken by 7 months of continuous nonseasonal employment, Case 

No. 60/36. *See appendix A, chart III, Calculation of off-season employment and duration of the off season, Case No. 3087/37.

SEASONAL WORKERS 

has been extended, excluding, however, to a steadily growing degree, employment which is not the principal means of livelihood of a wage or salary earner. The definition of "insurable" (covered) employment, however, has remained practically unchanged since 1920, but the exclusions have been reduced in the direction of covering any gainful employment which constitutes a worker's chief means of livelihood.

At present nearly all employment in Great Britain under any contract of service or apprenticeship is covered,40 including that of seamen on British vessels, agricultural workers, and domestic servants in certain categories. The most important exceptions from the standpoint of seasonality are: (1) Domestic service in private homes and in certain educational establishments. (2) Casual work not in the employer's trade or business. (3) Employment with relatively assured terms of office, which excludes a relatively stable group from the insurance system. These persons are excluded either categorically or through exception granted by the Minister of Labour. (4) Employment excepted by the Minister as employment which is ordinarily adopted as subsidiary employment only and not as the principal means of livelihood. The subsidiary employments excepted are specified classes of part-time employments in the following groups: Employment in connection with religious worship, under the Crown, under local authorities, under the Lighthouse Authority, in theaters, and miscellaneous (11: pp. 

1-6). These categories are, in many cases, seasonal. Employment "in any week only to a specified extent which in the opinion of the Minister is inconsiderable" is treated as excepted employment (8: sec. 3, subsec. 3 (a)). (5) Under the agricultural system certain "subsidiary" employment (a great proportion of which is seasonal) is excluded: for example, employment in gathering flowers; harvesting fruit, peas, or potatoes; training, picking, or drying hops; or in peeling onions.

No estimate can be made of the number of workers affected by these exclusions, but it is probable that these provisions exclude a considerable amount of seasonal employment.

Benefit Eligibility 

Definition of Unemployment.-The British statutes do not attempt to define "unemployment" or an "unemployed person." Eligibility for benefits is established when a person has fulfilled the four "statutory conditions for receipt of benefit," the first of which is discussed 

40 Based upon coverage provisions of 8.

E GREAT BRITAIN 

57 below under "qualifying period." Together with the disqualification provisions (similar to those in most American State laws), the second and third "statutory conditions" explicitly prescribe when a person shall be considered unemployed. The second statutory condition requires that the claimant make his application in accordance with the regulations in force and that he be continuously unemployed. The third condition is that he be able to work and available for work."1 

Definition of Suitable Work.-Nor does the British statute define "suitable work." It does, however, establish certain conditions under which work is "unsuitable" and may be refused without impairing benefit rights. This privilege is particularly important with regard to the off-season employments of seasonal workers. Employment is not suitable if the vacancy is in any way the result of an industrial dispute. Employment in the claimant's usual occupation in the district where he was last ordinarily employed, at lower wages or under less favorable conditions than he may reasonably expect to obtain considering the employment conditions and remuneration which he habitually obtained in that district in his usual occupation, is not suitable. The same applies to employment in the claimant's usual = occupation in another district if wages are lower or conditions of employment less favorable than those generally established by collective contracts or observed by "good" employers in that district. 

Employment in an occupation other than the claimant's own is considered suitable after the lapse of a "reasonable" period of time unless the wages and employment conditions fall below accepted standards and are less favorable than those established by collective bargaining or those generally observed by "good" employers. To be considered suitable, however, employment is not necessarily covered employment. 

Qualifying Period. The regular qualifying period is 30 weeks of covered employment in the 2 years immediately preceding the date on which the claim for benefits is first made.42 If this requirement has been fulfilled ("the first statutory condition") and the unemployed worker has exhausted his benefit rights in the preceding "benefit year" (this term has essentially the same meaning in both British and American statutory provisions for unemployment insurance), he must requalify for benefits in the subsequent benefit year by the payment of 10 additional weekly contributions. In the agricultural unemployment insurance system, the qualifying period for a first benefit The fourth statutory condition is attendance at a course of instruction under certain conditions. "This qualification is met by the payment of a weekly contribution even if the worker had as little as 1 day's employment within each of 30 weeks. 159727-40- -5

SEASONAL WORKERS 

www. application is fulfilled after 20 contributions have been paid in the 2 preceding years, and 10 weeks are required for later claims, as in the general system. Comparison with German or Austrian requirements shows that the general qualifying period requirement in Great Britain is considerably more liberal and disqualifies few seasonal. workers. On the basis of the qualifying period only, a seasonal worker who has had only seasonal employment for 15 weeks the first year and a like amount in the second year would qualify for benefits, and thereafter would qualify after having had 10 weeks of employ. ment. In the agricultural system, the qualifying-period requirement is still shorter and, normally, employment of 10 weeks a year qualifies an agricultural "contributor" (insured person) for benefits. It should be noted, however, that the ratio provisions (see below under "benefit duration") as well as limitations of the weekly benefit amount and the maximum benefit period are reliable safeguards against too heavy a drain on the fund.

Waiting Period. The waiting period (3 days in the general and 

1 week in the agricultural system 43) is the shortest in any unemployment insurance system with the possible exception of the Belgian. 

It is more liberal than the German (3 to 14 days), the Austrian (7 days), or the Polish (10 days), and considerably less restrictive than that required in the various State laws in the United States, which may be as high as 8 weeks of partial or 4 weeks of total unemployment. The British waiting period affects, of course, both seasonal and nonseasonal workers.

Means Test.-The British system does not require a means test for benefits under either the general or the agricultural systems. Only in unemployment assistance is a test of need applied. 

Benefit Amount and Duration 

Computation.-The weekly benefit amount is expressed in flat rates in both the general and agricultural systems. It varies with sex and age classes and is lower in the agricultural system. Weekly supplements are paid, also at flat rates, for the spouse and dependent children of the insured. In the agricultural system, the weekly benefit amount (including supplements for dependents) is limited to 30s. a week, but the general system sets no limit to the number of dependents for whom supplementary benefits may be paid. Duration. The normal maximum duration of benefits in the general unemployment insurance system is 156 days in a benefit year.

A person who has paid 30 weekly contributions in the 2 preceding years is thus entitled to benefits for 26 weeks in the case of first application and for the same period after he has paid 10 additional weekly contributions in the case of subsequent claim for benefits. 

Additional days of benefits are computed on the basis of 3 days for every 5 weekly contributions paid by the claimant in the last 5 years, less 1 day for every 10 days for which he had received benefits during the preceding 5 benefit years. Thus, if a claimant has been engaged in covered employment in the last 5 years and has never been unemployed during that period, he is entitled to an additional 156 days, so that in such a case he may receive benefits for a full year. 

Seasonal workers can rarely qualify for the full duration of this benefit period because, first, they do not contribute all the year round and, second, because they may have drawn benefits during the off season. If, for instance, a worker has had, in the last 5 years, covered employment for 26 weeks every year and has received benefits for the maximum period during those years (not taking into account the waiting period) he would not have accumulated any additional credit. 

His additional credit on the basis of his 180 contributions (130×, or 

78 days) would be entirely canceled by his days of benefit or 78 days). If he had been employed 30 weeks a year he would have paid 150 contributions in the last 5 years, earning 90 days as addi- 156×5 (15 10 tional credit (150×3). He would have drawn benefits for 66 days (52-30)×6×5 10 so that he would be entitled to 24 (90–66) additional

SEASONAL WORKERS 

benefit days. With 39 weeks' employment, the additional days would amount to 78; with full employment throughout the 5 years, to 156 days. Seasonal workers who draw benefits year after year find themselves at a disadvantage with regard to additional benefit days. 

In the agricultural system, the benefit period is determined by ratio provisions, i. e., 12 days of benefits for the first 10 weekly agricultural contributions and 3 more days for every additional weekly contribution, with a maximum of 300 days in any benefit year. A person who has had 39 weeks of agricultural employment will be entitled to 162 weeks of benefits. For the computation of the benefit period on the basis of credits at the beginning of a benefit year, benefits are deemed to have been paid at five-sixths of a contribution for each of the first 12 days' benefits paid to him in any benefit year and at the rate of one-third for each additional day of benefits in that benefit year.

Repayment of Contributions Under the Agricultural Program 

The British agricultural unemployment insurance system provides for a partial exemption from payment of contributions similar in principle to the guaranteed employment plans found in some State laws in the United States. If a contract is made for the employment of a person in agriculture for not less than 50 consecutive weeks (yearly hiring), or for not less than 21 weeks (half-yearly hiring), 25 and 12.5 percent, respectively, of the contributions paid for persons so hired are repaid to the contributors for the period covered by such long-hiring contracts. The relatively high proportion repaid on the flat contributions, combined with the comparatively low cash wages in agriculture, may, theoretically, have an excellent stabilizing influence. During the period between May 4 and December 31, 1936, however, 43,063 long-hiring contracts were made, about two-thirds of them yearly and one-third half-yearly hiring, and the total amount refunded was only £170 (28: p. 53). Coverage under the agricultural system was estimated at that time to be 600,000 workers. 

CONCLUSION 

Seasonal unemployment is not an urgent problem in British unemployment insurance. The temperate climate of the British Isles reduces the number of markedly seasonal industries, and the nearly all-inclusive coverage of the unemployment insurance system in an industrially diversified country allows almost complete pooling of

GREAT BRITAIN 

61 the seasonal risk with the general occupational risk. There is no indication that seasonal unemployment would ever result in too heavy a burden upon the unemployment fund, certainly not in times of relatively prosperous business conditions unless benefits were unlimited in duration. It was the general consideration of the principles and repute of the unemployment insurance system which gradually brought about special statutory restrictions of the benefit rights of wholly seasonal workers during off-season unemployment. 

The seasonal problem in Great Britain is approached by distinguishing two types of seasonal unemployment, according to which occupations may be classified as "partly seasonal" and "wholly seasonal." The first type includes occupations in industries where employment fluctuates regularly in the course of the year but where there is always some employment available even when operations are greatly reduced. The building and clothing trades are foremost in this category in Great Britain. The second group, "wholly seasonal," includes all those occupations in which, during certain regularly recurring periods of the year, no employment is available because operations cease completely. Wholly seasonal employment consists of two categories of occupations, those in holiday and health resorts where employment is available only during the holiday seasons, and those in industries which operate only during certain more or less definitely established periods of the year, determined by weather, climate, custom, or law.

Unemployment during certain periods of the year is a probability for members of the clothing and building industries, but it is a certainty for persons employed in wholly seasonal occupations, such as fishermen, beach attendants, beet-sugar workers, and professional soccer players. The British attitude is that the principle of the pooled risk allows and calls for payment of benefits during the slack period to the first group, but holds that insurance against a certainty is not insurance, but a subsidy.

The original British unemployment insurance system, established in 1911, was avowedly experimental in character and was an effort to provide unemployment insurance for workers who, since they were not organized in trade-unions, lacked the advantage of the unemployment benefits paid by many workers' associations. Since its actuarial basis was admittedly uncertain and its coverage limited to certain trades, it was to be safeguarded by the provision that benefits be paid in the ratio of 1 week for every 5 (later 6) weekly contribu-

SEASONAL WORKERS 

tions. Together with a maximum duration of 15 (later 26) weeks of benefits a year and certain qualifying provisions, the system took into account not only the seasonal but also the personal unemployment risk, obviating the necessity for special provisions for either partly seasonal or wholly seasonal industries. In fact, most of the trades originally covered were subject to considerable seasonal fluctuation. In spite of the limited coverage, the low contribution rates, and the seasonal character of many of the trades covered, the unemployment fund had accumulated a considerable surplus by 1920. 

The general eligibility conditions, that a claimant must be capable of work and available for work and (since 1924) genuinely seeking work, were applicable to wholly seasonal workers as well as to nonseasonal workers. The latter requirement in particular disqualified wholly seasonal workers from receiving benefits during the off season unless they could prove that they were actively seeking off-season employment. In the interpretation of the statutes, special attention is given to the question whether a person's seasonal employment is his normal employment or whether economic conditions rather than his own volition make him look for and follow a wholly seasonal occupation. Very decidedly, the worker and his industrial status are placed in the center of the problem, not the industry or establishment in which he happens to be employed-one of the characteristics of the British method of dealing with seasonality in unemployment insurance. If the seasonal worker can prove that he works during the off season in another occupation, he is, in principle, not disqualified for benefits, no matter how seasonal his normal employment may be.

Seasonal workers who are subject to special treatment when unemployed during the off season are defined as persons whose normal employment is in wholly seasonal occupations. Wholly seasonal occupations are those in which, at certain regularly recurring periods of the year, no substantial amount of employment is available in the district in which the unemployed seasonal worker resides. When in 1927 the ratio provisions were abolished and benefits were virtually unlimited in duration, the "genuinely seeking work" provision had to be enforced more strictly, and certain wholly seasonal workers who did not work for more than 18 weeks in the year were permitted to elect exemption from coverage. This voluntary exemption is still in operation, but is claimed by relatively few workers because the risk of unemployment even during the season makes it advantageous

GREAT BRITAIN 

63 to be insured, as special investigations have proved. The wholly seasonal workers who have not claimed exemption are subject to special conditions when claiming benefits during the off season. They are not eligible for benefits unless they prove (1) employment during one-fourth of the duration of two off seasons and (2) a reasonable expectation of obtaining off-season employment for at least onefourth of the current off season. What the off season is, how long it lasts, and similar necessary data are ascertained and determined, not by a uniform centralized procedure, but by the local authorities in the district in question, normally by the local courts of referees of which there are about 300 in Great Britain. 

This decentralized administration of a nation-wide uniform system of unemployment insurance is most significant. Individual treatment of each seasonal worker's claim, which is essential to equity, has its corollary in local administration and determination by agencies thoroughly familiar with the local industrial structure and the characteristics and customs of the region. 

Even though determinations are arrived at locally, there is no lack of uniformity. The statutes themselves, while not strictly defining fixed dates and certain other terms, are definite enough to make clear the fundamental principles involved, and the decisions of the Umpire establish the principles for the adjudication of claims of seasonal workers. Since the local insurance officer who makes the initial determination cannot disallow the claim of a seasonal worker under the special seasonal workers' provisions, the appeal tribunalthe court of referees in which employers and employees are represented in equal numbers-passes upon the validity of the claim and in doing so carefully examines the claimant's past employment record and the industrial situation in the locality. Either the claimant or the insurance officer may appeal to the Umpire, whose decision is final. 

The approach to the seasonal problem has been greatly facilitated by the absence of experience-rating provisions in the unemployment insurance law. Efforts to induce employers to stabilize employment by repayment of one-third of the employer's contributions when he had paid 45 weekly contributions for the worker in 1 year were soon abandoned. Since the employer is, therefore, not interested in a saving on his own account, benefits may be paid to seasonal workers during the off season as the general economic and social feasibility of such procedure demands, without reference to the individual em-

SEASONAL WORKERS 

ployer's account. In administration, no question arises and no extra work is involved in charging benefits paid during the off season. 

A particularly desirable feature of the British plan, the payment of additional days of benefit to workers with steady employment records, has its effect on seasonal workers also. Since such workers are very frequently unemployed during the off season and while unemployed pay no contributions, their additional credit will normally be lower, so that they are entitled to a smaller number of additional days of benefit than are steady workers. 

The problem of payment of benefits to seasonal workers during the off season will always resolve itself into two main questions: one of equity and social purpose, the other of actuarial soundness and financial stability of the fund. Any effort to solve the seasonal problem in unemployment insurance will have to be directed toward a compromise between the just needs of the individual and the collective guarantee of the funds accumulated for the various types of unemployment. The British approach seems worthy of close study because it has proved to be remarkably adaptable to changing conditions and has, at the same time, retained the fundamental principles of insurance.
