PART III Seasonal Workers and Unemployment Insurance in Germany INTRODUCTION 

Two basic purposes governed the disposition of funds and the administrative organization of the original German unemployment insurance system. These were the adequate care of the family, an outgrowth of the pronounced social basis of the system, and the control of the labor market. Social aims were furthered by supplementary benefits granted to families, special consideration shown in a reduced waiting period for persons with dependents, payment of proportionally higher benefit amounts to low-paid workers, emergency benefits granted on the basis of a needs test, and in many other major and minor features. The stabilization of the labor market was the purpose underlying the close relationship which was maintained by the joint administration of the employment service, unemployment insurance, vocational education, and the so-called productive unemployment relief and similar work projects with the same objective, which were financed by unemployment insurance funds. Both these ends were best served by centralizing unemployment insurance administration. The present system is relief-like in character and its surplus funds are used for projects through which the Government may control the labor market and thus carry out the program of the Four-Year Plan for increasing German selfsufficiency.

Without taking into account the early structure of unemployment relief, with its compulsory employers' and workers' contributions in effect before 1927, three periods of development may be distinguished: (1) October 1927 to June 1931, a time of increasing cyclical unemployment, during which the insurance character of the system was maintained although benefits were gradually restricted and contributions greatly increased. (2) June 1931 to late in 1934, during which the change from insurance to relief, accelerated by the economic crisis, was accomplished by emergency measures in the form of Presidential decrees. (3) The period since 1934, during which the relief character of the program became more and more prominent as a result of efforts to utilize 67

SEASONAL WORKERS 

the unemployment insurance and relief system as a means of managing and manipulating the entire German labor market in accordance with the social, economic, and political philosophy of the new regime. 

The approach to the problem of seasonal workers under the unemployment insurance system varied with each period. In the first period efforts were mainly directed toward balancing risk and benefits, and the special hazard of seasonal unemployment was recognized through the requirement of longer waiting periods, shorter benefit duration, and the reduction of the benefit rate for seasonal workers. In the second period, seasonal unemployment hazards were considered of slight significance since the main emphasis of the entire system was placed on emergency benefits granted on the basis of need. The objectives of German unemployment insurance in the third or present period is best illustrated by the following quotation from a review of the first 10 years of unemployment insurance in Germany: "Unemployment insurance-relief will remain one of the chief tasks of the authority (i. e., the Federal Institute for Employment Service and Unemployment Insurance) which is charged with directing and managing the national labor market. Its form and scope will be determined solely by the goal which the Government sets in its policy for the labor market. Its administration must be centralized and uniform, yet adjusted to the employment possibilities available to unemployed workers who, as benefit recipients, remain members of the work community of the people. The payment of unemployment benefits is, therefore, to be considered a temporary measure; the ultimate goal is to translate unutilized working capacity into productive activity which contributes to the common good" (41: p. 56).¹

THE BACKGROUND OF THE LAW OF 1927 

Seasonal unemployment (berufsübliche Arbeitslosigkeit, or "unemployment customary in the trade") was an important consideration in drafting the first Government bills for unemployment insurance in the years 1921-23. It was frequently proposed that risk classes be established for various occupations, but these proposals were never adopted. While the unemployment insurance law of 1927 was being drafted, one of the primary issues was the problem of pooling the occupational risk.

The question of occupational risk was discussed in the official ex- 

1 For literature cited, see pp. 104-107.

I GERMANY 

69 planation of the draft of the bill (32: pp. 94–95) in connection with a section which provided that the benefit period might be shortened for "members of occupations or industries in which regularly recurring unemployment is customary" (32: sec. 54, subsec. 3) and that benefit duration could be modified in proportion to the number of weeks of covered employment (32: sec. 55, p. 13), instead of being fixed at the 26 weeks in 12 months proposed for occupations in which employment was more stable. It was clear, of course, that certain occupations might prove a heavy drain on the unemployment fund; nevertheless, the framers of the law rejected the principle of the Ghent system in which the various occupational groups carry their own risk.2 Modern production methods utilize many different occupational groups within an industry with the result that the unemployment of members of individual crafts depends solely upon the industry as a whole. The volume of unemployment in one occupational group, the official explanation continued, bore no fixed relationship to that in other industries; occupations which, in the recent past, had been considered very stable had become unstable in 1926. It was considered justifiable, therefore, that industrial groups with relatively inconsiderable unemployment share the burden with those in which the hazard was great. Thus it was recommended that contribution rates be made uniform for all occupations, regardless of unemployment risk, but that benefits be varied with such risk, either by shorter duration for workers in less stable occupations or by adjustment of duration in ratio to length of previous employment. The ratio method would have made it possible to take into account both occupational and individual risks. It was also recommended that during the off season an unemployed seasonal worker would have to accept employment offered him by the local office, even in an occupation other than his own (32: sec. 47, subsec. 3).

Because, in the opinion of those who formulated the proposed bill, seasonal unemployment formed only a small proportion of the total volume of unemployment, these special provisions were considered sufficient to cope with the seasonal problem. In addition, however, it was proposed to extend the waiting period for seasonal workers to a maximum of 3 weeks while the regular waiting period in the pro- 'In this connection it may be noted that the modified Ghent system in Denmark provides Government subsidies to the various (occupational) unemployment insurance societies, not on the basis of a proportion of the benefits paid by them, but as a proportion of the contributions paid by their own members, which proportion increases with the decrease of the annual earnings in the respective occupations. 'Such as mining in Westphalia and in the Rhine District.

SEASONAL WORKERS SPECIAL REGULATIONS FOR SEASONAL WORKERS, 1927-31 

The employment service and unemployment insurance law of July 16, 1927, which became effective on October 1, 1927, replaced the former unemployment relief system and incorporated some of the provisions proposed in the bill with special reference to members of occupations or industries in which regularly recurring unemployment was normal (1).

Extended Waiting Period, 1927-28 The regular benefit period provided in the new act for nonseasonal workers was 26 weeks and might be extended to as long as 39 weeks.* 

The governing body of the Federal Institute for Employment Service and Unemployment Insurance was authorized to reduce the benefit period for seasonal workers (1: sec. 99, subsec. 3) and to lengthen the regular waiting period which at that time was 7 days and might be waived under certain conditions (1: sec. 110, subsecs. 

2 and 3). An unemployed seasonal worker could refuse to accept work during the off season outside his regular occupation only when acceptance would jeopardize his future career (1: sec. 90, subsec. 3).

In computing the benefit amount, however, the general provisions applied. The weekly benefit amount was fixed at a certain percentage of the average weekly earnings during the 3 months of employment prior to the registration of unemployment.

The governing body of the Federal Institute did not immediately avail itself of its authority to reduce the benefit duration for members of seasonal occupations or industries; an extension of the waiting period was considered sufficient to prevent the depletion of the fund. 

The administrative order of December 2, 1927, effective December 12, required a 2-week waiting period for persons who had been employed for at least 6 of the 12 preceding months in an establishment which every year reduced or temporarily suspended operations in consequence of weather conditions (7). If employment in such an establishment had lasted 8 months, the waiting period was to be extended to 3 weeks. Workers who had employment in another occupation during the off season received preferential treatment in that the committees of management of the district labor offices were The qualifying period at that time was 26 weeks of covered employment during the 

12 months immediately preceding registration of unemployment. 'On which employers, employees, and public bodies were represented in equal numbers.

E GERMANY 

73 authorized to reduce the waiting period to the normal 7 days for workers who, on account of the unfavorable condition of the labor market, could not obtain their customary off-season employment. 

Categories of establishments in which the workers' employment was considered subject to weather conditions were not specified in the order but were to be set up by the chairmen of the local employment offices in accordance with local economic and climatic conditions. The so-called outdoor occupations, such as agriculture, forestry, horticulture (except when excluded from insurance coverage)," building, and brickmaking were those generally affected. 

The inevitable variations in the classifications of seasonal occupation by the various local employment offices gave rise to dissatisfaction among many of the workers, although the district labor offices used their discretionary power to reduce the special waiting period for seasonal workers in all possible cases. Thus the intent of the statute was, to some extent, defeated. The order had been issued shortly after the creation of the governing body of the Federal Institute and had necessarily been put into operation hastily. Even if the extended waiting period had been enforced properly, it alone would not have been sufficient to prevent large expenditures for seasonal unemployment (46: p. 114). Consequently, during the winter of 1927-28, a disproportionately large number of seasonally unemployed workers received benefits with the result that the resources of the newly established unemployment insurance system were taxed to the utmost.

The number of persons receiving the principal benefit, many of whom were also entitled to a family supplement, increased from 

329,734 on October 15, 1927, to 1,371,103 on January 15, 1928 (46: p. 133). Consequently, although contributions had regularly exceeded disbursements during the summer months of 1927, benefit payments during the winter months not only kept pace with current contributions (about 70 million Reichsmarks a month), but produced • Employers, employees, and public bodies were represented in equal numbers on the committees of management. 'Farm servants, farm tenants, and persons engaged in fishing in inland and coastal waters were notable exclusions from coverage. 

The number of recipients was 642,180 on Mar. 5, 1928. The ordinary insurance benefits (Arbeitslosenunterstützung) as well as the emergency or extended benefits (Krisenunterstützung) consist of two parts: (1) the so-called principal benefit (Hauptunterstützung), to which unemployed workers are entitled as insured individuals, and (2) a family supplement (Familienunterstützung), which workers receive on behalf of dependents, such as spouse and minor children. The family supplement is payable only to workers in receipt of principal benefits. 159727-40- 6

SEASONAL WORKERS 

a deficit of about 152 million Reichsmarks by the end of March, an amount nearly 52 million Reichsmarks in excess of the reserve which had been available on October 1, 1927.

Under the general provisions in force, the ratio of the contributions of a seasonal worker to the benefits paid to him was as follows: His contribution, at the rate of 3 percent of his wages, amounted in the 26 weeks of his qualifying period to 78 percent of 1 week's wage and in 39 weeks to 117 percent. He was entitled, however, if the off season lasted 3 months during the winter, to benefits for 12 weeks at a rate as high as 80 percent of his former wage rate. His total benefits would thus amount to 8 or 9 times his total previous contributions. 

Even if he had paid contributions for 39 weeks, his total benefit amount would be 5 or 6 times his total contributions. 

Reduced Benefit Period, 1928-29 

The experience of the winter 1927-28 convinced the authorities that the decentralized administration of the extended waiting period for seasonal workers should be abolished. The alternative provided in the unemployment insurance law was therefore put into effect, and the benefit period for seasonal workers during the off season was reduced.

Seasonal Workers' Order of December 1928.-The governing body of the Federal Institute, in an order of December 18, 1928 (retroactive to December 2, 1928), repealed the special regulations increasing the waiting period for seasonal workers and issued the following new provisions (13): The committees of management of the district labor offices were authorized to determine for parts or for the whole of their territory the occupations or industries in which regularly recurring unemployment was to be considered normal and likewise to fix the duration, beginning, and end of the off-season period. The duration of the off season could not, however, exceed 4 months. The governing body of the Federal Institute could, at its discretion, classify certain occupations or industries as seasonal and establish the limits of the off season for the whole or parts of the territory of the German Reich. The classification was binding upon the district labor offices; but the dates of beginning and end of the off season were not.⁹

A worker in an occupation and industry classified as seasonal was For the dates of the off-season period in the territory of the various district labor offices, see table 5.

GERMANY 

75 eligible to receive ordinary unemployment insurance benefits for a maximum of 6 weeks during the off season, although, during the entire year, he, as well as the nonseasonal worker, might receive benefits for a total of 26 weeks. If the seasonal worker had not exhausted = his claim to ordinary benefits by unemployment during the season, he was still eligible for the remainder of his benefit period, beginning immediately after the end of the off season. 

The governing body of the Federal Institute, on the basis of its authority to determine the occupations and industries to be considered seasonal throughout the German Reich and likewise to fix the limits of the off season, issued detailed instructions for the administration of the order of December 18, 1928. The off season for the winter 

1928-29 was fixed at 4 months, December 1, 1928-March 31, 1929, inclusive.10 The district labor offices were permitted to change the dates according to climatic conditions, but the season thus fixed could not be longer than 4 months or less than 3.

Exhaustive codes of seasonal industries ¹¹ and of seasonal occupations 12 were published by the Federal Institute for Employment Service and Unemployment Insurance in 1928 and were made mandatory except that the district labor offices might add industries considered seasonal. The Census of Industrial Establishments (June 

16, 1925) served as a basis for the code of industries (Betriebsarten); and the list of occupations (Berufsarten)¹ used by the employment offices in reporting statistics of the labor market was utilized for the code of seasonal occupations. Persons who had been employed for more than 13 weeks out of their last 26 weeks of covered employment in an establishment included in the code of seasonal industries were considered seasonal workers if they were likewise members of an occupational group classified in the code of seasonal occupations.

Although reference is made in both the unemployment insurance law and in the order of December 18, 1928, to occupations or industries of a seasonal nature, administrative instructions defined seasonal workers as those engaged in seasonal occupations within seasonal industries. Since persons engaged in seasonal occupations might be employed in establishments which were not influenced by seasonal 10 Limits of the off season as determined for the years 1928-31 are shown on p. 79. 

11 See appendix B, chart II. "See appendix B, chart III. 

See appendix B, chart IV. 

14 See appendix B, chart V.

SEASONAL WORKERS 

fluctuations, the instructions demanded membership in a seasonal occupation and in a seasonal industry. Only when a worker engaged in a seasonal occupation had been employed in a seasonal industry for more than 13 weeks was he subject to the reduction of the benefit period during the 4 winter months, December through March, which comprised the off season.

Individual examples may make this distinction clearer: On the one hand, a mason was not considered seasonally unemployed because his last covered employment had been in a nonseasonal industry. He had followed his occupation in a factory manufacturing electrical appliances, and, when he was dismissed in January after the work on a new power plant was finished, he could not find new employment as a mason during the winter months. His occupation was seasonal but his industry was nonseasonal. On the other hand, a building locksmith was not considered seasonally unemployed because his occupation was nonseasonal. He had been employed in the construction of an apartment house which suspended operations with the beginning of frost. His industry was seasonal but his occupation was nonseasonal. An unskilled laborer, however, who had worked part of the year as a transport worker in a chemical factory and subsequently for 14 weeks digging a ditch for the construction of a sewer line was considered seasonally unemployed because both his occupation and the establishment for which he worked were classified as seasonal. 

This strict limitation of benefit rights may have been justifiable if the seasonal worker had an opportunity to obtain employment during the off season; but it is doubtful whether many workers could obtain other employment during the winter off season which coincided exactly with the period when unemployment was always at its peak. 

Ordinary and Emergency Benefits.-The benefits paid nonseasonal workers under the unemployment insurance system were of two kinds : (1) ordinary benefits payable for 26 weeks to qualified unemployed workers without test of need; and (2) emergency benefits payable at the expiration of the 26-week benefit period to workers who could prove their need. For the higher wage classes emergency benefits were lower than ordinary benefits; for the lower wage classes (I-VI, inclusive) the maximum rates of ordinary and emergency benefits were identical.

As it became apparent that off-season benefits limited to 6 weeks were often insufficient for the requirements of the unemployed, a

special seasonal workers' relief system was established to assist the most needy. On December 24, 1928, an extension of the benefit period beyond 6 weeks was provided for seasonal workers during the off season, as a relief measure, retroactive to December 2, and distinct from the unemployment insurance system. Needy seasonal workers who had exhausted their rights to benefits by drawing 6 weeks of unemployment insurance benefits during the off season were entitled to this special relief, similar to emergency benefits, during the remainder of the off season (2). The number of weeks (26 weeks for the entire year) during which they might receive ordinary unemployment insurance benefits after the expiration of the off season was reduced in the ratio of 1 week for every 2 weeks for which they had received special relief payments during the off season. 

The rates of special relief payments were somewhat lower than ordinary benefits in the upper wage classes and were the same as the emergency benefits paid to unemployed nonseasonal workers. The 

Federal Government was to bear four-fifths of the cost, just as it bore four-fifths of the cost of emergency benefits for other insured workers who had exhausted their rights to ordinary benefits under the unemployment insurance system and who were in need. A comparison (see table 3) between the numbers of seasonal workers receiving ordinary benefits and those receiving special seasonal workers' relief shows clearly that the restriction of benefits in the off season to 6 weeks achieved its purpose of curtailing the number of seasonal workers receiving ordinary benefits. The proportion of seasonal workers among the total number of recipients of ordinary benefits fell from 50.4 percent on December 31, 1928, to 14.6 percent on March 

15, 1929, but the number of persons benefiting by the special seasonal workers' relief increased steadily from zero on December 31, 1928, to approximately 900,000 in March 1929, a figure which reflects the number of unemployed seasonal workers in need who had exhausted their 

6-week benefit period.¹ 17 The appropriation of 28 million Reichsmarks voted to cover the Federal Government's share of the cost of this special relief proved insufficient, and, at the end of the winter of 1928-29, it was found that almost 105 million Reichsmarks was the share to be paid by the Government (35). After the winter season of 1929-30, the district labor offices were bound by the uniform determination of the off season made by the Federal Institute except for industries and occupations not enumerated in the codes shown on charts II and III of appendix B. 

Conference of Unemployment Insurance Experts.-In the summer of 1929, the Minister of Labor appointed a Commission of Experts to review the 2-year experience gained under the Unemployment Insurance Act of 1927 and to make suggestions for its amendment (37). 

The Commission was made up of representatives of eight political parties (those which constituted the Government majority at that time); five representatives each of public bodies, employers, and employees; and five persons qualified by their theoretical and practical knowledge of unemployment insurance.

It is significant that the first item on the Commission's agenda was the problem of seasonal workers. It had been estimated by the Government that approximately 800,000 persons would be seasonally unemployed during 3 months every winter, a number which, when averaged throughout the year, meant approximately 200,000 seasonally unem-

GERMANY 

81 ployed each month. One of the first possibilities considered to cope with the seasonal problem was that of fixing the amount of benefits according to length of previous covered employment, a plan which, it was thought, would effect a saving to the unemployment fund, particularly when applied to certain covered groups with recurrent seasonal unemployment. It was suggested that persons who had been continuously employed in covered employment for more than 52 weeks before claiming benefits should be entitled to the full benefit rate; those who had been in covered employment from 39 to 52 weeks should receive a certain proportion, for example, 75 percent of the full benefit amount; and those with less than 39 weeks of covered employment should receive still lower benefits. Thus it was expected that seasonal workers who became unemployed every year for a certain period of time would be ineligible for the full rates. On the other hand, those seasonal workers who found other covered employment during the off season could receive more generous allowances because their employment for 52 or more weeks was at least a possibility. Employment during the off season had to be covered employment; however, in 

1929 this requirement was not a severe restriction because the German unemployment insurance law covered almost all industrial and many agricultural occupations.

This plan was approved by a majority of the Commission. It was amended by a provision doubling the waiting period for seasonal workers and granting them benefits during the off season at the rates of emergency benefits for nonseasonal workers, the maximum rates of which were less in the upper wage classes than ordinary unemployment insurance benefits.

In the Commission's discussion of the seasonal problem, the point of view of the employers was expressed in the following argument: 

The risk of seasonal unemployment could not be covered on an insurance basis at all because it occurred regularly each year. There was little doubt that a large proportion of seasonal workers would become unemployed during the off season, and, as a matter of fact, there was reason to assume that every year the same seasonal workers would apply for benefits. For certain groups of seasonal workers, particularly those possessing some farm property, unemployment during the off season was a certainty because such workers did not desire yearround employment. The existing regulations, the employers continued, provided a supplement to the wages of seasonal workers, furnished by contributions from other persons and by taxpayers. As a

SEASONAL WORKERS 

consequence, seasonal workers rarely looked for jobs outside the season. 

This subsidy of seasonal workers, it was argued, tended to demoralize the recipients. An increasing number of workers, it was feared, would take advantage of the situation and would prefer seasonal employment in the city to steady agricultural work. It was obvious, the employers' representatives declared, that the number of seasonal workers was steadily increasing. Even if the seasonal risk could be insured, they thought, it would require a separate program apart from the general unemployment insurance system.

On the other hand, the workers' representatives on the Commission, who were members of the most important German labor unions, recommended keeping seasonal workers within the general unemployment insurance system. Cyclical fluctuation, they showed, was also observed in winter unemployment, according to trade-union statistics of the preceding 10 years. The difficulty of finding work in any occupation during the winter varied from year to year and was greatly influenced by cyclical trends. The theory that seasonal workers were receiving proportionately higher wages than nonseasonal workers had been refuted, the labor unions contended, by the findings of a recent study authorized by the Federal Institute for Employment Service and Unemployment Insurance (see table 6) which showed that seasonal workers who were receiving benefits were found in approximately the same wage classes as nonseasonal workers. The seasonal wages of agricultural workers and of employees in brickmaking establishments and similar enterprises could not be proved to be higher than wages paid unskilled day laborers in nonseasonal work. The exclusion of seasonal workers from unemployment insurance, it was asserted, would result in increasingly heavy burdens on public-assistance authorities. The seasonal workers who could rely on other financial resources during the off season would have to be distinguished from those who had no other sources of income. 

The amount and scope of off-season employment, a question which was brought up during the discussion, could not be ascertained because no information was available.

The Commission as a whole thought that the operation of the special regulations for seasonal workers had been generally satisfactory up to that date, in spite of certain drawbacks, such as the fact that workers preferred seasonal employment to other occupations; that administrative difficulties were encountered in redetermining the

GERMANY 

83 benefit amount and benefit eligibility for each worker who changed from ordinary benefits to special relief status; and that benefits at the beginning of seasonal unemployment were disproportionately high because of the high seasonal earnings in the 3 months on which the benefits were based. 

Groups within the Commission presented widely diverse recommendations. At one extreme stood the building-trade workers' unions which wanted seasonal unemployment to be subject to the same regulations as nonseasonal unemployment, a proposal which was not supported by any other group. The Association of the German Employers' Federations, at the other extreme, recommended that seasonal workers be considered ineligible for benefits throughout the off season.

The Association of the Agricultural and Forestry Em ployers' Federations wished to have seasonal workers excluded from coverage.

Certain other proposals recommended modification of the duration and amount of benefits. The German Nationalist Party suggested trebling the waiting period during the off season, and representatives of certain public bodies proposed a flat waiting period of 4 weeks for certain seasonal workers during the off season (the regular waiting period at that time was a week); the Democratic Party suggested 2 weeks, and the German People's Party, 3 weeks. The representatives of the majority parties of the Reichstag proposed a reduction of benefit rates to one-half of the ordinary benefits for seasonal workers in wage classes higher than V, with more liberal provisions for workers in certain large cities. The employers' representatives recommended that only one-half of the seasonal earnings be taken into account in computing the benefit amount. Another proposal was that an increase in the employers' (not employees') contributions be ordered by the committees of management of the district labor offices in those establishments which, on account of the nature of their work, were more liable to unemployment than others. 

The recommendations of the Commission of Experts were considered of fundamental importance when the amendment of the unemployment insurance law was formulated the following year. 

The numbers and wage classes of persons found by the Commission to be receiving unemployment benefits under the general unemployment insurance system and under the special seasonal workers' relief provisions on February 15, 1929, are shown in table 6.

Reduced Benefit Rates, 1929-31 

The amendment of the unemployment insurance law in the fall of 1929 (3) was based upon the findings of the Commission of Experts (37). It discontinued the special relief system for seasonal workers, with the result that the needs test no longer was applied at the expiration of 6 weeks of ordinary benefits during the off season. 

On the other hand, the amendment reduced the benefit rates throughout the off season for seasonal workers in the five highest wage classes, placing off-season benefits at the level of emergency benefits although none of the conditions for the receipt of emergency benefits was applied. When the rates of emergency benefits were reduced on October 11, 1930, the reduction did not affect the rates of the off-season benefits, which remained, until June 1931, higher than emergency benefits and lower than the ordinary insurance benefits for nonseasonal workers.18

The general provisions for computing the benefit amount were modified so that, whereas formerly the weekly benefit amount was a percentage (varying with wage classes and number of dependents) of the average weekly wages during the last 3 months of employment, 16 In wage classes I through VI the benefit rates for seasonal and nonseasonal workers were identical. The benefits of seasonal workers in wage class VII were equal to the ordinary insurance benefits of nonseasonal workers in class VI; seasonal workers in classes VIII and IX received the equivalent of benefits for nonseasonal workers in class VII; and benefits for seasonal workers in classes X and XI equalled those of nonseasonal workers in class VIII.

GERMANY 

85 it was now to be similarly computed on the basis of employment during the 26 weeks (or 6 months) prior to registration of unemployment. Thus a close relationship between the base period for computation of wages and the qualifying period (26 weeks of covered employment in the preceding 12 months) 19 was established, and at the same time earnings over a much longer period of time could be counted-a feature which was particularly important to seasonal workers. This amendment retained the former provision that shorttime work and short-time earnings caused by shortage of work were to be taken into account at a rate equivalent to the full wages which the claimant would have received if his hours of work had not been shortened.

Other sections of the amendment of 1929 which affected seasonal workers provided that: (1) An unemployed person who had worked in another locality for more than half his base period was ineligible for benefits higher than those paid in his own district. This provision made it possible to reduce the benefit amount for a seasonal worker who received wages higher than he would have earned in his own district. (2) The worker's daily benefit amount could not exceed his average daily remuneration during the base period of employment used in determining the wage class to which he belonged. (3) The waiting period, previously 7 days, was graduated from 3 to 14 days, according to the number of dependents of the unemployed. 

The Seasonal Workers' Order of December 18, 1928, (see p. 74) 

was reissued on November 11, 1929, and adapted to the new amendments. The only important change was that, instead of 13 weeks of employment in a seasonal industry, 14 weeks within the preceding 26 were now set as the standard for the definition of a seasonal worker. Even though this change provided a somewhat more equitable method of determining whether or not an individual was a seasonal worker, it by no means achieved the equity attained under the British practice of taking a period of several years into account and thus preventing determinations based on temporary or accidental employment conditions (see pp. 50-51). The Federal Minister of 

Labor in Germany subsequently ruled that seasonal workers who were receiving emergency benefits on December 9, 1929, at the beginning of the winter season, were entitled to such payments until the benefit period had expired (14). 

10 But in a first claim for benefits, a total of 52 weeks of covered employment in the 2 years immediately preceding was required.

The distinction between seasonal and nonseasonal employment made by the Federal Institute for Employment Service and Unemployment Insurance applied only to the winter months, but the committees of management of the district labor offices were authorized to classify certain types of unemployment in the spring, summer, and fall as seasonal. The district labor offices availed themselves of this authority but made no extensive classifications. REDUCED BENEFIT AMOUNT AND MAXIMUM 

DURATION, 1931-34 The appointment by the German Government in January 1931 of an Advisory Commission (38) to study and report on the problem of unemployment inaugurated the second period of German unemploy-

GERMANY 

87 ment insurance, that of emergency measures. In its recommendations, the Commission pointed out that seasonal unemployment in Germany consisted primarily of unemployment in outdoor trades during the winter months, particularly in agriculture and in the building industry (38: pp. 78-101). Germany had to reckon with about 12 million workers who were unemployed every winter for about 3 months. SEASONAL WORKERS 

rejected the proposal to exclude seasonal workers from benefits during the off season and recommended that three special measures for seasonal workers be put into effect, as follows: (1) Extension of the qualifying period from 26 to 30 weeks. (2) Reduction of the benefit period from 26 to 20 weeks. (3) Decrease of the benefit rates from the level of ordinary insurance benefits to that of emergency benefits (38: p. 91).

These measures, unlike all previous regulations for seasonal workers within the general unemployment insurance system, related to unemployment at any time during the year. The extended waiting period required during the winter of 1927-28 applied only to the winter season, as did the reduction of benefit duration in effect during the winter months of 1928-29 and the reduction of benefit rates during 1929-31. Persons becoming unemployed in the off season sometimes owed their enforced idleness to changes that were cyclical or technological rather than seasonal. But, if they belonged to a seasonal occupation and a seasonal industry, it was presumed that their unemployment was seasonal in origin; hence they were subject to the special seasonal provisions until the off season was officially ended. 

It was asserted that persons declared seasonally unemployed were out of work on account of cyclical and technological unemployment only in rare instances (47: p. 510). Although this hypothesis is at least debatable, the prior legislative intent was to subject only unemployment during the off season to special regulations. 

The findings of the Advisory Commission served, in part at least, as a basis for the second emergency order, which was issued on June 

5, 1931, by the Federal President (15) 20 under article 48, section 2, of the German Constitution. It imposed stricter disqualifying conditions and amended the definition of suitable work by the requirements that an unemployed person had to accept work even if he was not fitted for it by training or previous employment and that benefits to persons over 21 years of age might be made conditional upon the acceptance of any work offered.21 The same order reduced the duration of benefits for seasonal workers from 26 to 20 weeks in a year. 

Under the new regulations, benefit restrictions for seasonal workers within the unemployment insurance system were not limited to the off season. The determination of the beginning and the end of the off season was no longer necessary, for the restrictions now 

20 This order became operative on June 29, 1931. "Previously required only of persons under 21.

GERMANY 

89 applied throughout the year to all persons who were classified as seasonal workers. Prior to that time seasonal workers had been entitled to benefits under the same conditions as nonseasonal employees except during the 4-month off season; they now became subject to special regulations throughout the year. Thus, from an economic standpoint, unemployment benefits for seasonal workers were no longer determined by season or off season, but only on the basis of the membership of a worker in a seasonal industry and occupation. 

Benefit amounts for seasonal workers were computed on a revised basis, in that the worker's wage class was determined from the average remuneration received during the 3 months (13 weeks) immediately preceding his registration as unemployed. This provision penalized the worker who dovetailed two or more types of employment if, after having been employed during the season at comparatively high wages, he found a job at low wages for 13 weeks at the beginning of the off season and received benefits computed at the lower rate. Short-time earnings had to be counted at the amount actually earned; formerly, for purposes of wage-class determination, they were valued at possible full-time wages (i. e., 40-hour week).

The ordinary benefit rates were reduced at this time by an amount between 7 and 14 percent, and the rates of emergency benefits were reduced also. Seasonal workers, under the provisions of the new de- 

I cree, received ordinary benefits at the rates established for emergency benefits of nonseasonal workers. From that time on, seasonal workers 

I were subject to any changes made in the rates of emergency benefits, although they were not required to pass the means test until they had drawn benefits for 20 weeks. The extent of the reduction in the benefit rates is illustrated in the following example: Until 1931 an unemployed seasonal worker in the highest wage class with spouse and one child was entitled to a weekly benefit of 20.25 Reichsmarks in the off season 

I and of 28.35 Reichsmarks at other periods. Beginning in 1931, his benefit amount was the same the whole year round and, at the rates of emergency benefits in force, it was 16.58 Reichsmarks, a decrease of

I about 17 percent from previous rates during off-season employment and of more than 40 percent from the former rates for unemployment during the season.

To compensate for the decrease of 6 weeks in the duration of ordinary benefits for seasonal workers, the duration of emergency benefits for this group was extended by 6 weeks. The waiting period, fixed according to family responsibility, was increased for both seasonal 159727-40-7

SEASONAL WORKERS 

and nonseasonal workers from 14, 7, and 3 days to 21, 14, and 7 days; and married women were excluded from benefits at any time unless they were indigent.

Previously, a seasonal worker had been defined as a person who, during the 26 weeks of covered employment preceding his registration as unemployed, had been employed more than 14 weeks in a seasonal establishment and in a seasonal occupation. Establishment, occupation, and length of employment were the determining factors. The stringent new restrictions for seasonal workers' benefits occasioned much hardship. Since so brief a period of employment was used as a basis in classifying a worker as seasonal, nonseasonal workers sometimes suffered. A person who had worked in a seasonal occupation and industry for 14 weeks, although his regular work was nonseasonal, was in danger of being classified as a seasonal worker when he lost his job. In order to remedy this injustice, the governing body of the 

Federal Institute provided, by an order of August 27, 1931, that employment in a seasonal occupation and industry for at least 26 weeks out of 52, rather than 14 out of 26, should determine whether or not a person was a seasonal worker (16).22 Thus the classification was less subject to chance. The new provision involved additional administrative work in the increased checking of employment records. In many cases, however, the record of previous employment for 26 weeks and 1 day was sufficient to classify a worker as seasonal or nonseasonal. 

Under previous regulations, the worker's employment record for 26 weeks prior to his last registration of unemployment had to be considered in determining his classification and in computing his benefits. 

The 26-week period was different for each new registration of unemployment after a spell of employment, no matter how brief. When the benefit restrictions for seasonal workers applied only to the 3 or 4 months of the off season, during which employment was infrequent, the provision was difficult to administer, but after 1931, when the restrictions for seasonal workers applied to the whole year, the administrative burden was still greater. To reduce the volume of administrative work and to simplify computations, the 52-week period preceding the first registration after the completion of the qualifying period was used as the worker's employment record in determining his benefits.

The severe restrictions of the emergency order of June 1931 aroused such opposition that the Government, in consideration of the large This order became effective on Sept. 7, 1931.

GERMANY 

91 number of unemployed (more than 42 millions in the late fall) and the expectation of a further increase during the winter months to about 6 millions, issued the third emergency order of October 6, 1931, rescinding some of the strictest provisions (18). For example, an earlier provision was restored, basing wage class and benefit amount on the wages earned in the 26 weeks (6 months) prior to registration as unemployed. In the face of the rapidly advancing depression and of decreasing wage levels, the 13-week period which had been used for wage-class determination since June 5, 1931, had resulted in much hardship.23 The order of October 6 also restored the benefit rates for seasonal workers to the level of ordinary unemployment insurance benefits except during the off season when they were identical with those for emergency benefits of nonseasonal workers. The off season was fixed for the entire Reich by the governing body of the Federal Institute. A resolution of October 1, 1931, which became effective 

October 5, 1931, reduced the benefit period for nonseasonal workers to 20 weeks and that for seasonal workers to 16 weeks a year (17). 

Although the general provisions of the emergency order of October 6 came into force on October 12, 1931, the special regulations for seasonal workers did not become effective until March 28, 1932, because it was impossible to make the necessary redeterminations on such short notice. For seasonal workers, therefore, the restrictions of the 

June decree remained in force during the winter of 1931-32. 

Social and economic changes arising from the new political and economic situation in Germany set in motion forces which, in June 1932, transformed the unemployment insurance system into a combination of relief and insurance, with relief in the position of primary importance. By the emergency decree of June 14, 1932, the Federal

Government reserved the right to modify provisions for ordinary insurance benefits, to reduce the rates (but not below those for emergency benefits), and to require claimants to prove their need for relief after they had received insurance payments for a specified period (19). These sweeping modifications likewise authorized the Federal Government to set aside any provision of the unemployment insurance law, to change the emergency benefit system, to revise the health insurance contributions for persons in receipt of unemployment benefits, or to modify the system otherwise as expediency demanded. "The contribution rate had been increased to 3% percent of wages in January 1930, to 

4 percent in August 1930, and to 6%½ percent on Oct. 6, 1930.

SEASONAL WORKERS 

The Government established flat benefit rates varying with wage classes, number of dependents, and place of residence of the unemployed. Benefit rates were reduced, on the average, by 23 percent. 

Since the benefit amount varied with the locality where benefits were paid, the provisions for reducing benefits to the level paid in a worker's home district were repealed as unnecessary. At the same time, the special benefit rates for seasonal workers were abolished, with the result that the benefit amounts were the same as for nonseasonal workers during the first 6 weeks of unemployment, whether in the season or off season. After the 6 weeks (36 benefit days) had elapsed, a means test was required preliminary to additional benefits of 10 weeks for seasonal workers and 14 weeks for nonseasonal workers. The maximum duration of ordinary insurance benefits-16 weeks for seasonal and 20 weeks for nonseasonal workers-was not changed. At the expiration of the maximum weeks of ordinary benefits, a more restrictive needs test was required for emergency benefits (20). 

On June 17, 1932, the rates at which emergency benefits were to be paid were announced. The rates were to be lower than those for the reduced ordinary insurance benefits. As a result, for some wage classes emergency benefits were about 10 percent lower than formerly. 

The actual amounts depended on the outcome of a second means test at the end of the 16-week benefit period for seasonal workers and 

20-week period for nonseasonal (21).

A period of 58 weeks was set as the duration of ordinary and emergency benefits combined and was applicable to both seasonal and nonseasonal workers. Thus, although seasonal workers could draw ordinary benefits for only 16 weeks instead of 20, they could draw emergency benefits for 6 weeks longer than under the earlier provisions. 

The 6-week period of ordinary insurance benefits, granted without the needs test, restored in part the conditions prevailing for seasonal workers during the winter season of 1928-29. The drastic reductions in the amount of benefits under the new rates necessitated a special winter supplement to persons in wage classes I to IV, inclusive, payable during the period from October 3, 1932, to April 1, 1933 (22). 

Furthermore, between November 28, 1932, and March 31, 1933, emergency benefits were paid whether the unemployed worker had exhausted his rights or not (23); thus the seasonal worker could draw benefits throughout the winter, the season when his unemployment hazard was greatest.

GERMANY 

93 

EQUAL TREATMENT FOR SEASONAL AND NONSEASONAL WORKERS, 1934-38 

There has been no distinction between seasonal and nonseasonal workers since 1934, which marks the beginning of the third phase of German unemployment insurance policy, a period during which the insurance principle has been subordinated to consideration of individual need. Emergency benefits were paid to all groups without any time limitation after March 15, 1933 (24). The exclusion from coverage of female domestic servants on May 12, 1933 (4), and on October 1, 1933, of workers in agriculture (including arable farming, horticulture, fruit growing, viticulture, pasture and meadow farming, stock breeding and fattening), forestry, and fishing, removed certain seasonal occupations from the insurance system (5).24 An order of 

December 11, 1933, reduced the waiting period for all workers from 

21, 14, and 7 days to 14, 7, and 3 days (25). On December 9, 1934, the maximum duration of ordinary insurance benefits for both seasonal and nonseasonal workers became 20 weeks (26). This simplification eliminated much of the burden of administrative work, such as separate accounting for seasonal workers' benefits, determination of seasonal workers' benefit period, and similar procedures. 

The sharp decrease in unemployment and the high contribution rates, maintained at the 1930 level of 62 percent of wages equally divided between employer and worker, resulted in a steadily increasing surplus in the fund. These factors, together with the strict control of the labor market and of employment conditions, made the need for special seasonal workers' regulations less urgent, particularly since the local administrative agencies had authority to grant or deny the benefit claim of each individual applicant on the basis of a means test at the end of 6 weeks of ordinary benefits. 

The years 1935-37 were marked by a further increase in employment, a growing surplus in the fund, a reorganization of labor codes, a tightened control of the labor market, and the launching of the Four-Year Plan. A new policy was put into effect by a revision of the unemployment insurance system in the acts of December 1937 (27; 28) and January 1938 (29). Ordinary benefits are unlimited in duration but, at the end of 6 weeks of benefits (36 days), are payable only after a means test. A reduction of the benefit period to not less than 20 weeks (120 days) is permitted, however, for certain regions, groups of persons, or occupations. It is still possible to restrict the 24 "By this order the Federal Government took over the total cost of emergency benefits.

SEASONAL WORKERS 

duration of ordinary benefits payable to seasonal workers, but so far no such condition has been imposed. Emergency benefits have been superseded entirely by ordinary benefits, paid without a time limit. 

The social and economic objectives of the present administration are directed toward controlling the national labor market and caring for all able-bodied workers by means of the insurance-relief system. 

A part of the large income of the Federal Institute for Employment Service and Unemployment Insurance goes now to old-age insurance, and another part to projects for increasing employment opportunities and for relief payments to short-time workers. 

With the gradual transformation of the insurance system into a system of relief and labor-market control, the seasonal problem, which was one of the most urgent and frequently debated questions during the first years of German unemployment insurance, has become less important to the financial status of the unemployment fund. The 

Government has established a vast system of public works and labor service in which it utilizes supervised hiring and firing to manage the labor market. By these means, and by the use of the means test, the Government keeps seasonal workers from becoming too heavy a financial burden to the fund during the winter when seasonal unemployment is greatest.

GENERAL PROVISIONS IN FORCE IN 1938 

Seasonal workers are, at the present time, subject to no conditions other than those required of all claimants. Many groups among them are entirely excluded from the system by the general coverage stipulations; furthermore, certain requirements, for example the one involving length of previous employment, fall more heavily on the seasonal than on the nonseasonal worker. The close correlation of benefits with need, however, insures protection for this group. Coverage At the present time, seasonal workers are affected by the exclusions provisions of the unemployment insurance law rather than by its coverage provisions. In contrast to the general trend in the British system, the excluded groups have become more numerous and more significant as the system has developed during the last 11 years. At present, the outstanding exclusions influencing the status of seasonal workers are the following:

GERMANY 

95 (a) Employment in agriculture, forestry, fishing in inland waters (including fish breeding), and fishing in coastal waters is not covered. “Agriculture" includes arable farming, horticulture, fruit growing, viticulture, pasture and meadow farming, stock breeding, and stock fattening. Employment under a contract of apprenticeship in any of these four categories is excluded also, irrespective of its nature and duration." (b) Employment compensated by a share of the catch in large-scale herring fishing carried on with luggers (sharefishing) has been exempted from the beginning. The number of persons engaged in these occupations, however, is almost negligible (about 9,000 in 1925). (c) Employment as a female domestic servant has been excluded since May 12, 1933. This exclusion applies to women who work as domestic servants in the seasonal tourist, hotel, and restaurant businesses (4).20 (d) Employment of children attending grade school, including certain young seasonal workers who work full time during the school vacations and part time during the rest of the year, is not insured. (e) Work on relief projects is also excluded from coverage. (f) Certain other categories of employment, such as home work of certain kinds, work that is "inconsiderable" in extent, and casual or temporary work are likewise not covered, although unemployment in these fields is of great importance in the total volume of seasonal unemployment. High-paid salaried work is also excluded from coverage. (g) The employment of foreign migratory workers and frontier-zone workers is in most cases excluded from coverage. This provision applies particularly to seasonal workers who migrate into Germany from other countries year after year. Many of these workers are excluded also by the exemption of agriculture.

Benefit Eligibility 

The general requirements for benefit eligibility likewise operate to limit payments of benefits to seasonal workers. 

Definition of Unemployment.—The German law defines an unemployed person as one normally a wage or salary earner who is temporarily out of a job and is not earning enough for his maintenance by independent work, particularly in agricultural or industrial work The exclusion of these occupations removed a large number of persons who are subject as a rule to the risk of seasonal unemployment. As shown above (see table 4), more than 9 percent of the seasonally unemployed persons receiving benefits on February 28, 1929, were employed in agriculture, horticulture, forestry, and fishing (more than 122,000 of the 1,300,000 seasonal workers receiving benefits or relief). More than 44 percent of these seasonally unemployed workers were women. 

Of the 32,296,496 gainfully occupied persons, 9,343,500, or almost 29 percent, were engaged in agriculture, according to the Census of June 16, 1933. Of these, 6,698,564 were owners, managers, or assisting family members (more than 20 percent of the total gainfully occupied); and 2,644,936 (more than 8 percent of the total gainfully occupied) were wage or salary earners. *According to the Census of June 16, 1933, there were 1,249,637 female domestic servants in Germany.

SEASONAL WORKERS 

on his own account; or who cannot support himself by continuing to operate a private undertaking; or who is not earning or cannot earn a livelihood jointly with his or her close relatives. 

This definition and its interpretation by the employment offices may easily lead to the disqualification from benefits of seasonal workers who, although they have completed the qualifying period, have an opportunity to perform independent work, particularly in agriculture or in enterprises conducted by close relatives. It may render ineligible many persons who have moved from the farm to the city for seasonal work.

Definition of Suitable Work.-Work is considered suitable, regardless of the claimant's training or experience, if standard wages are being paid for the job, if the job is reasonably in accord with his physical capacity and future career, if the place in which he has to live conforms to sanitary and moral standards, and if appropriate care can be taken of the worker's family when the work requires him to take up a new residence.

Qualifying Period.-Since 1927 the qualifying period in German unemployment insurance has been 26 weeks of covered employment in the 12 months immediately preceding a claimant's registration as unemployed at the public employment office of his district. Persons employed under a monthly contract must show 6 months of covered employment. Any employment lasting from Monday through Saturday is deemed to be a week of employment. Otherwise, the qualifying period is 182 days, including Sundays, of covered employment, whether consecutive or not. A stricter regulation has been in effect since 1929 for workers who claim benefits for the first time. These workers are required to have fulfilled a qualifying period of 52 weeks of covered employment in the preceding 2 years. Under certain conditions these periods may be extended to the preceding 3 years for a first claim and to 26 weeks in the preceding 3 years for subsequent claims. Thus a seasonal worker who is in covered employment for less than half the year cannot qualify for benefits unless he finds additional covered employment. This provision particularly affects short-season industries, such as canning and certain other agricultural processing industries. The seasonal worker who supplements his regular work with off-season employment has the advantage over other seasonal workers in that, if he obtains supplementary employment, he can more readily fulfill his qualifying-period requirement. 

It is not necessary that he show a specified amount of wages earned

GERMANY 

97 during the period, as is prescribed in most State laws in the United States.

Waiting Period.-The waiting period extends only for a period of 3 to 14 days, according to the family status of the claimant. In actual practice, it is more liberal than in many American State laws, particularly since it may be waived when unemployment follows a period of short-time work. This provision offers an advantage to workers in seasonal industries in which the season may be terminated gradually by means of work-sharing devices which entail short-time work.

Means Test.-After 6 weeks of benefits a claimant must prove a state of need in order to retain his eligibility. Thus a seasonal worker with high seasonal earnings is likely to be disqualified after 6 weeks unless he can prove that he is in need of relief. The means test is applied by the local welfare authorities, and the employment offices "should be guided," although not necessarily governed, by their decision. In making their decisions, the employment offices consider particularly the social position of the unemployed worker and endeavor to preserve his working capacity. Married women are not entitled to unemployment benefits unless they are in need of relief. This general provision disqualifies married women who are employed in seasonal occupations as well as those in nonseasonal employment. In the means test, the Government has created a potentially effective instrument which may be used to disqualify certain groups of persons or certain occupations, to adjust benefit terms for seasonal workers in conformity with specific aims, and to regulate the entire German labor market.

Benefit Amount and Duration 

Seasonality is no longer a direct factor in determining the amount and duration of benefits. The rates of benefits are adjusted to conditions of need as indicated by number of dependents, locality, and wage class; and the duration of benefits is limited by a needs test or by the state of the labor market.

Computation.-Under the scale effective since June 1937, benefit amounts are adjusted to variations in family responsibility, in costs of living, and in previous earnings. Flat amounts are granted according to number of dependents, type of locality, and wage class. 

The locality class is determined by the place of residence rather than by the place where the worker's wages are earned. If seasonal work-

SEASONAL WORKERS 

ers who have had employment in a large city during the season live in a community of lower locality class, they are entitled to no more than the lower rate. The deciding factor in the determination of the wage class is the average remuneration received during the preceding 26 weeks of employment or during the preceding 6 months if remuneration is paid monthly. The period in both instances is figured from the time of first registration of unemployment after the completion of the qualifying period. If a claimant's wages are reduced because shortage of work in the enterprise prevented his working the customary number of hours, the remuneration which he would have received for full-time work is used as the basis for his wage classification.

Furthermore, in determining the wage class, no wage may be considered that is either higher or lower than that which served as the basis for the payment of contributions. Periods of sickness, local or legal holidays for which no wages are paid, and vacations without pay are not taken into account. The days thus excepted are added to the actual days of work during the 26 weeks by counting days prior to this period until a total of 156 days is reached. 

The "unit wage," established as the typical weekly wage for each wage class, which was formerly used in computing benefits as a percentage of the average wage, has lost its practical significance because the benefit amount is now expressed in flat rates. The maximum benefit, including the family supplement, is 80 percent of the unemployed person's average wage and may be increased to 100 percent for persons in wage classes I, II, and III. The benefit amount for any day during the benefit period, determined according to wage class, may not be higher than the average daily wage during the base period of 26 weeks.

Duration. In principle there has been no limitation of the benefit period since December 22, 1937 (27). Unemployment benefits are paid as a right for the first 36 benefit days (6 weeks) to all unemployed insured persons except married women and persons who be come unemployed after having been engaged in the labor service or in military service. At the end of this time, a means test is required, after which the period may be extended indefinitely. For certain groups of persons or for certain districts, however, the Federal Minister of Labor may terminate benefit payments at the end of 20 weeks or 120 benefit days. The administration of this provision was entrusted to the President of the Federal Institute

GERMANY 

99 for Employment Service and Unemployment Insurance (28) and delegated by him to the chairmen of the district labor offices. The chairmen may limit the benefit duration for districts, localities, occupations, or groups of persons if the state of the labor market war- Irants such a procedure. The period of benefits cannot be reduced to less than 120 days and, when the limitation is rescinded, benefit claims again become valid for persons who had qualified for benefits before the benefit period was reduced (29). No such limitation has thus far been ordered by the district labor offices. 

The present plan is similar to the procedure adopted during the winter of 1927-28, when the waiting period could be lengthened at the discretion of the district employment offices. It had proved to be an unsatisfactory expedient at that time because of variations in local decisions. The political organization was quite different 10 years ago, however. At the present time, the ascendency of centralized leadership insures unified operation, in spite of decentralized administration.

CONCLUSION 

Germany, in contrast to Great Britain, has been forced to give serious attention to the problem of seasonal unemployment. Industries which in Great Britain are only slightly seasonal show marked fluctuation in Germany. In view of the nature and scope of German unemployment insurance, seasonal unemployment had to be taken into account from the outset and special eligibility conditions provided to modify the benefit rights of seasonal workers. A series of special regulations for seasonal workers under the general unemployment insurance system had been tried between 1927 and 1934, but with little success. Finally, by 1934, all statutory distinctions between seasonal and nonseasonal workers had been repealed in the gradual transition from an insurance program to one of relief and of labormarket control.

The basic approach to the seasonal problem in Germany lay in the method of determining who was to be considered a seasonal worker. 

In Great Britain this determination centers on the individual worker; a seasonal worker is defined as one whose normal employment is in a wholly seasonal occupation, and the actual decision on all relevant matters is left to the judgment of local authorities. In Germany, on the other hand, a seasonal worker was considered one who had been employed for a uniformly and strictly defined period of time in a

SEASONAL WORKERS 

seasonal occupation in a seasonal industry. Both seasonal industries and seasonal occupations were enumerated in the statutes, and this classification applied to all regions of the country. With the same disregard of local climatic and industrial characteristics, the off season in Germany was uniformly determined as a period of 3 to 4 months between December and March. The choice of these dates shows that the Germans were concerned primarily with winter unemployment in outdoor occupations. 

The definition of a seasonal worker as one who had been engaged in a seasonal occupation within a seasonal industry for more than one-half of the 26 weeks preceding his registration of unemployment during the off season covered several distinct types of seasonal workers. 

Wholly seasonal workers (those who looked for and performed only seasonal work without expecting employment during the off season) were not differentiated from seasonal workers who sought and obtained off-season employment or from chance seasonal workers who had taken up seasonal work temporarily because no employment was available in their regular nonseasonal trade. The wholly seasonal worker was an inactive member of the labor market during the off season; however, the chance seasonal worker and, to a lesser degree, the worker who attempted to get off-season employment, offered their services at any time during the year. This failure to differentiate the various types of seasonal workers was partially corrected in later years by basing the seasonal determination on a period of one-half of 52 weeks, although even then any worker who had been employed in a seasonal industry and occupation for 26 weeks and a day was considered a seasonal worker if he became unemployed during the off season. After 1931, the special provisions for seasonal workers applied not only to the off-season period but to unemployment at any time of the year, with the result that the seasonal factor bore little relation to the worker's benefit rights.

German policy toward seasonal workers shows two phases of development prior to 1934. The first period lasted from 1927 to 1931, during which time benefit eligibility and benefit rights of seasonal workers were subject to restrictions only during the off season. After 

1931, however, these restrictions were in force throughout the year. 

During the first of these two stages, three methods were tried: extension of the waiting period, reduction of benefit duration, and reduction of benefit rates. The main defect, common to all these methods, was that centralized and strict determination precluded the consid-

GERMANY 

101 

ORE 

eration of differences in climatic and personal factors. In addition, the provision for an extended waiting period was inadequately administered because, unlike the other measures, it was left to local authorities, who worked under insufficient guidance in interpreting hastily drafted regulations. Because the extension was expressed as a flat duration of either 1 or 2 additional weeks, it did not permit individual treatment of seasonal workers. 

The local authorities at their discretion were permitted to waive this requirement for persons who had had off-season employment in previous years. The pressure of great numbers of unemployed was too strong to be resisted by local administrators, with the result that the extension was seldom required. Even if the regulations had been enforced strictly, the extension of the waiting period would have been insufficient to safeguard the fund from depletion. With a maximum benefit duration of 26 weeks and an off season of about 16 weeks, an "additional waiting period of 1 or 2 weeks could hardly accomplish a great saving. At the same time, this small difference of 1 or 2 weeks was insufficient to distinguish effectively between persons who were normally not active members of the labor market during the off season, and those who, as a rule, looked for and obtained off-season employment. The measure not only created dissatisfaction among the workers but also resulted in a deficit which in a few months wiped out the reserve which had been accumulated in the fund. 

Not much more successful was the second expedient employed in the 1927-31 period, i. e., shortening the duration of ordinary benefits 

I during the off season to 6 weeks. The short flat duration could be justified only on grounds of protecting the fund without regard for other objectives. If unemployment during the off season is a certainty for wholly seasonal workers, there can be no reason for paying insurance benefits to them during that period, no matter how short a benefit period may be adopted. If, on the other hand, unemployment during the off season is a probability but not a certainty, benefits should be payable throughout the slack season to unemployed workers who ordinarily obtain dovetailing employment. Because most seasonal workers exhausted their rights to the maximum 6 weeks of benefits, a special relief fund had to be established, four-fifths of which was provided by the Federal Government. Since a means test disqualified only about 12 percent of the unemployed seasonal workers from this relief, seasonal unemployment remained an extremely grave fiscal problem.

SEASONAL WORKERS 

The third expedient adopted during the period 1927-31 was that of reducing benefit rates for seasonal workers during the off season. On the whole, the same objections apply to this method as to the reduction of the benefit duration. There was, however, some justification for reducing the benefit rate for high-paid seasonal workers when they became unemployed during the off season. In a system in which the benefit amount was computed on the basis of previous earnings, some high-paid seasonal workers had an undue advantage in that their benefits were higher than those of steady workers whose wages, presumably paid throughout the year, were proportionately lower. 

Since, however, contributions in Germany were paid at a fixed percentage of actual wages received, in a given period the high-paid seasonal worker contributed more than the low-paid steady worker in actual amount, although both paid identical percentages of wages. 

In the payment of benefits, the rates were lower for workers in higher wage classes, and, when benefits for seasonal workers were reduced, the reduction applied only to the higher-paid workers. An important factor in wage-class determination was the length of the period on which the computation of wages was based. When the wage class was determined for seasonal workers on the basis of earnings in a short period (for example, 13 weeks), the wages earned tended to reflect temporary or chance conditions rather than normal wage rates. 

During the period 1931-34, seasonal workers' benefit rights were decreased, not only during off-season periods but during the season as well. The distinction between season and off season thus lost its significance; a worker who happened to have been employed in a seasonal occupation and industry was entitled to only 20 weeks of benefits a year, regardless of his unemployment and of when it occurred. The benefit period for nonseasonal workers was 26 weeks. 

In addition to the reduced benefit duration, seasonal workers of all wage classes were subject to reduced benefit rates throughout the year. These rates were the same as those of emergency benefits for nonseasonal workers.

The strained financial condition of the unemployment fund soon made it necessary to reduce the yearly benefit duration to 20 weeks for nonseasonal workers and to 16 weeks for seasonal workers. In 

June 1932, payment of benefits beyond 6 weeks was made conditional upon a means test. At the same time, benefit rates, both ordinary and emergency, were reduced considerably. On the other hand, sea-

GERMANY 

103 sonal workers again received benefits at the ordinary rates. Seasonal workers had always been entitled to an extended duration of benefits either under the special relief program for these workers or under emergency benefit regulations, and these payments were a heavy drain upon the unemployment fund.

The period 1931–34 clearly demonstrates the change of the system from insurance to relief financed by compulsory contributions from workers and employers and by grants from the Government. Since the end of 1934, and increasingly since December 1937, the predominant emphasis upon relief has made it impossible to appraise the effect of the repeal of all special provisions for seasonal workers. 

With regard to the seasonal problem, the 10 years' experience of the German unemployment insurance system would seem to make one point particularly clear: In its original, as well as in its modified form, German unemployment insurance has never been able to cope with the high rates of seasonal unemployment during the winter months. Virtually all attempts in this direction were motivated and directed by the need for reducing the financial burden on the unemployment fund. The fund was always in a precarious condition until the Government took over a large share of the costs of extended benefits. The other phase of the seasonal problem, that of economic and social equity, was carefully considered, but all measures to achieve equity were outweighed by the financial exigencies of the fund. No permanent solution was found which satisfied both the actuary and the social economist. 

There have been no special regulations for seasonal workers in operation in the German unemployment insurance system since the end of 1934. The trend is toward a unified provision for all workers, and is quite unlike that observed in other countries, notably Great Britain, Austria, and Poland, where a differentiation between seasonal and nonseasonal workers has been gradually developed. Since 

1933, German policy towards seasonal workers has evolved in response to the two primary objectives of the totalitarian Government: to control the German labor market and to regulate the German economy. An unprecedented change has taken place in economic and employment conditions, enabling the Government to reabsorb most of the unemployed within a short period of time. With the increase in the number of employed persons, the income of the unemployment fund increased, while decreasing unemployment brought about a

SEASONAL WORKERS 

sharp reduction in benefit payments. Thus there was accumulated a considerable surplus which is now being used to finance other social insurance and relief programs and public works of all kinds. Even the seasonal increase of unemployment during the winter months does not greatly diminish the favorable balance of the unemployment fund. On the other hand, the application of the means test enables the local employment offices to deny benefits to workers who do not need them. The limitation of benefit rights, combined with the high contribution rate, makes any special regulations for seasonal workers unnecessary. 

As concern with the problem of seasonal unemployment in the Government program for unemployment insurance decreased, the problem and scope of short-time work and short-time benefits became steadily more important, at least until 1937. Although short-time work is usually attributed either to lack of raw materials or to lack of markets, it is not unreasonable to assume that a certain amount of short-time work is disguised seasonal unemployment and that shorttime benefits may represent, to some extent, benefits paid for reduced employment arising from seasonal fluctuation in production or demand. Whether this presumption can be justified, and to what extent, can be determined only on the basis of data gathered by more detailed and extensive study.
