PART IV Seasonal Workers and Unemployment 

Insurance in Austria

INTRODUCTION 

The most distinctive feature of the Austrian unemployment insurance system is the means test. The first draft of the original law, designed to replace a relief system established at the close of the World War, carried the significant title of "unemployment relief bill," and throughout the 18 years of Austrian experience with unemployment insurance the receipt of benefits has been made conditional upon the financial needs of the recipient ¹-a requirement which is diametrically opposed to the British and American concept of unemployment compensation as an insurance system, paying benefits to an insured worker as a right, regardless of his financial resources. 

Moreover, Austria was the first country to differentiate the unemployment risks of stable and unstable industries by increasing the contribution rates for employers and employees in industries with a high incidence of unemployment.

The act of 1920 and successive laws established three tests of need varying in severity with the duration of unemployment: (1) To obtain the first 12 weeks of benefits the unemployed worker's "subsistence" had to be "endangered";2 (2) to obtain an extension of benefits to 30 weeks (or to 20 weeks for persons who had been employed mainly in agriculture or forestry prior to their entering covered employment), an insured worker had to be "in need"; and to obtain emergency benefits (unemployment assistance), a worker had to be "in particularly necessitous circumstances."

THE ORIGINAL LAW, 1920-22 

The first draft of the Austrian unemployment insurance law provided for the organization of unemployment insurance by industries in separate systems to be set up gradually as the conditions of the labor market permitted (40: p. 631). The Government bill, when it 

1 This provision was originally adopted as a temporary measure but was never repealed. 

As a rule, subsistence was presumed to be endangered when a wage or salary earner lost the job upon which he normally depended for a livelihood. 

For literature cited see pp. 146-149. 108

AUSTRIA 

109 was finally submitted to the Constitutional Assembly of the newly established Austrian Republic, proposed a classification of occupations by unemployment risk, to be used in scaling contributions. 

The bill did not propose to establish an unemployment insurance system in the strict sense of the term, but rather to provide for payments to needy unemployed workers in the insured group from a Government fund made up of contributions from employers, employees, and the Government, each bearing one-third the cost of the program. The proportions of contributions actually borne by the 

Government, by employers, and by workers varied frequently during the period 1920-22 because of the increasing costs of emergency benefits. In 1922, for example, workers and employers each paid 40 percent of the total costs of all benefits, including emergency benefits or unemployment assistance; the Federal Government paid the remaining 20 percent of the costs but was reimbursed in part by local governments.

The groups of persons covered by the new unemployment insurance program were those insured at that time under the compulsory health insurance system: wage and salary earners in manufacturing, handicrafts, commerce, and transportation. The health insurance agency collected the contributions under the unemployment insurance law which were computed as a percentage of the health insurance rates. Notable exceptions were agriculture and domestic service. A large number of workers (almost one-third of the gainfully employed population) and many in seasonal occupations were excluded from the unemployment insurance system by the exclusion of agriculture.

The official explanation of the Government bill pointed out, in discussing the problem of the occupational and seasonal risk, that the volume of unemployment varied considerably among the different groups of workers (33: Vol. 4, No. 680). Seasonal industries afforded employment for only a certain number of months during the year, whereas other industries provided steady employment throughout the year, except for periods of cyclical depression. In determining the proportion of the burden to be placed on each of the various industries to meet the cost of the insurance system, it seemed just, the report declared, that industry as a whole should bear the risk of cyclical unemployment, no matter what branches were affected by cyclical depression. The cost of the recurrent unemployment which

SEASONAL WORKERS 

resulted from the seasonality of an industry, however, should be borne by that industry because the maintenance of its workers during periods of idleness should be considered a part of the cost of production. The Government bill (33: Vol. 5, No. 783), therefore, stipulated that the contribution rates for employers and workers in certain industries should be fixed according to the volume of their regularly recurring unemployment as classified in a "code of unemployment categories." The original unemployment insurance law which was enacted March 24, 1920, and became effective May 9, 1920, included this provision (2: sec. 25, subsec. (2)). It was expected that, after the economic situation had become more stable, the experience gained in the operation of the code of unemployment categories would make possible a relatively fixed assignment of an unemployment risk classification and a contribution rate for each industry. The law permitted the adoption of such categories for a limited period, and their adjustment on the basis of experience. On June 10 an executive order of the Ministry of Social Administration established three "unemployment risk" groups for this purpose, classifying workers and employers according to the rate of unemployment found in the industries to which their establishments belonged. 

The first group was composed of industries in which the unemployment risk was slight and employment relatively stable. Workers in these industries, who paid the lowest rate of contributions, included wage earners and salaried employees on railways and steamships, because their employment contracts covered long periods and their unemployment was relatively minor in volume and intensity. 

Persons employed in mining and smelting establishments were included because of the shortage of workers in these industries at that time. Subsequently, by an order of August 25, 1920 (15), employees of the sickle and scythe industries and of banks and savings banks were classified in this group as well as employees of Government agencies such as the administration of the Tobacco Monopoly, the Government Salt Works, the Government Printing Office, the Government Mint, and employees of the social insurance agencies whose employment was comparatively steady or permanent. 

Group 3, which paid the highest contribution rates, consisted of industries with great employment fluctuations and high unemploy- The principles of this bill were incorporated in the unemployment insurance law of 1920.

AUSTRIA 

111 ment risk: persons employed in the building trades, i. e., in industrial = establishments engaged in construction work, in work on buildings, and in building and wrecking. Hotel and restaurant employees were also placed in this contribution class. Workers in establishments engaged in the sale and transportation of merchandise, both wholesale and retail, came within this category because the economic depression had created a very high unemployment risk in these occupations.

All other industries were placed in group 2 which was considered the "normal" group because it was composed of workers who were subject to an average unemployment risk. Approximately 85 percent of all persons covered by the program belonged to group 2, about 6 percent were in group 1, and between 8 and 9 percent in group 3.

A further adjustment in contribution rates was made in accordance with daily wage rates divided into 15 wage classes. SEASONAL WORKERS 

The higher contribution rate was the only requirement made of seasonal workers and their employers to compensate for their disproportionate claims for benefits as compared with those of more steadily employed persons. They paid at the rate of 250 percent of the contribution rate imposed on the most stable industries, and 125 percent of the "average" contribution rate. The original act of 1920 imposed no special restriction on seasonal workers such as reducing the benefit period, extending the waiting period, or excluding such workers from benefits during the off season. The qualifying period was likewise uniform for the three unemployment risk groups: 20 weeks of covered employment during the preceding year (under certain conditions during the preceding 2 years). Benefits were paid for 12 weeks in 12 months. No person was entitled to benefits unless "his means of subsistence were endangered" by his unemployment. The claimant was not eligible for benefits if he refused to accept suitable work, defined as work appropriate to his physical capacity, free from danger to health or morals, properly paid, and offering no material hindrance to his resuming his own occupation. After 8 weeks of benefits, he had to accept work even when it was not in his own trade or when it was outside the district in which he resided, if the work was otherwise suitable, if adequate accommodations were available, and if proper care could be taken of his dependents. 6 From 1920 until 1922, when the original law was superseded, seasonal workers could be disqualified from extended benefits during the season when employment conditions in their occupations were good. The district industrial commissions, which were the State administrative authorities for unemployment insurance and employment offices, were authorized to specify, for the whole or for part of their areas, industrial groups to be excluded from benefits on account of the favorable condition of the labor market."

By the act of July 9, 1920 (effective August 30, 1920), unemployment contribution rates were established at a fixed percentage of the contribution rates effective under the health insurance program. 

The same act modified the contribution rates for health insurance in accordance with the new monetary values of inflation, and unemployment insurance rates were correspondingly increased. The practical determination of the unemployment risk group in which an insured person should be classified, however, was a difficult problem for the health insurance agencies. The Ministry of Social 

Administration, accordingly, formulated more detailed instructions in its order of August 26, 1920. The unemployment risk group (contribution class) was determined in accordance with the "prevailing" (überwiegend) nature of the enterprise in order that the entire establishment might be classified in a single group even though its work covered more than one industry or industrial branch. This regulation permitted uniform contribution rates for all employees of the establishment. A uniform classification was also applied to manufacturing establishments which had their own stores for the sale of their products or those of other manufacturers. Only when separate

SEASONAL WORKERS 

branches of establishments maintained individual accounts with the health insurance agency directly was separate classification permissible.

This regulation remained in effect until March 28, 1921, when the schedule adopted on August 30, 1920 (see table 2), was superseded. 

Unemployment insurance contributions were set at the following increased percentages of normal health insurance contributions for each of the three groups: 7.5 percent for group 1, 15 percent for group 2, and 18 percent for group 3. The new system of computing unemployment insurance contributions according to health insurance rates greatly simplified procedure for the health insurance agency which collected both contributions.

GENERAL COVERAGE AND BENEFIT PROVISIONS, 1922-26 

On January 30, 1922 (16), the "unemployment risk" groups were abolished because, it was alleged, the special rates had had little effect upon the total amount of contributions (38: p. 19). It is more probable, however, that the determining influences were the difficulty of administration and the opposition of Austrian workers, particularly those in seasonal trades.

Between 1922 and 1926, no special regulations for seasonal workers were in force, although certain exclusion provisions and modifications of the qualifying-period requirement had considerable effect upon the administration of unemployment insurance for seasonal workers.

Subsequent to October 1920, the district commissions had been permitted to extend to 30 weeks, instead of the statutory 12 weeks, the benefit duration for persons in need. A new act of October 11, 1922, permitted restrictions upon the payment of benefits to workers who lost their jobs at the time when activity in their customary occupations was deemed at its height. The district industrial commissions were authorized to grant an 18-week extension of benefits to unemployed persons who were in distress and willing to work "if the condition of the labor market in their particular occupations continued to be very unfavorable" and to specify the occupations to which this extension was applicable (17). On the other hand, they could deny all extended benefits to unemployed workers in a given trade by declaring a favorable state of the labor market, with the result that a workman who became unemployed during the season

AUSTRIA 

115 could be ruled ineligible for benefits beyond the 12-week statutory minimum. Each district commission carried out the administration of this provision as local and industrial circumstances demanded, and a wide diversity of practice was the result. Individual circumstances (except for application of the needs test) were not taken into account in making this determination, and numerous appeals from decisions of the local employment offices resulted from discrimination against seasonal workers. 

The practice of disqualifying workers during the season, when their unemployment risk, although not excessive, is still very real, became as typical of the Austrian unemployment insurance system as was the means test. The measure was apparently designed to safeguard the unemployment fund, but it was hardly defensible as a social or economic policy, since workers and their employers contributed the major share of the fund from which benefits were paid. 

Seasonal workers who had exhausted the 12-week maximum duration of benefits could be taken off the register of eligibles when the busy season in their particular occupation began, but the extent to which this practice prevailed during the years 1922-26 is not ascertainable. 

The available official documents disregard the fact that seasonal workers were disqualified during the season through the local interpretation and application of this provision of the law. Since the final decision in all such cases rested with the district commissions, no appeal could be taken to the Federal authorities except to the highest administrative court in Vienna.

The law of December 15, 1922 (4), liberalized the qualifying-period requirements so that, after drawing benefits for the maximum period in the benefit year, a worker needed only 10 weeks of covered employment to make him eligible for another 12 weeks of benefits. The same act introduced emergency benefits for persons in particular distress who had exhausted their 30-week period of benefits and who, on account of the situation in the labor market, were unable to find employment. In the absence of special restrictions during the off season, the liberalization of the qualifying-period requirement was decidedly favorable, because seasonal workers could qualify for bene- • These benefits were unlimited in duration. Rates were lowered in July 1925, but, although an attempt was made to limit the period of benefits on the basis of the age of the unemployed, the unlimited duration remained unchanged. In 1931 a large number of persons had been receiving emergency benefits for over 6 years. After 1927, unemployed persons over 60 years of age who were incapable of work or who had exhausted their benefits were transferred to the old-age relief system (38: p. 25).

SEASONAL WORKERS 

fits on the basis of even short-season employment if it lasted as long as 312 months.

Table 3 indicates the average number of persons receiving benefits in each of the years 1919-26, inclusive, and the number in the highest and lowest months of each year.

These provisions did not exclude from benefits persons who worked temporarily in covered employment but who were dependent for their livelihood upon other employment, particularly upon employment in agriculture (38: p. 16). In an official explanation (10) of a later 

Government bill, it was pointed out that a large group of seasonal workers were following a nonseasonal occupation during certain parts of the year only. (See also (See also pp. 121-122.) It had become possible for these workers to qualify for benefits extending for more than half a year (and for emergency benefits of much greater duration) on the basis of 20 weeks of covered employment and, under the new provisions of December 15, 1922, even on the basis of 10 weeks' employment. In many cases persons who had other means of livelihood, either employment in other occupations or within their own families, were eligible for benefits. These provisions resulted in increased demand for jobs of short duration which made it possible for workers to alternate periods of employment with intervening periods of unemployment during which they were entitled to benefits. It was further asserted that such employment appealed particularly to the rural population, increasing the tendency for workers to shift from Table 3.-Average number of recipients of ordinary and emergency unemployment benefits in Austria and total number in month of highest and lowest unemployment reported, 1919-26 ¹

As a result of this liberal policy, the fund was depleted to such an extent that in 1923 a period of reform directed toward a sounder financial basis for meeting the unemployment risk was inaugurated. 

The first measure, provided by an act of April 28, 1923 (effective 

October 1, 1923), excluded from coverage all employment in exclusively rural communities (6), which were defined as those in which an inconsiderable number of covered workers resided or were employed. The Ministry of Social Administration was charged with the designation of communities as exclusively rural upon the recommendation of the provincial governor (Landeshauptmann) or the district industrial commission. By the order of September 5, 1923 (18),10 a list of exclusively rural communities was issued for the provinces of Carinthia, Upper Austria, Salzburg, Tyrol, and Vorarlberg; by the order of September 21, 1923 (19),10 that for Burgenland and Styria; and by the order of January 26, 1924 (21),¹º that for

Lower Austria. The purpose of this regulation was to exclude from benefits the part of the farming population which, except for occasional employment in covered occupations, was mainly engaged in excluded agricultural work, though some genuinely seasonal workers were thus barred from coverage. The location of the enterprise was the deciding factor in this exclusion of workers; those employed by an establishment which was situated in an industrial urban community were covered even when their work was performed in rural communities. 

Though many communities were declared exclusively rural, the classification did not affect a great many workers, because the population of most of these districts was small. Furthermore, an exception to the exclusion was made for workers employed in building enterprises and for those employed in establishments in covered industries normally employing more than five persons.11 Employment in rural communities in establishments which normally employed more than five persons during a limited period of the year only, was covered for the busy season of the year, though employment in the construction of homes and farm buildings customary in the region was • Only manual workers and not salaried employees were affected by this exclusion. 

10 Slightly modified in 1926 and 1929. " Building enterprises only, not allied industries (see appendix C, chart II).

SEASONAL WORKERS 

exempt even when more than five persons were employed in the establishment.

Persons employed only during the season in health, summer, or tourist resorts which had been declared exclusively rural communities retained, however, their insurance coverage. The provincial governors determined the localities to be considered resorts, and likewise the duration of the busy season. This regulation was to assure benefit eligibility for seasonal workers employed in such resorts only during the season, if they were engaged in covered employment in other localities during the rest of the year. 

Duration of seasons fixed for determining the coverage of seasonal workers is shown in table 4 for six Austrian provinces. 

An anomalous situation resulted from the provision which granted insurance eligibility to workers in resorts in exclusively rural communities, in that such seasonal workers were insured while nonseasonal workers in the same establishments were not.12 Except in such communities, employees of an establishment as a whole were usually covered or excluded from coverage.

The 1923 amendment contained, furthermore, a provision whereby benefit rights during the off season might be modified for the seasonal workers who were engaged in covered employment in exclusively rural communities and resided in these communities (6: sec. V). It was expected that the scope of the measure and its procedures would be regulated by an executive order; this order, however, was never issued.

MODIFIED QUALIFYING-PERIOD REGULATIONS, 1926-31 The developments outlined in the preceding pages for the years 1920-26, a period of post-war maladjustment, may be considered the first stage in the evolution of the Austrian unemployment insurance system. The first experiment in meeting the seasonal problem was the adoption of unemployment risk groups for adjusting contribution rates to occupational and seasonal risk. When this plan was abolished in 1922, there followed an attempt to eliminate certain highly seasonal risks by excluding employment in rural communities whose industrial structure was subject to the fluctuations characteristic of agricultural employment and production.

The second stage comprised the years 1926-29, a comparatively stable period in which the financial structure of the system was reorganized, but no special methods of dealing with seasonal risks were established.18 This does not mean that seasonal unemployment was not recognized or discussed. On July 28, 1926 (7), an amendment of the law (the eighteenth since 1920) provided that unemployment insurance for seasonal workers was to be regulated by a special act, which, however, was never passed. The qualifying period was increased in this amendment for persons whose work had been exclusively or mainly in agriculture before they entered the covered employment on which their benefit claim was based. The normal 20week qualifying period for such workers was extended to 40 weeks, but persons temporarily employed in agriculture were not subject to this requirement. The extension of the qualifying period was clearly designed to exclude agricultural workers who took up short-term 1ª In 1926, contributions from covered workers and their employers met the entire cost of benefits for unemployed workers, except for emergency benefits (unemployment assistance or relief). The relief system was financed as follows: Three-sixths of the cost was met from supplemental contributions required of workers and their employers; the 

Federal Government, the province, and the local community in which the recipient resided each bore one-sixth of the cost. The local government's share was later transferred to the province and subsequently to the Federal Government.

SEASONAL WORKERS 

seasonal jobs in covered employment, qualified in 20 weeks, and then drew benefits for 12 or even 30 weeks. On the other hand, another seasonal group was added to the coverage of unemployment insurance by the act of November 22, 1927 (8), which extended coverage to employment which was exclusively or chiefly in sawmills operated in connection with agriculture or forestry. 

In 1929, the provisions for a longer qualifying period for persons formerly engaged in agricultural employment were extended to include persons who had been engaged exclusively or mainly in forestry before entering covered employment. The period of required employment was increased from 20 to 40 weeks in the preceding 12 months. Employment as forester on an estate, however, did not come under this provision. Simultaneously, the extended benefit period of 

30 weeks a year was reduced to 20 weeks for persons formerly employed in agriculture or forestry except when they had been engaged in covered employment for 52 weeks in the preceding year (9). The majority report of the Committee on Social Affairs of the Nationalrat justified the measure by the assertion that the purpose of the amendment was to check the easy transfer from excluded employment in agriculture or forestry to covered employment in which they would acquire eligibility for benefits after a brief period of work, particularly in occupations in which working opportunities existed for part of the year only. The special qualifying-period provisions, which had previously applied to former agricultural workers only, were to be extended to workers who had been employed exclusively or mainly in forestry (34), but, in order that workers who had been employed for years in Government forests or on large estates might more readily enter other occupations, these workers were not subject to this requirement. Many of them had made forestry their life work and were dependent upon it for their livelihood. When they were dismissed, many, especially those in mountain districts, were found to be in serious distress. Other forest workers, however, had to prove 40 weeks of covered employment in the preceding year orunder certain strictly defined exceptions such as sickness-in the past 2 years. Workers who could prove completion of this qualifying period were entitled to benefits for 20 weeks instead of the usual 30 weeks, unless they could show covered employment throughout the preceding year. At the expiration of the benefit period, emergency benefits were allowed these workers for only 10 weeks, whereas for other covered workers emergency benefits were virtually unlimited in duration.

Even these restrictions do not seem to have been effective, because a bill was drafted by the government to impose further limitations on seasonal workers' benefits. The proposals in the draft amendment were as follows: The qualifying period for persons formerly engaged mainly in agriculture or forestry was to be increased to 80 weeks of employment in the 2 preceding years. Persons "mainly engaged in agriculture or forestry" were those who for more than half of their total employment period, or at least during the past 10 years, had been employed in agriculture or forestry. The general qualifying period was to be increased from 20 to 26 weeks during the preceding 12 months and to 52 weeks in the preceding 

2 years in the case of first application for benefits.

It also was suggested in the draft amendment that seasonal workers who, in addition to their wages, received income from an independent business or occupation or from farm property be considered ineligible for benefits during the off season if the aggregate income from all these sources assured their livelihood during the whole year. Income of family members was to be taken into account if the worker had a family. If, during the season, a seasonal worker earned as much as or more than the annual wages of anonseasonal worker with equal skill, he was not to receive any benefits during the off season. Instead of the regular waiting 159727-40- -9

SEASONAL WORKERS 

period, other seasonal workers were to serve a special waiting period at the rate of a week for every month of seasonal employment. 

Special administrative orders were to define "seasonal occupation" and the periods of the off season (10). It was not intended to disqualify needy seasonal workers from benefits altogether but to limit total benefit expenditures for the group to the amount that seemed a justifiable proportion of total contributions paid by all employers and employees.14

In connection with the draft amendment it was further pointed out that many seasonal workers, when no work was available in their own occupations during the off season, looked for other work. 

When, as in times of general business depression, they were unable to obtain off-season employment, benefits were to be paid them during a part of the season. Steady year-round employment, however, was not the rule for such workers even during normal times; therefore a longer waiting period, varying with the length of employment during the season, was justifiable. Many persons took up industrial (covered) employment temporarily although they were regularly engaged during the rest of the year in agricultural work or in small enterprises (usually agricultural) owned by themselves or by their families; some, such as married women or young persons were maintained during the off season by the income of other members of their families. Likewise, workers normally engaged in seasonal employment often combined seasonal earnings with other sources of income. These workers, too, frequently depended for their livelihood mainly upon the earnings of agricultural property or upon the income from a small independent business, and their seasonal wages were, in a measure, supplementary. In the past such persons had gained a livelihood without public help, and there was no particular reason for paying them benefits.

Seasonal workers whose wages were so fixed (in view of the seasonal nature of their work) that their yearly earnings equaled the annual wages of nonseasonal workers with equivalent vocational training would not need benefits during the off season, it was asserted. Finally, it was pointed out that the determination of seasonal occupations and of the busy season and off season should not be attempted in the law but should be left to administrative orders. 

The special provisions were to apply only to occupations in which no work was available during the off season except in unusual cases, 14 See also pp. 116-117.

AUSTRIA 

123 and were not to apply to the occupations in which there were some employment opportunities throughout the year. 

Although this proposed amendment of the unemployment insurance system was never enacted, it is noteworthy because many of its features were embodied in the 1935 reorganization. The practice of disqualifying seasonal workers during the season seems to have been abandoned as a result of the special provisions enacted in 1931, providing increased contribution rates for seasonal workers and their employers during the season.

SPECIAL PROVISIONS, 1931-35 

The twenty-seventh amendment to the unemployment insurance law, which was made effective on August 3, 1931, finally established the special regulations for seasonal workers which had been planned as early as 1923 (11). The amendment was based on the provisions of the Government draft bill of 1930 (see pp. 121-122), but, although some of the bill's principles were retained, the measures adopted were considerably less strict than those proposed. 

Under the new legislation, the qualifying period was 52 weeks in the preceding 24 months for persons not regularly engaged in covered employment who claimed unemployment benefits for the first time. 

This requirement was more stringent than that made for persons normally in covered employment who had to show 20 weeks in the preceding 12 months. One of the basic principles of Austrian unemployment insurance policy is illustrated by the use of the flat qualifying period of covered employment to exclude from benefits all but the wage or salary earners who contributed regularly to the system. The chief groups affected were persons engaged in occasional or temporary work and those who were not regularly engaged in covered employment but depended mainly upon agriculture or independent work on their own account. By this requirement, funds were conserved for wage and salary earners who depended solely upon their employment for their maintenance throughout the year. The practical application of this provision in the individual case met with serious difficulties, one of which was that of distinguishing between the two types of workers. Perhaps to overcome these difficulties, provisions were incorporated in the general reorganization act of 1935 which, instead of discriminating against workers with little covered employment, allowed a shorter qualifying period (26 weeks in the preceding 2 years) for persons with long records of covered employment.

SEASONAL WORKERS 

The amendment of 1931 retained the qualifying period of 40 weeks' covered employment in the preceding year for persons who formerly had been exclusively or mainly engaged in agriculture and forestry, and the provision excluding from coverage persons employed in exclusively rural communities. The amendment further exempted employment in construction work in rural areas financed with public funds, except when the persons thus employed were normally engaged in covered employment. Employment in particular branches of such construction work could be declared insurable (11). 

This new exclusion did not, however, affect the coverage of employment in building and construction in rural communities carried on by private establishments customarily employing more than five per- An administrative order of August 3, 1931, effective on the following day, defined rural construction work as that which was carried on in a community of less than 4,000 inhabitants. Construction work financed with public funds included highway, road, and bridge construction; water and canal works; construction of power plants; improvement projects; railroad construction and other work on railroads; and certain other construction, if the cost was wholly or partly met by the Austrian Federation or by a State, county, municipality, or other public agency (22). Employment on such projects was exempt because the projects were, in many cases, purely relief programs. sons.

These general provisions affected seasonal workers somewhat, but special regulations were likewise provided for that group in the same amendment. Seasonal workers were defined as wage earners (salaried employees were not affected by these special regulations) whose normal employment was in the following establishments or industries: (1) Limestone quarries and lime-burning establishments; brick-burning establishments with the exception of those manufacturing fireproof bricks. (2) Contract construction (Baumeistergewerbe); masonry or bricklaying, carpentry, and excavation (Deichgräbergewerbe); building enterprises; plastering, roofing, and paving.

The off season for all these industries was uniformly the period from 

December 1 through March 31.15

The basis for determining whether an establishment belonged to the seasonal industries enumerated in the law was the Austrian industrial code. Where this code did not contain the necessary information, the code of industries and occupations published by the Federal 18 This off-season period was the same as that in Germany.

AUSTRIA 

125 

Statistical Office was to be consulted (36).18 The Federal Ministry of Social Administration issued instructions to this effect on August 

4, 1931 (23).

The occupation of the individual was of no significance in classifying a wage earner as a seasonal worker; the character of the industry or establishment in which he was employed was the only test. All workers, except salaried employees, who were employed in a seasonal industry or establishment were considered seasonal workers, regardless of the nature of their particular occupations or jobs. The increased contribution rates 17 in effect on November 1, 1931, for seasonal employment (see table 6) offered no particular difficulties. If the industry was classified as seasonal, the workers and their employers were charged at the higher contribution rate by the health insurance agency, which acted as collecting agent for the Ministry of Social Administration, and, when a wage earner was employed during part of the qualifying period (normally 20 weeks of covered employment) in a seasonal industry and during another part in a nonseasonal industry, he paid at the rate assigned for the establishment in which he was working at the time. The determining factor in the increase of contribution rates was the work of the establishment, not that of the individual worker. If, however, in addition to its building and construction department, a building enterprise operated a nonseasonal department, such as a wholesale or retail wood business or a cabinet-making branch, the increased contribution rate applied only to the workers who were employed in the building department. It was the duty of the employer or manager, when he registered his employees as covered or noncovered, to indicate which were employed in the building department and which in the nonseasonal branches of the establishment. The special contribution rates did not apply to workers employed on projects carried on by Government agencies under their own management and with their own funds. A few selected groups were permitted a reduced rate: Year-round contributions for permanent employees of public railways were one-eighth of the "normal" contribution rates; those for members of the defense forces, one-third.

When seasonal earnings exceeded 2,400 schillings in the case of unskilled or 3,000 schillings in the case of skilled workers, seasonal workers were subject to another special provision, reducing their benefits during the off season. An unemployed seasonal worker with high seasonal earnings who had been employed partly in seasonal and partly in nonseasonal establishments was classified on the basis of whether his seasonal or his nonseasonal employment had been of the greater duration during the qualifying period. If he had been employed in a seasonal establishment for the greater part of his qualifying period, his benefit amount was reduced during the off season. The normal benefit amount in 1931 varied with wage classes, number of dependents, and living arrangements. The basis for the determination of the wage class was the wages or salary which the worker had received from covered employment and on which contributions had been paid. Changes in the wage rate in the 4 weeks prior to the claimant's unemployment were not taken into account, nor was a short period of employment intervening between a long period of employment and one of unemployment. Except for workers with relatively high prior wages, seasonal workers' benefits were not reduced in amount during the off season, as in Germany. As early as 1920-21 the Austrian law, however, had provided for higher contribution rates for seasonal workers by fixing the contributions of seasonal workers and others in trades with high unemployment risks at 125 percent of the contribution rate in normal industries and at 250 percent of the rate required in particularly stable industries. Although contributions for seasonal workers were increased in 1931, the rates were not based on actuarial premises and the amounts collected proved inadequate to cover the seasonal risk 

According to an executive order of the Ministry of Social Administration issued on April 11, 1933, the benefit duration for ordinary insurance benefits was to be determined as follows: The district industrial commissions were authorized to increase the benefit period,

SEASONAL WORKERS 

which was normally 12 weeks in 12 months, to 20 weeks for needy unemployed persons who were able and willing to work (the second stage of the means test was applied). No extension was to be granted, however, for persons who had formerly been engaged in occasional or temporary employment. Unemployed persons who, during the 10 years preceding their application for benefits, had been engaged in covered employment for at least 7 years could receive benefits for 30 weeks (24).

On July 26 of the same year an order of the Ministry effected a general reduction in benefit rates which also applied to the rates for seasonal workers during the off season. (See footnote 1, table 7, and 25.)

After 1932, the economic situation in Austria grew steadily worse. 

Prior to 1925, the total number of unemployed persons receiving ordinary or emergency benefits had never exceeded 200,000 a month; for the year 1931 the monthly average was 250,000. In December 

1932, however, the figure had jumped to almost 370,000 and in July 1933 it was approximately 300,000. The contributions by workers and employers, which supposedly financed the system with supplementary Government contribution toward emergency benefits, were only a fraction of the total expenditure for unemployment insurance and assistance.18 The Federal Ministry of Social Administration called attention to this situation in special instructions sent to all district industrial commissions on August 2, 1933, and required them to apply the standards formulated for the examination of the "state of need" instead of "endangered means of livelihood" (see p. 139140), for even the first 12 weeks of benefits. "In this connection," the 

Ministry's instructions continued, "the question must be raised as to what decisions shall be made when members of certain occupations in which little or no employment is available during certain parts of the year receive a wage which exceeds the average." It pointed out that some district industrial commissions had decided that benefits should not be paid to such claimants immediately upon the ex piration of the waiting period (on the assumption that their means of livelihood were not endangered), but that benefits should be denied until after the expiration of a period determined on the basis of the duration of the person's seasonal employment and the amount 18 In 1931, for example, contributions from employers and workers amounted to about 150 million schillings, while total expenditures (including regular benefits, emergency benefits, and old-age relief) were 277 million schillings.

AUSTRIA 

129 of wages received during that time. This policy, the Ministry concluded, was wholly in accord with the definition of "endangered means of livelihood," and was recommended to the district industrial commissions for their adoption (26).

This interpretation of endangered livelihood was, strictly speaking, entirely within the authority of the administrative agencies. 

It contained nothing new which would necessitate an amendment of the unemployment insurance law. It will be remembered that the determination of endangered livelihood was made according to the combined income of the unemployed worker and of the persons living with him in the same household from all sources, including income from odd jobs and subsidiary work and from an independent business or agricultural property. The extra earnings prior to the person's unemployment had now to be taken into consideration in addition to other income or property, particularly in the case of seasonal workers with relatively high seasonal earnings. It is evident that until 1933 the means test as applied in Austria was not in itself an effective device for disqualifying seasonal workers from benefits during the off season. On the other hand, the means test might be utilized as an important instrument to keep seasonal workers from receiving benefits during the off season or, for that matter, during the season.

The various district industrial commissions used the instructions of August 2, 1933, as the basis for their determinations of the waiting period for seasonal workers, who remained subject to the higher contribution rates during the season. The Vienna District Industrial 

Commission, which was by far the largest agency in number of workers in its territory, issued special detailed regulations which became the basis of the statutory regulations interpreting the seasonal workers' provisions in the 1935 social insurance law and of the orders issued under it (27). The Vienna regulations were incorporated almost verbatim in the new law and in an order of the Ministry of Social Administration issued in May 1935 (29). 

THE SOCIAL INSURANCE ACT OF 1935 

The Austrian social insurance program was reorganized in 1935 (12). Since the new law continued in force in December 1938, a summary of the general and special provisions for unemployment insurance as they affect seasonal workers describes the latest Austrian policy toward these workers. The Austrian unemployment insur-

SEASONAL WORKERS 

ance statute was at first not extensively modified by Austria's union with Germany, but the local and district offices of the Austrian system were made part of the German Federal Institute for Em ployment Service and Unemployment Insurance. By the Order of 

December 22, 1938, effective January 1, 1939, the social insurance programs of the German Reich were introduced in the State of Austria. The German employment service and unemployment insurance law of July 16, 1927, as amended, superseded the unemployment insurance sections of the Austrian social insurance law of 1935. Coverage 

Under the Social Insurance Act of 1935, unemployment insurance coverage depends largely on membership in the general social insurance system. Wage-earning and salaried employees covered by health insurance are normally subject also to unemployment insurance, i. e., those engaged in employment under a contract of employment, service, or apprenticeship in manufacturing and mechanical industries including construction, mining, handicrafts, commerce, transportation, money, credit, insurance, the professions, and public service. Public service in the defense forces, in salt works, and on public railroads also is covered.

Exclusions.-The most important exclusions specified in the act which affect seasonal workers are the following: (1) Employment in agriculture and forestry, i. e., all persons who are normally employed under a contract of employment, service, or apprenticeship in agriculture or forestry enterprises (including horticultural enterprises not carried on for market-gardening); in hunting, fishing, extracting resin, and in the seasonal resin trade; persons employed in enterprises subsidiary to agriculture or forestry (chiefly engaged in the processing of their products, except when they are independent economic units under separate management), and domestic servants working for an agricultural or forestry employer. These exclusions remove a large proportion of the seasonal workers from the insurance system.10 (2) Public employment, including a large number of Government employees, and employment in private establishments with long tenure of office (an extremely stable employment group); salaried employees of Fed- 

19 According to the Census of March 22, 1934, more than 1,000,000 persons were gainfully employed in agriculture, forestry, and fishing. This was about 31.7 percent of the total gainfully occupied population of somewhat more than 3,000,000. About 360,000 were wage and salary earners or apprentices (about 11 percent of the total gainfully occupied) and about 640,000 (or more than 20 percent) were owners or assisting family members. Certain groups of agricultural or forestry workers are covered, however, such as workers in sawmills, salaried employees of large estates, and workers employed in certain agricultural consumers' associations.

AUSTRIA 

131 eral, State, and local governments; employees of Federal Theaters; physicians in Government institutions; postal workers; workers employed by the Federal Government (as in the Tobacco Monopoly and the Government Printing Office); and similar classes. This group is numerically small: approximately 2 percent of the total gainfully occupied population. (3) Employment of artists in vaudeville and circus activities without permanent contract. (4) Odd-job and subsidiary employment which may, in some cases, be of a seasonal nature.

No special provisions for exclusion or exemption of seasonal workers from coverage are included in the Austrian law of 1935 (12). 

Definition of Seasonal Workers.-The act of 1935 and an administrative order issued under its general provisions defined seasonal workers (Saisonarbeiter) as persons belonging to occupations in which unemployment for part of the year is customary. The Federal Minister of Social Administration, after consultation with the governing body of the National Federation of Social Insurance Carriers (which acts as a national advisory council for all matters concerning general social and unemployment insurance) is charged with specifying the occupations in which unemployment for a part of the year is customary and the periods to be considered as the off seasons. 

An administrative order issued in May 1935 defined seasonal workers as follows: 

(1) Workers in seasonally conducted and operated limestone quarries and lime-burning and brick-burning establishments. (2) Workers employed in architects' (builder-contractor) establishments, those employed in building enterprises, in masonry, carpentry, excavating (preparing the ground for building and construction work), plastering, roofing, painting, whitewashing (including house painting), and paving. (3) Workers in seasonal establishments of the hotel, restaurant, and bar industries. (4) Workers in seasonally operated health and sports establishments (29).

For this classification an establishment which is completely closed down during the off season or considerably reduced in its operation is considered a seasonally operated establishment. This seasonal determination applies only to manual workers; salaried employees are never considered seasonal.20 

20 In this connection it should be pointed out that salaried employees' contributions to the unemployment insurance system are considerably lower than those of wage earners, at present 2.25 percent of wages instead of 12 percent; this latter, however, includes 4 percent for old-age relief, for which salaried employees have no analogous provision since they are covered by a separate old-age insurance system.

SEASONAL WORKERS 

Two categories of seasonal workers are established. One class is comprised of workers in enterprises which are conducted on a seasonal basis. It includes workers employed in lime-burning and brickmaking plants, in hotels and restaurants, and in health and sports establishments operating seasonally. When these establishments are conducted on a year-round basis, however, their wage-earning employees are not considered seasonal workers. The second class, that of industries or trades which are seasonal in their very nature, includes workers employed in building and allied trades. No distinction is made between workers employed only during the season and those who have, in addition, dovetailing employment during the off season, nor is there any statutory basis for such a distinction. 

Determination of the Season. Whether a seasonal worker's unemployment is due to the slack season or to any other cause, if he becomes unemployed during the off season he is subject to the restrictions provided for seasonal workers during the off season. The uniform determination of the off-season period makes this provision even stricter. For the application of the special seasonal workers' benefit regulations, the season has been determined as follows: 

(1) For seasonal establishments in the restaurant, hotel, and bar industries and for seasonally operated health and sports establishments, the season lasts from May 1 through September 30, and, if there is a winter season, from December 15 through March 15 of the following year. (2) For all other seasonal industries the season lasts from April 1 through November 30.

The rest of the year is considered the off season. Although the district labor offices may, with the consent of the Federal Ministry of Social Administration, define the seasons for their territories, such local determinations have never been made.21 Only employment and earnings during the season are taken into account in computing the additional waiting period for seasonal workers during the off season. Workers who have supplementary employment are, therefore, subject to the same regulations as exclusively seasonal workers. 

Contributions 

Seasonal workers pay the same contributions as nonseasonal workers during the season and off season alike. The increased contribu- 

Under the general provisions of the unemployment insurance statute between 1922 and 1931, the season was determined by the district commissions at their discretion. See p. 118 and 39, p. 71.

AUSTRIA 

133 tion rates during the season (150 percent of the normal contribution rate), introduced in 1931, were discarded in the reorganization of 1935.22 The costs of the unemployment insurance system, including old-age relief for wage earners (but not for salaried employees, who have a separate old-age insurance system), are met by contributions from workers and employers in equal shares. The Federal Government pays the administrative cost and an annual sum for emergency relief fixed in the budget. The provinces (States) pay one-sixth of the amount spent for emergency relief to unemployed persons residing in the province but not more than certain amounts specified by law. 

They pay also one-third of the amount spent for old-age relief for wage earners paid to pensioners residing in the province. 

Benefit Eligibility 

Definition of Unemployment.-A person is considered unemployed if he has lost his position as a wage-earning or salaried employee and has not found new employment. A person is not unemployed if he: (1) Is employed without remuneration, or (2) Collaborates in the earnings of joint means of livelihood by working in a business owned by his (her) spouse, parents, or children, or (3) Can collaborate in earning the means of subsistence by working for a close relative, when such collaboration may reasonably be required of him in view of his former employment and his actual circumstances (this requirement does not apply to long-service employees, i. e., those who have been in covered employment for 260 weeks in the previous 10 years), or (4) Is being trained in school or in some regular course of instruction or is undergoing practical training without entering into a contract of employment, or (5) Is working as an independent businessman, in his own firm or in a liberal profession (author, artist). 

Nor is a person unemployed if he works on short time. Benefits are not payable for days on which he performs occasional or odd jobs for remuneration, and such days are not counted in the term of the benefit period.

Definition of Suitable Work.-Any employment is considered suitable which is appropriate to the physical capacity of the unemployed, 22 The varying occupational risk is taken into account in a few instances: The contribution rate for workers employed in the Salt Works (Government monopoly) is only 4 percent of wages (instead of the normal 12 percent), that for members of the defense forces 3.5 percent, and that for permanent workers on public railroads 1 percent (but temporary workers pay the regular rate of 12 percent). It should be noted that the 12percent contribution rate for wage earners covers also old-age relief; Austria has no general old-age pension system.

SEASONAL WORKERS 

is not injurious to his health or morals, is adequately paid, and does not materially hinder his return to his own occupation. If a person has been receiving benefits for 8 consecutive weeks and there is no prospect of his finding employment in his own occupation within a definite period, he must accept work, even if it might hinder his future return to his own occupation. He has to accept suitable employment even when it is to be performed elsewhere than at his accustomed place of employment or residence, if a suitable dwelling place can be obtained and if his dependents can be properly taken care of.

Qualifying Period.-As a rule the qualifying period is 20 weeks of covered employment in the year preceding the claim for unemployment benefits. When benefits are claimed for the first time, the qualifying period is 52 weeks of covered employment in the preceding 2 years. Workers who cannot prove that they have worked in covered employment at least 20 weeks in the preceding year are thus excluded from benefits. For the worker who is employed for only part of the week, only actual working days may be counted toward his 20 weeks, and 6 days is considered a week so that his qualifying period is 120 days. For the worker who is employed throughout the week, 7 days is considered a week so that his qualifying period is 140 days. This provision is somewhat more liberal than the German requirement of 52 weeks in the preceding 2 years for a first claim and 26 weeks in the preceding year for subsequent claims. It is, however, more restrictive than the British requirement of 30 weeks of covered employment in the preceding 2 years for first claims and 10 weeks in the preceding year for subsequent claims; in the British system, moreover, any week for which any contribution is made is counted. as a week of employment, even though the worker was employed for only 1 day.

For unemployed persons who were employed chiefly in agriculture or forestry (excluded employment) until the beginning of the covered employment on which their benefit claim is based, the normal qualifying period of 20 weeks is increased to 40 weeks of covered employment within the preceding year; and for the first benefit claim of such persons, covered employment for 52 weeks in the 2 preceding years is required (40 weeks of which must have been in the year immediately preceding the application for benefits). This provision is a serious obstacle to eligibility for seasonal workers who were originally engaged in agriculture or forestry. Proof of the required

AUSTRIA 

135 amount of covered employment is demanded each time benefits are claimed, as long as there is a question whether the worker is still chiefly engaged in agriculture or forestry. 

The Federal Minister of Social Administration may reduce the qualifying period to 26 weeks in the 2 years preceding the benefit claim for persons with long employment records (those who in the past 10 years have been in covered employment for at least 260 weeks 23 or 5 years). Seasonal workers who can prove that they have long employment records may thus qualify-unless excluded by other provisions-if they have had employment for one-fourth of each of the last 2 years.

Employment on public works financed by public funds cannot be counted toward the qualifying period if it has been of less than 20 weeks' duration. If the employment was longer than 20 weeks, only the period in excess of 20 weeks is counted.24 Thus persons employed solely on public works have to prove 40 weeks of such employment to have 20 weeks count toward the qualifying period. For workers formerly engaged chiefly in agriculture or forestry who have to prove 40 weeks of covered employment, public-works employment can never serve to meet the qualifying-period requirement. Since 20 weeks of such employment are disregarded, it is impossible within 1 or 2 years to accumulate the required equivalent which would amount to 

60 weeks in a year.

The "base" periods of 1 and 2 years may be extended by periods equal to time lost because of sickness or certain other causes, but by not more than 2 years except in employment abroad, for which the maximum extension is 4 years.

No special qualifying-period provisions for seasonal workers have been in force since 1935.

Waiting Period.-The waiting period of 7 days applies to seasonal workers and nonseasonal workers alike. The special treatment accorded seasonal workers consists mainly in an extension of the waiting period during the off season. Seasonal workers, as defined by statute and administrative order, are subject to a special waiting or disqualification period in addition to the regular waiting period of 7 days if they become unemployed after the termination of the season, but not during the season. The length of this additional waiting period is fixed by the Federal Minister of Social Administration for the differ- 

28 Now reduced to 156 weeks (18: p. 1966). 

24 This provision has been repealed (20: p. 39).

SEASONAL WORKERS 

ent trade groups, taking into account the amount of average seasonal earnings. It is computed differently for seasonal workers whose wages include tips in addition to their wages. (1) For seasonal workers whose wages consist, to an appreciable extent, of income from tips (such as waiters, chambermaids, and porters), the additional waiting period is determined as a fixed time period: (a) Two weeks' additional waiting period for each month of seasonal employment for head waiters who act as cashiers and for hotel receptionists whose remuneration consists mainly of tips considerably higher than those received by other servants or employees in the service trades; (b) one week's additional waiting period for each month of seasonal employment for all other persons receiving tips as part of their remuneration. Periods of seasonal employment of less than a month's duration are taken into account proportionally. (2) For other seasonal workers, particularly those in the building industries, the waiting period is computed in each case on the basis of the difference between the earnings of a seasonal worker during the season and the earnings during the same period of a nonseasonal worker with the same Vocational qualifications. For the purpose of determining the difference between seasonal and nonseasonal wages, the various district labor offices have established schedules of the average weekly wages of nonseasonal skilled and unskilled workers for their territories, subdivided into three zones. The wage schedules used in the computation of the additional waiting period for seasonal workers as of August 1936 are shown in table 8. 

The length of the additional waiting period is computed in the following manner: The average earnings of the seasonal worker during the season are compared with the average wages (according to the schedule) of a nonseasonal worker of like skill. The difference between the two wage rates is divided by the average wage of the nonseasonal worker, and the result is the number of weeks of additional waiting period for each week of seasonal employment. An example may make this clear: A skilled building worker was employed for the duration of the season, from April 1 to November 30, and thus had 35 weeks of seasonal employment. His wages were 

70 schillings a week, and a skilled nonseasonal worker's wages, according to the schedule, were 50 schillings a week. The difference between the seasonal and nonseasonal earnings is thus 20 schillings a week. This difference, divided by the average weekly wage of the nonseasonal worker, 20/50, equals 0.4. Thus the additional waiting period is determined as 0.4 weeks for each week of seasonal employment (35 weeks), 0.4 X 35 weeks, or 14 weeks. The same result would have been obtained by dividing the difference between the total earnings for the 35 weeks' employment of the seasonal and the nonseasonal worker by the average weekly wage of the latter.

The district labor offices determine the average wages for their territories. 

The classification of localities by zones is determined on the basis of cost of living and the industrial structure of the territory. Zone A contains only large cities, such as the capital cities of the various States or cities over 10,000 population, or districts with one large establishment which determines the industrial character of the locality and its surroundings, or districts with several large- or medium-sized establishments. 

In Zone B are those districts in which manufacturing industries and agriculture are more or less evenly balanced; in Zone C those districts which are predominantly agricultural. 

4 Skilled workers are those who have completed the customary or prescribed apprenticeship in their occupations.

For the territory of the Tyrol district: unskilled male workers over 18 years. 

For the territory of the Tyrol district: unskilled male workers under 18 years. 

For the computation of the additional waiting period, only periods of employment during the season are used even when the employment had begun before the beginning or had lasted beyond the end of the season. Another example may clarify this operation: A cook had been employed in a seasonal restaurant from March 1 through December 15. Only the period of the season, from May 1 through September 30, can be counted. His average weekly wages, including room and board, amounted to 70 schillings, while, according to the 159727-40-10

SEASONAL WORKERS 

schedule, skilled workers in nonseasonal establishments make 60 schillings a week. The difference between the wages of the seasonal and the nonseasonal worker is thus 10 schillings a week, which, divided by the average weekly wage of the nonseasonal skilled worker (60 schillings), gives a quotient of 0.16 or roughly 1 day for each of the 

22 weeks of employment during the season-May 1 through September 30-not taking into account employment weeks outside the season. After the cook became unemployed on December 15, he would have to serve the regular waiting period of 7 days plus 22 days' special waiting period and would become entitled to benefits (if he had met all other requirements) on January 13 of the following year.

The various district labor offices are authorized to determine uniformly, on the basis of collective wage agreements, the additional waiting period during the off season for groups of seasonal workers paid by the hour, week, or piece (but not for those receiving tips in addition to their wages). This procedure results in an additional waiting period of uniform length for all seasonal workers in certain industries without taking into account the actual rate of seasonal earnings in the individual case. Most district offices have established a uniform additional waiting period for seasonal workers in building and allied industries. It varies from 2 to 3 days for each week of seasonal employment, depending upon the collective wage agreements (39: p. 72; 28).25

In accordance with the markedly social objectives of the Austrian unemployment insurance system these restrictive provisions, as well as other general features, are liberalized by taking into account family responsibility, cost of living, and adequacy of earnings during the season: (1) For family breadwinners with at least two dependent children, the length of the additional waiting period is reduced by one-half; (2) The district offices may reduce the additional waiting period to onehalf if the business operations of the establishment in which the seasonal worker was employed have been obviously unfavorable (for family breadwinners with at least two children the reduction may be as high as threefourths); (3) The additional waiting period may be reduced by one-fourth if the seasonal worker, in connection with his seasonal employment, has had to incur extraordinary expenses, such as maintaining two households simultaneously. 

25 Mitteilungen der Industriellen Bezirkskommission Wien, 1934 contains an exhaustive list of seasonal trades in the building and allied industries (56 groups) and the number of additional waiting days for each week of seasonal employment.

AUSTRIA 

139 Means Test. The granting of benefits depends upon whether the unemployed person's livelihood is endangered by his unemployment, or, in other words, upon whether he is in need of benefits. The examination of the intensity and extent of need becomes stricter as the benefit period lengthens.

In determining eligibility for both ordinary and emergency benefits, the various district labor offices have established schedules fixing the amount of cash income and the value of agricultural holdings which, when exceeded, disqualify the claimant from benefits. In the determination of the state of need, the resources of the members of the common household are taken into account as well as the cash income and farm property of the claimant. The exempted amounts depend upon the cost of living in the locality and the number of persons in the family. As of 1936, the minimum income which precluded eligibility in Vienna was 35 schillings a week for a family of two, increasing with every additional member by 12 schillings a week up to 95 schillings for a family of seven. If the combined income of the family does not reach this amount, the livelihood is considered endangered and the unemployed person is entitled to benefits at a rate determined according to his wage class (see p. 141). Agricultural property is taken into account in a similar manner. 

Until 1935, three stages at which a means test was applied for ordinary benefits were distinguished. At that time one of these stages was discontinued because the extension of the benefit period to 30 weeks (see p. 141) was no longer possible except for long-service employees. Ordinary benefits were thereafter paid for a period of 

20 weeks to unemployed workers whose livelihood was considered endangered, and emergency benefits were paid for 30 weeks or more to workers in particularly necessitous circumstances. The intermediate stage of extended benefits was discontinued. 

Continued receipt of emergency benefits allowed to unemployed workers in particularly necessitous circumstances is subject to a frequent determination of need made on a strict basis, taking into account the age of dependents and the duration of the period for which emergency benefits have been received; the minimum income which precludes eligibility decreases as the benefit period increases. 

The amounts of emergency benefits were fixed until 1938 at flat rates for each of three categories to which workers were assigned on the basis of the length of time they had been receiving benefits, family responsibilities, and cost of living in the community. Since 1938

SEASONAL WORKERS 

the three categories of emergency benefits have been consolidated (cf. 31: p. 35).

Exclusion From Benefits During the Off Season.-Seasonal workers whose earnings during the season equal or exceed the annual earnings of nonseasonal workers with the same vocational qualifications are not entitled to benefits during the off season. The annual earnings of a nonseasonal worker are fixed at 52 times the average weekly wage, used for the computation of the additional waiting period (see table 8). For example, if a seasonal worker during 26 weeks of seasonal employment should receive an average weekly wage twice as high as that of a nonseasonal worker (or in 35 weeks, 112 times the average weekly wages of a nonseasonal worker), he would be ineligible for benefits.

Furthermore and here the means test is significant-seasonal workers who derive an income from work on their own account or from their own farm property, in addition to their employment as wage earners, are not entitled to benefits during the off season if their livelihood is assured for the whole year by their aggregate income from all these sources. The same disqualification applies during the off season to a seasonal worker living in the same household with a spouse, children, or parents whose income from business or from agricultural property, when added to the seasonal worker's earnings, assures the subsistence of the family throughout the year. 

The question whether or not the subsistence of the seasonal worker or his family is endangered is determined in accordance with instructions from the various district offices. Some district offices disqualify seasonal workers from benefits during the off season if the value of the worker's agricultural property (or that of his family) or the value of his income earned in independent work equals or exceeds one-half the amount which, on application of the means test, disqualifies nonseasonal workers from unemployment benefits on the ground that their subsistence is not endangered. This disallowance is effective, however, only when the worker's seasonal employment has lasted 5 or 6 months.

One of the provisions of the Austrian unemployment insurance law, not strictly a special regulation for seasonal workers, authorizes the district labor offices to disqualify certain groups of persons from unemployment benefits in districts of a predominantly agricultural character, particularly in Zone C districts 26 during the periods of 

28 See table 8, footnote 3.

AUSTRIA 

141 seasonal work in agriculture. This disqualification may be ordered only if sufficient employment opportunities are available in agriculture and the persons thus disqualified are those able to perform agricultural work, such as young unemployed persons, persons who own small agricultural holdings, and persons who are normally employed in agriculture during the summer season. 

Benefit Amount and Duration 

The Austrian law of 1935 contains no special provisions for calculating the amount and duration of benefits for seasonal workers; the general provisions of the law apply to these workers. 

Computation. The daily benefit amount is determined on the basis of schedules of flat amounts that vary with the family status, the number of dependents (taking into account family responsibility), and the wage class of the claimant based on his previously earned wages. The basis for assigning a claimant to the appropriate wage class is the wage which he has received on an average during the 20 weeks (identical with the normal qualifying period) of covered employment prior to his application for benefits. The maximum benefit is 80 percent of the claimant's previous wage. The daily wage is computed as % of the weekly wage, 26 of the monthly, and 12 of the annual remuneration. To the cash wages are added payments in kind (room and board), overtime pay, regularly recurring bonuses, and payments by third parties (tips) when customary. The daily benefit amounts payable from 1935 to 1938 are shown in table 9. 

Duration.-Since 1935, the normal benefit duration had been 20 weeks, the same as the normal qualifying period, with 30 weeks' duration for persons who were engaged in covered employment for not less than 7 years during the 10 years immediately preceding their application for benefits. [By an order of the German Federal Minister of Labor on May 5, 1938 (30), the provisions of the German unemployment insurance law regarding the maximum benefit duration were declared to be applicable to the Austrian unemployment insurance system, so that now benefits are paid for an unlimited period of unemployment.]

CONCLUSION 

The development of Austrian policy toward seasonal workers, through its 18 years' experience as the oldest compulsory unemployment insurance system on the continent of Europe, affords much that is of value for study and comparison. Various methods were tried in
