Slight changes were made in this schedule by the act of Dec. 24, 1937. an effort, on the one hand, to protect the unemployment fund from possible insolvency which might result from failure to restrict benefits for seasonal workers, and, on the other hand, to provide for the just needs of this and similar groups.

The volume and degree of seasonal fluctuations in employment and unemployment were recognized early as an important problem in Austrian unemployment insurance. Attempts to find an actuarially sound and socially equitable solution divided Austrian economists and administrators into two main schools of thought: One group reasoned that seasonal workers should not be entitled to benefits to any great extent during the season because the state of the labor market enabled them to find employment more readily than workers in nonseasonal occupations. This line of reasoning cannot be defended from an economic or social point of view. The seasonal worker's risk of unemployment during the season is hardly less than that of a nonseasonal worker. Furthermore, seasonal workers make a greater effort to obtain employment during the season than during the off season because they expect to use their skill and experience to greater advantage in their usual seasonal trade than they could in an occupation which they might take up temporarily during the slack season. The Austrian law, which provided 12 weeks of statutory benefits in 12 months

AUSTRIA 

143 (a provision which was never changed, even during the years of depression), made payment of benefits mandatory for this period if all eligibility requirements had been fulfilled by the claimant. The flat statutory benefit duration could not, therefore, be reduced in the case of seasonal workers, during either the season or the off season. After 

1922, however, the district industrial commissions were authorized to extend the benefit period to 30 weeks when a claimant was in need and when the situation of the labor market was deemed unfavorable in his occupation or trade. The district commissions commonly ruled that conditions were favorable during the season, basing their determination upon local climatic and industrial circumstances, and so disqualified seasonal workers during the whole or parts of the season. Although this method was adaptable to the characteristics of a region and to its industrial structure, thus assuring the most desirable, because the most flexible, determination, the principal objection remains: That to reduce the seasonal worker's benefit rights during the season, when he is subject to no less unemployment risk than is the ordinary worker, is to deny him protection to which his contributions have entitled him. The significant motive underlying such rulings was, therefore, the attempt to preserve the unemployment fund. 

The second school of thought attacked the seasonal problem from the opposite direction: Unemployment during the season should be compensable, it was asserted, but off-season unemployment should not entitle a person to benefits except under certain conditions. If it was made compensable, the higher unemployment risk should be balanced by higher contribution rates. To this end, the original unemployment insurance law established risk classes in three categories: one for industries with relatively stable employment, a second for industries with an average unemployment risk, and a third for highly unstable industries within which were classified the seasonal industries. 

Although benefit rates were the same for all three categories, contribution rates in the second were twice and in the third category 2¹2 times that for the most stable class. The classification was based upon conditions of the labor market at the time the law was enactedshortly after the end of the World War-when the economic situation was abnormal and the normal variations in unemployment risk were obscured by the disorganization resulting from the war aftermath with its attendant demobilization and unrest. The classification was therefore arbitrary and based on temporary factors. The risk classes were abolished in 1922 because the difference in rates did not effect

SEASONAL WORKERS 

the financial savings that had been planned, because administration was too cumbersome, and because the measure was unpopular with Austrian workers, who realized that weighted contribution rates were contrary to the pooled-risk principle. Since the risk classes had been established without actuarial basis, it was not surprising that the increase in the contribution rate for seasonal workers proved insufficient to cover the cost of seasonal unemployment benefits. 

Another attempt to reapportion contributions was made in 1931 when seasonal workers and their employers were required to pay 12 times the amount required of nonseasonal workers during the season. 

This policy necessitated an extensive volume of work and detailed adjustment because the increased contribution rate applied only during the season, whereas in 1920 the higher contributions were paid throughout the year. Seasonal workers were defined as wage earners (salaried employees were not affected) whose normal employment was in industries and establishments classified according to a strictly defined schedule. Any wage worker employed in a seasonal industry or establishment had to pay the higher contribution rate regardless of the nature of his particular occupation or job; the nature of the work of the establishment was the criterion. Thus the personal circumstances of the worker were disregarded; the wholly seasonal worker was not differentiated from the chance seasonal worker, or from the worker who had dovetailing employment during the slack season. A uniform increase of workers' contribution rates in certain industries considered seasonal, although an extremely crude method, was the means employed for balancing the seasonal risk. Opponents of the method asserted that it penalized the seasonal worker who often took seasonal work not by choice but by necessity and could hardly be held responsible for recurrent fluctuations in his industry. 

He not only paid a higher contribution, but he also ran the risk of exhausting his benefit rights during the off season, and, if his seasonal earnings were high, he was likewise subject to a reduction of the benefit amount.

Austria tried other methods of adapting its system to the seasonal risk by enacting special provisions for seasonal workers. Like most countries, it excluded employment in agriculture, and, for a time, all employment in agricultural regions, including that on construction works carried on in rural districts. Austria also extended the qualifying period for persons whose original employment was in noncovered seasonal industries, namely, in agriculture and forestry.

AUSTRIA 

145 Under the 1931 regulations, the industries considered seasonal were designated by law; later, however, they were announced by administrative order, a more elastic instrument. The season was determined by a central authority, the Federal Minister of Social Administration, as a period of 4 months, December through March, and was, in principle, identical for all parts of the country, although the regional authorities could, if they chose, fix its term locally. Limits of the official season were established for each of two categories of seasonal workers: First, workers employed in certain establishments which were conducted on a seasonal basis, i. e., completely closed down or considerably reduced in their operations during the off season; second, workers in trades or industries whose very nature entailed their classification as seasonal. Although Austrian classification, in its single distinction between two groups of seasonal workers, does not approach the flexibility of the British plan, which leaves to the local agencies the responsibility for classifying the worker and fixing the limits of the season, it is relatively free from the rigidity seen in the detailed German industrial and occupational schedules. 

Another method of meeting the seasonal risk would be to shorten the duration of benefits either by extending the waiting period or by reducing the number of weeks for which benefits are payable, especially in the off season. Extending the waiting period during the off season, probably the most equitable and feasible plan, in view of the general scope of the Austrian unemployment insurance system, was a policy used at first by the district commissions. When it had been proved workable, its principles were incorporated in the Austrian social and unemployment insurance system as revised by the reforms of 1935.

Under the 1935 law, if the seasonal worker is unemployed outside the defined season, he must serve, in addition to the usual 7 days, an extended waiting period, depending upon the amount of his average seasonal earnings or the length of his seasonal employment. The extended period is based on a comparison of his earnings with the amount earned during the same period by a nonseasonal worker of equal occupational training and skill. Only earnings in employment during the season are taken into account; off-season employment is excluded. The status of the seasonal worker in the labor market during the off season is thus disregarded so that, whether or not he finds off-season employment, the application of the additional waiting-period provision is not altered. Seasonal workers who receive

SEASONAL WORKERS 

tips in addition to their remuneration are subject to an extension of the normal waiting period at the rate of 1 week (in certain cases, 2 weeks) for each month of seasonal employment. All other seasonal workers serve an additional waiting period computed on the basis of the difference between the average earnings during the season and those of year-round workers. If the seasonal earnings exceed the annual earnings of a nonseasonal worker, the seasonal worker receives no benefits during the off season.

The computation of the additional waiting period is simplified by an average scale which can be applied for all members of a certain occupation in seasonal industries where there are collective wage agreements, with the result that computation for each individual case is unnecessary. Thus the worker himself knows exactly how long his waiting period will be.

From a social point of view, the lengthened waiting period has proved to be an extremely successful instrument for adjusting benefits to varying needs. Although it permits sharp curtailment of offseason benefits to seasonal workers, it is more elastic than British practice which, in accordance with strict insurance principles, regards as a subsidy and inappropriate to a pooled-risk system the payment of benefits during the off season to workers who do not have dovetailed employment. Austria includes, moreover, among her seasonal industries some which the British do not classify as seasonal, such as the building trades, in which unemployment is not a certainty, although it is highly probable. The seasonal worker who earns high wages presumably does not need a subsidy during the slack season, but those with low incomes are not able to save enough to keep them from want during the off season. Austria's reorganized plan, which imposes an additional waiting-period condition, graduated according to previous earnings, and, modified in length according to family responsibility, unsatisfactory season, and similar factors, has been an extremely interesting experiment.
